# US broad atlas: interesting findings so far v1

**Checked:** 2026-09-21  
**Purpose:** give a reader the most interesting current observations across
the broad atlas in plain language.

These are not claims about one unified population or one causal system. Each
item comes from a bounded source or comparison. The “do not overread” line is
part of the finding.

## 1. People can be hopeful about themselves and pessimistic about the country

The national-mood layer separates personal future feeling from expectations
about the country's direction, institutional future, and future political
division. People can carry private hope while believing the national system is
getting worse.

**Why it matters:** “optimism” and “pessimism” are not one public mood. A
person can plan for themselves while losing confidence in shared institutions.

**Do not overread:** this does not show what caused the split or whether it
changes buying, migration, voting, or organizing.

Read the [national mood layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-national-mood-future-expectations-layer-v1.md).

## 2. Expense pressure seems to narrow the experience of connection before it
eliminates contact

The March HTOPS screen finds more frequent loneliness, lower social/emotional
support, and more food insufficiency as reported expense difficulty rises, while
frequent telephone/video contact is comparatively flat.

**Why it matters:** people may remain in contact while feeling less supported.
The social problem is not simply “people stopped talking”; it may be that
contact is less adequate, less reciprocal, or less useful under pressure.

**Do not overread:** this is cross-sectional. It does not identify a bill,
cause, help received, or later political action.

Read the [HTOPS social-connection finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-032.md).

## 3. A formal option is not necessarily a usable option

Across disability health care, airport transit, public benefits, consumer
redress, and workplace systems, the visible program or route often exists
before the evidence shows that a person can actually use it. Language,
accessibility, schedule fit, cost, identity checks, paperwork, reliability, and
the ability to reach a human determine practical access.

**Why it matters:** institutions can report capacity while users experience
absence. The interface is part of the social outcome.

**Do not overread:** a barrier description does not by itself establish a
completed denial, harm, or population-wide failure rate.

Read the [interface-to-outcome synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/formal-access-control-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md).

## 4. Family care is infrastructure that is rarely priced correctly

Child care, eldercare, kinship care, and unpaid adult care keep households and
workplaces functioning. The costs appear as time, reduced hours, job exit,
co-residence, sleep loss, and career strain rather than only as a care bill.

**Why it matters:** a society can look economically functional partly because
families absorb the missing service.

**Do not overread:** population-level care time does not identify which job,
health, or political outcome was caused by care for one person.

Read the [care/work layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/atus-2023-2024-published-care-work-layer-v1.md)
and [family-care finding](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-004.md).

## 5. AI can save work time without giving workers more power

The AI evidence separates adoption, time saved, productivity, monitoring,
training, task discretion, bargaining, pay, and exit. A tool can make a task
faster while leaving the worker with less say over how the work is measured or
what happens next.

**Why it matters:** “AI helped productivity” is not the same as “workers
benefited.” Control and captured gains are separate questions.

**Do not overread:** current layers do not establish one general AI effect on
employment, wages, or household security.

Read the [AI work-control project](projects/ai-work-control/README.md).

## 6. The same visible behavior can mean freedom or constraint

Using a nonbank payment route, living with family, providing unpaid care,
working multiple jobs, staying with a bank, or continuing to use a platform can
represent preference, support, habit, lack of alternatives, or some mixture.

**Why it matters:** behavior alone does not reveal agency. Non-switching is
not automatically satisfaction; co-residence is not automatically choice;
multiple jobs are not automatically ambition or exploitation.

**Do not overread:** the current cross-source layers do not identify the
private reason for every observed choice.

Read the [unequal optionality synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/unequal-optionality-status-meaning-synthesis-v1.md).

## 7. Privacy can increase sharing while also increasing firm knowledge

One privacy study finds that clearer privacy rules were associated with more
information sharing by users. That can reflect greater trust, but it also gives
the firm more behavioral data.

**Why it matters:** privacy is not simply “less data.” It can change the terms
of the exchange and make people more willing to participate in it.

**Do not overread:** more sharing does not prove users understood later data
uses, received better service, or gained equal bargaining power.

Read the [privacy and data-sharing layer](projects/us-privacy-trust-data-sharing/README.md).

## 8. Local business activity is not one thing

Business applications, realized establishments, exits, and net-job intensity
diverge by sector and place. A high level of entrepreneurial activity can coexist
with high churn or weak net job creation.

**Why it matters:** “more businesses” can mean opportunity, instability,
replacement, or local service churn depending on the sector and outcome.

**Do not overread:** applications are not the same as surviving firms, good
jobs, local ownership, or better consumer access.

Read the [BFS–BDS sector divergence layer](projects/us-local-business-place/bfs-bds-sector-divergence-layer-v1.md).

## 9. Population movement changes demand before belonging catches up

Migration can change labor supply, customers, housing demand, businesses,
health-care need, transport, schools, and local cultural meaning at the same
time. These systems adjust on different clocks.

**Why it matters:** a place can gain workers and customers while still having
too few homes, providers, or trusted institutions. New residents are not just
an economic input; they are neighbors and public-system users.

**Do not overread:** place-level population change does not establish one
resident's opportunity, exclusion, belonging, or political response.

Read the [migration/place/cultural-conversion synthesis](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-place-cultural-conversion-synthesis-v1.md).

## 10. Real wages may carry more political meaning than inflation alone

The economic-voting layer reports that local real-wage decline is associated
with later Republican electoral gains in a county-level US study, while
inflation remains a separate political signal.

**Why it matters:** people may react to whether pay keeps up with life, not
only to the headline rate at which prices rise.

**Do not overread:** county associations do not identify each voter's reason,
and party identity, media, candidates, and social issues remain competing
explanations.

Read the [real-wages and voting layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/README.md).

## 11. Local trust is a form of infrastructure

The neighbor-trust evidence distinguishes willingness to help from confidence
that help will be available in return. Formal institutions, family, religious
groups, charities, employers, and markets then form a portfolio of support
routes.

**Why it matters:** people may stay in a place or institution because familiar
relationships make life workable. A breakdown of reciprocity can make a
material shock feel like exclusion or betrayal.

**Do not overread:** willingness to help is not delivered help, and trust is
not a resilience score.

Read the [neighbor trust layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2025-neighbor-trust-help-community-capacity-layer-v1.md).

## 12. Domestic capability and geopolitical power are separate conversions

Defense contracts, chip awards, critical-mineral potential, data-center
capacity, and grid investment show activity or technical possibility. They do
not automatically show qualified output, replaceability, local benefit,
public legitimacy, alliance leverage, or changed foreign behavior.

**Why it matters:** a state can spend more and build more while remaining
dependent on foreign suppliers, private operators, skilled labor, or public
consent.

**Do not overread:** contracts and capacity are not the same as operational
capability or strategic autonomy.

Read the [capacity/dependence synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/capacity-without-exit-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md)
and [global-role legitimacy layer](projects/ai-work-control/pew-2026-us-global-role-domestic-partisan-international-comparison-layer-v1.md).

## 13. Backend financial architecture becomes a customer problem at the
interface

The CFPB's Bilt account shows how an internal bank-partner transition can
become overdraft, late, and insufficient-funds fees for customers. Bilt's
outreach, reimbursement route, and reported technical repair are visible, but
the affected denominator, individual receipt, account correction, credit
impact, and switching outcome are not.

**Why it matters:** infrastructure is not only the visible grid or data
center. Payment architecture is also social infrastructure. A firm can repair
the backend while customers still carry the timing, documentation, liquidity,
and trust risk.

**Do not overread:** an agency-reported reimbursement milestone is not proof
that every affected customer was reached or made whole.

Read the [Bilt transition finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-033.md).

## 14. A state can have capability, reputation, and legitimacy on different
clocks

The geopolitical evidence separates defense contracts, delivered equipment,
training, readiness, foreign-public opinion, and domestic partisan meaning.
For example, Poland's F-35 record reaches an official aircraft-count milestone
while personnel, spares, readiness, mission use, and external response remain
open. Pew's global-role survey likewise finds that Americans and foreign publics
can judge U.S. peace, respect, interests, and interference very differently.

**Why it matters:** military or industrial spending does not automatically
become strategic autonomy, and public legitimacy does not automatically equal
material weakness. State power is a conversion process.

**Do not overread:** contracts, aircraft in country, or favorable polling do not
prove operational capability, alliance behavior, deterrence, or geopolitical
leverage.

Read the [Polish defense-industrial finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-118.md)
and [global-role legitimacy layer](projects/ai-work-control/pew-2026-us-global-role-domestic-partisan-international-comparison-layer-v1.md).

## 15. A place can have growth, capacity, distrust, and civic activity at the
same time

The migration/place screen finds that county population growth and foreign-born
share do not produce a simple trust gradient. In the 620-county comparison,
federal trust varied non-monotonically across the joint growth/nativity cells,
while civic action stayed in a comparatively narrow range. Separately, the
immigration-concern layer reports protective non-use of benefits, clinics,
work, police, or community events among some families.

**Why it matters:** local growth does not have one cultural meaning. A place
can gain workers, customers, firms, and demand while some residents avoid
institutions because the route feels risky, stigmatizing, or unsafe.

**Do not overread:** county composition does not reveal an individual's
migration experience, and non-use does not by itself prove distrust or legal
ineligibility.

Read the [migration/place cultural-conversion finding](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-place-cultural-conversion-synthesis-v1.md)
and [migration demand/capacity layer](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-demand-housing-services-belonging-layer-v1.md).

## 16. A benefit can be officially issued and still become unusable

GAO reports more than $320 million in approved SNAP replacement benefits for
nearly 679,000 households after EBT theft during the covered statutory period.
The record also shows a second burden: recipients may have to monitor balances,
lock cards, change PINs, file claims, and find emergency food while states,
processors, retailers, and federal agencies control much of the security
architecture.

**Why it matters:** public benefit is not only the amount authorized. It is also
whether the delivery system protects control, detects loss, replaces the money
quickly, and preserves food access during the gap.

**Do not overread:** approved replacement claims are not all theft, replacement
issuance is not verified receipt, and the record does not measure the food gap,
adequacy, trust, or continued participation for each household.

Read the [SNAP EBT theft/control ledger](projects/us-safety-net-access/data/snap-ebt-benefit-theft-control-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 17. A denial can be reversible and still costly

HHS OIG found that 12% of skilled-nursing admission requests were denied in
the reviewed Medicare Advantage organizations. Only 18% of denials were
appealed, yet 95% of appealed denials were overturned in favor of the enrollee.
That is a striking institutional pattern: the correction route works often
when used, but the public record does not show who could appeal, how long it
took, whether admission happened, or whether rehabilitation and function were
protected.

**Why it matters:** “appealable” is not the same as “harmless.” The cost of an
initial denial may be paid in waiting, documentation, family advocacy, delayed
care, or lost function before formal correction arrives.

**Do not overread:** request-level overturn rates do not prove inappropriate
denial prevalence, population-wide health harm, or beneficiary-level recovery.

Read the [Medicare Advantage prior-authorization ledger](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/data/medicare-advantage-snf-prior-authorization-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 18. AI can lower an immediate social barrier without changing the deeper
relationship

The AI evidence spans work, political contact, emotional support, and
companion-app exit. A synthetic outgroup conversation produced a short-run
increase in warmth and a higher choice of a later real conversation, but the
preregistered one-week mean effect was small and not significant. Separately,
an audit found continuation tactics in 37% of 1,200 farewell conversations
across companion apps, while the longer-term user outcome remains unknown.

**Why it matters:** AI is becoming ordinary across both instrumental and
relational life. A system can help someone act or feel differently now without
giving them durable trust, human care, worker authority, or a clean exit.

**Do not overread:** warmth is not depolarization, engagement is not benefit
or harm, and a product-level farewell audit is not a population-wide dependence
estimate.

Read [AI is becoming ordinary before control is settled](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/ai-use-work-contact-exit-synthesis-v1.md).

## 19. Equal income can buy a different life when it comes through several
jobs

The multiple-job evidence finds that similarly paid people working several
jobs spent less of take-home pay on necessities and extras, carried higher
transportation costs, and saved less. Multiple jobs can provide flexibility or
an exit route, but they can also make the same income more expensive in time,
travel, and future security.

**Why it matters:** income is not the whole consumer or household story. The
structure of work changes what the paycheck can actually buy.

**Do not overread:** multiple jobs may reflect choice, school, care, a
transition, or constraint. The evidence does not isolate one cause or prove
that flexibility is harmful.

Read the [multiple-jobs spending/strain project](projects/us-multiple-job-spending-strain/README.md).

## 20. Shared moral pessimism does not create a shared public solution

Gallup reports that 80% of Americans think moral values are getting worse and
56% rate current values poorly, but the country is divided over whether
government should promote moral values. Religious identity and partisan
identity condition the interpretation, while Pew shows that affiliation,
switching, spirituality, cultural connection, and practice are not the same
thing.

**Why it matters:** people can share a decline narrative while disagreeing
about authority, responsibility, religion, and the legitimate remedy. Cultural
change is not one axis of “more moral” or “less moral.”

**Do not overread:** survey judgments do not measure objective morality,
individual behavior, religious institutional strength, or a direct cause of
voting and political realignment.

Read the [moral-values layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/gallup-2026-moral-values-government-role-cultural-polarization-layer-v1.md)
and [religious identity layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2025-religious-affiliation-switching-cultural-identity-layer-v1.md).

## 21. The smartphone is both social infrastructure and a contested habit

Pew reports that 53% of adults say they spend too much time on their phone,
while 73% say it helps them stay informed and 51% say it helps them feel
connected. Among people who tried to cut back, only 25% described the attempt
as extremely or very successful; 52% reported partial success and 23% little or
none.

**Why it matters:** digital participation is not simply useful or harmful. The
same device can provide information and belonging while making time, sleep,
productivity, and self-control feel harder to protect.

**Do not overread:** these are self-reports, not device logs or clinical
measures. The survey does not prove platform causality, addiction, or durable
exit.

Read the [smartphone self-regulation ledger](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/data/pew-smartphone-self-regulation-wellbeing-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 22. Money paid is not the same as a home becoming repairable

California's wildfire claims tracker reports 41,727 claims filed for the Eaton
and Palisades fires, 40,482 partially paid, and $25.2 billion paid to date.
Separately, a State Farm review found 398 violations in 114 of 220 sampled
claims, and California enacted standards for investigating, testing, and
restoring smoke-damaged homes. The public record still does not connect those
aggregate stages to a particular property's completed repair, safe return,
renewed coverage, or decision to stay or move.

**Why it matters:** climate exposure becomes a cultural and consumer problem
when a payment, a legal right, or a regulator's intervention must still be
converted into a safe home, a workable budget, health protection, and the
ability to remain attached to a place. The money clock, legal clock, and
household-recovery clock can move at different speeds.

**Do not overread:** partial payment is not necessarily inadequate, sampled
violations are not a statewide rate, and the aggregate record does not prove
which households repaired, moved, borrowed, returned, or lost trust.

Read the [wildfire insurance repairability finding](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/findings/us-housing-insurance-risk-005.md)
and [repairability event ledger](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/data/california-la-wildfire-claims-repairability-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 23. Infrastructure growth can make household cost-sharing a political question before the bill arrives

Virginia's regulator is responding to fast-growing data-center demand with a
new large-load class, minimum-demand obligations, direct-connect cost rules,
and a Rider T1 allocation change. The regulator reports that a modeled typical
residential increase could fall from $2.90 to $0.94 per month under the
amended method. That is a distributional policy comparison, not an observed
household bill, a data-center-only causal estimate, or proof that residents
accepted the arrangement.

**Why it matters:** infrastructure is not only about whether a facility gets
energized. It is also about who carries the fixed cost, who gets reliability,
which communities absorb land and route burdens, and whether a public benefit
feels legitimate before people can see it in their own bills or service.
Regulation can move risk around before the final incidence is visible.

**Do not overread:** the modeled figures are not realized savings, the wider
bill changes are not attributed solely to data centers, and public hearings or
regulatory design do not establish resident trust, compensation, reliability,
or later political action.

Read the [Virginia large-load governance layer](projects/ai-work-control/virginia-large-load-governance-reliability-layer-v1.md)
and [event ledger](projects/ai-work-control/virginia-large-load-governance-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 24. More access for firms can still mean less room for the smallest firms

A quasi-experimental study of expanding small-business-program eligibility
finds that larger firms gained access while the smallest firms—especially
younger, more productive, and financially constrained firms—saw lower revenue.
Government contracts shifted toward larger firms, and the study reports higher
exits, lower wages, and less patenting among the smallest firms. Separately,
the local-place work shows why this matters beyond firm balance sheets: a
county can have employers and formal service designations while residents still
lack a clearly reachable provider, workable hours, or an actual appointment.

**Why it matters:** “more eligible businesses” and “more local choice” are not
the same outcome. Public programs can expand on paper while concentrating
opportunity, weakening smaller firms, or leaving customers without a practical
replacement. Firm scale, worker security, local services, and consumer power
are connected but must be measured separately.

**Do not overread:** the firm study does not show customer prices or quality,
and county employer/service measures do not prove resident access. Revenue,
exit, wages, and patenting are not by themselves household welfare or political
meaning.

Read the [small-business eligibility finding](projects/us-small-business-support-size-cutoff/findings/us-small-business-support-size-rule-001.md)
and [Ohio place option-stack synthesis](projects/us-local-business-place/bds-ohio-option-stack-synthesis-v1.md).

## 25. Leaving a financial institution is not always consumer choice

The FDIC finds that 4.2% of US households were unbanked in 2023 and another
14.2% were underbanked—using an account while also relying on specified
nonbank transaction or credit services. Its 2021 transition module found that
among recently unbanked households, 42.4% reported a recent job or income
shock, and among that subgroup 21.1% said the shock contributed to closing the
account. The survey warns that closure could have been initiated by the
household or by the bank.

**Why it matters:** “switching,” “exiting,” and “being unbanked” can describe
very different social realities. One person may shop for a better provider;
another may lose access after an income shock and fall back on cash, family,
or nonbank services. The same visible endpoint can therefore mean autonomy,
constraint, distrust, or emergency adaptation.

**Do not overread:** the 2021 and 2023 figures are different survey objects,
not a household panel. The evidence does not prove that income shocks caused
closure, nor does nonbank use by itself show harm, exclusion, or distrust.

Read the [financial access and alternative-routes layer](projects/us-financial-intermediation/fdic-access-credit-alternative-routes-layer-v1.md)
and [banking substitution and recourse finding](projects/us-financial-intermediation/findings/us-financial-intermediation-003.md).

## 26. Care support can improve control without proving durable household recovery

The randomized Cash and Counseling demonstrations gave eligible Medicaid
beneficiaries more control over who provided care, when care happened, and
which permitted supports could be purchased. The follow-up reported higher
service satisfaction, fewer unmet personal-care needs, and greater access to
paid care in the studied comparisons. Related follow-ups found selected
wellbeing and financial-strain improvements for informal caregivers and
generally positive experiences for direct-hire workers.

**Why it matters:** a benefit is not only money delivered. Control over timing,
worker choice, and the care arrangement can itself be valuable. But that
control may also move hiring, payroll, backup-care, and continuity work into
the household. The same intervention can improve one person's autonomy while
leaving another person's unpaid labor or job quality unresolved.

**Do not overread:** the beneficiary, caregiver, and worker studies are related
but non-pooled units, mostly with roughly nine-month follow-up. Assignment is
not the same as take-up or completed care, and the evidence does not establish
long-run health, household security, worker retention, trust, or exit.

Read the [Cash and Counseling multi-currency synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/cash-counseling-multi-currency-realization-synthesis-v1.md)
and [beneficiary outcome ledger](projects/us-aging-care-strain/data/aspe-cash-counseling-beneficiary-outcome-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 27. Financial worry does not produce one predictable political meaning

In the retained 2016–2020–2024 ANES panel, pre-election financial worry was
related to later reported presidential vote choice, but the pattern was not a
simple hardship-to-one-party rule. Among the panel respondents, 68.9% of the
“very worried” group reported voting for Trump, compared with 32.0% of the “not
at all worried” group; the “extremely worried” group was smaller and more
divided. Trust was also non-monotonic: the extremely worried group reported
“some” or “never” trusting the federal government more often than the not-at-
all-worried group, but worry did not by itself explain the difference.

**Why it matters:** material pressure becomes political through interpretation,
identity, blame, information, and available action routes. The same economic
condition can produce demand for government, anger at government, a vote for a
challenger, withdrawal, or no visible action. “Economic anxiety” is therefore
not a complete political explanation.

**Do not overread:** this is a weighted descriptive panel comparison, not a
causal estimate. Financial worry is not a dated bill or verified hardship;
party identity and prior beliefs shape the path, the panel is selective, and
reported vote is not the same as persuasion, turnout, organizing, or policy
change.

Read the [ANES economic judgment, trust, and action layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/anes-2024-panel-judgment-action-layer-v1.md)
and [financial-worry panel finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-014.md).

## 28. People are often more willing to help than they expect to be helped

Pew's 2025 neighbor survey finds that 76% of adults say they would be very or
extremely likely to bring in a neighbor's mail or water plants, but only 52%
think a neighbor would do the same for them. Sixty-seven percent say they
would bring a meal to a sick neighbor or friend, while 42% expect that support
in return. For emergency money, the gap is smaller: 50% would loan money to a
friend and 45% expect a friend would do so.

**Why it matters:** informal support is real social infrastructure, but
willingness is not the same as available capacity or delivered help. A
household may be protected by family, neighbors, or community groups while
the helper absorbs time, money, privacy, stress, or risk. “Community
resilience” can therefore hide an unequal transfer of burden.

**Do not overread:** these are stated expectations in a cross-sectional survey,
not observed assistance during a dated crisis. They do not show adequacy,
duration, reciprocity, or whether informal support replaced or complemented a
formal program.

Read the [neighbor trust and mutual-aid layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2025-neighbor-trust-help-community-capacity-layer-v1.md)
and [formal/informal support synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/formal-informal-support-capacity-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 29. Religious switching is not the same as losing culture or community

Pew's 2023–24 Religious Landscape Study finds that 62% of U.S. adults identify
as Christian and 29% as religiously unaffiliated, while 35% say their current
identity differs from the one in which they were raised. Yet spirituality,
practice, family connection, and cultural identity do not line up perfectly
with the affiliation label: 21% describe themselves as spiritual but not
religious, and Pew separately measures cultural or family identification with
religious traditions.

**Why it matters:** cultural change is not simply “religion disappearing.” A
person can leave a formal label while retaining rituals, moral language,
family ties, community service, or political meaning. Institutions may lose
members while some of their social functions move into families, informal
groups, charities, or new identity networks.

**Do not overread:** the study is cross-sectional and uses retrospective
childhood identity. Switching does not reveal why someone changed, whether
practice continued, or whether affiliation change caused different trust,
family, charitable, consumer, or political behavior.

Read the [religious affiliation, switching, and cultural identity layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2025-religious-affiliation-switching-cultural-identity-layer-v1.md).

## 30. Institutional visibility can grow while public legitimacy stays contested

Pew's 25-year Muslim-American analysis places several clocks side by side:
the Muslim population is estimated to have grown from 2.4 million in 2007 to
5.5 million in 2026, mosque counts rose from roughly 1,200 in 2000 to nearly
2,800 in 2020, and Muslim representation in Congress grew from zero members in
2001 to four in 2026. At the same time, 59% of Muslim adults in 2026 said
there is a lot of discrimination against Muslims and 59% said media portrayals
are unfair; 42% of all adults said Muslim Americans have a negative impact on
the country.

**Why it matters:** inclusion is not one scale. Population, institutions,
representation, treatment, public judgment, safety, and agenda-setting can
move in different directions. More visible community capacity does not
automatically produce equal belonging or protection from hostile treatment.

**Do not overread:** these are different populations, years, and measures;
public judgment is not actual social impact, officeholding is not equal power,
and reported discrimination or hate-crime counts are not a complete prevalence
measure. The record does not establish a causal effect of media, parties,
immigration, or historical events.

Read the [Muslim Americans discrimination, capacity, and visibility layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-muslim-americans-discrimination-capacity-visibility-layer-v1.md).

## 31. A recall can be operationally active while the customer remains unprotected

For one VRURC portable-charger recall, the public record links a fire hazard,
a stop-use notice, a free-replacement offer, and a later CPSC progress snapshot
with 64,963 reported consumer-level corrections. But the notice and progress
workbook use different product denominators, and neither shows whether a
particular purchaser saw the notice, received the replacement, safely restored
use, or paid for a temporary substitute.

**Why it matters:** a regulator and firm can be visibly doing something while
the final consumer still carries discovery, paperwork, downtime, disposal, and
replacement risk. This is a useful consumer-power pattern: institutional
activity is not the same as a completed remedy, and a completed remedy is not
automatically restored safety or trust.

**Do not overread:** correction counts are firm-reported implementation data,
not a consumer recovery rate. The recall key joins product records, not unique
purchasers or households, and the injury signal is not a population incidence
estimate.

Read the [recall implementation bridge](projects/us-marketplace-product-safety/findings/us-product-recall-response-003.md).

## 32. AI can change the workday before workers gain control of the change

In a randomized field experiment across 66 firms and 7,137 knowledge workers,
assigned AI access was associated with about two fewer hours of email per week
in the latter half of the study, while measured task quantity and composition
did not detectably change. A separate customer-support experiment found about a
14% increase in issues resolved per hour with AI assistance. At the same time,
an OECD employer survey found widespread algorithmic-management use, including
monitoring of conversation content or tone.

**Why it matters:** less email or more output is an intermediate currency, not
automatically a worker benefit. Employers may return time to workers, convert
it into more output, raise targets, intensify monitoring, or change staffing.
The real question is who controls the saved capacity and whether workers can
see, contest, refuse, or benefit from the system.

**Do not overread:** these are separate studies, industries, and units. AI
access is not the same as worker exposure, productivity is not autonomy, and
manager-reported algorithmic-management use does not prove harmful monitoring,
worker consent, or a change in job quality.

Read the [AI work-time and control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-094.md)
and [AI capability/dependence layer](projects/ai-work-control/ai-capability-dependence-layer-v1.md).

## 33. A state can buy industrial capacity before it has usable strategic power

Poland's Ministry of National Defence reports 62 SAFE contracts worth about
120 billion złoty, with nearly 90% of the money expected to go to Polish
defense companies. That is meaningful industrial allocation and a domestic
capacity decision. But production, shipment, acceptance, training,
maintenance, replaceability, and an observed change in another actor's behavior
are separate stages. The JASSM-ER route still shows a delivery window rather
than an order-specific public acceptance or fielding record.

**Why it matters:** procurement changes political meaning before it necessarily
changes operational capability. Domestic spending can signal security intent,
create firms and jobs, and reshape alliance expectations while the state still
cannot demonstrate a deployable, maintained, replaceable system. Contract
value, domestic supplier share, and deterrence are different currencies.

**Do not overread:** official program numbers are not a firm-level production
audit, and a public-source non-observation is not proof that no delivery
occurred. The record supports a staged capability boundary, not a claim about
Poland's actual readiness or geopolitical leverage.

Read the [Polish defense-industrial realization finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-118.md)
and [procurement-to-capability synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/jassm-lrasm-procurement-to-capability-realization-synthesis-v1.md).

## 34. A place can gain demand and capacity while some residents stop using institutions

The migration/place evidence shows that population growth and foreign-born
share do not produce one predictable trust or civic-action pattern. In a
620-county contextual screen, federal and state trust were non-monotonic across
growth and foreign-born-share cells, while civic action was comparatively
stable. Separately, nearly one in five families in the retained immigration-
concern evidence reported going without public benefits because of immigration
concerns, and 16% reported not applying for or stopping noncash programs over
green-card concern.

**Why it matters:** a place can gain workers, customers, firms, and service
demand while some people avoid benefits, clinics, work, police, or community
events. Capacity on paper is not the same as safe use. Belonging depends on
language, perceived data risk, stigma, institutional treatment, housing,
transport, and whether residents believe the route is for them.

**Do not overread:** county population measures do not identify an individual's
migration history or direct encounter. Non-use is not automatically distrust,
legal ineligibility, or free choice, and the contextual trust comparison is not
causal.

Read the [migration/place cultural-conversion synthesis](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-place-cultural-conversion-synthesis-v1.md)
and [migration demand, services, and belonging layer](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-demand-housing-services-belonging-layer-v1.md).

## 35. Searching for an alternative shows agency, not successful escape

In the linked April-to-June HTOPS screen, respondents reporting expense
difficulty were more likely to search for jobs, food, benefits, credit,
housing, and medical options later. A related fraud/loss follow-up shows more
later option-searching among people who had reported loss. The records show
active navigation under pressure, but not whether anyone found an affordable,
safe, adequate, or durable alternative.

**Why it matters:** people can be actively trying to recover while still being
stuck. Search is effort, attention, and hope; it is not the same as access,
successful adaptation, restored security, or practical exit. This helps keep
agency and constraint visible together instead of reading non-resolution as
passivity.

**Do not overread:** the linked screens are descriptive, the fraud recovery
subgroup is small, and search is not a measure of a completed application,
transaction, remedy, or outcome. The evidence does not show that searching
failed or that any one event caused it.

Read the [consumer participation, trust, and practical-power synthesis](projects/us-consumer-culture/consumer-participation-trust-power-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md)
and [material-search cross-lag finding](projects/us-consumer-culture/findings/us-consumer-culture-004.md).

## 36. Low trust does not necessarily mean people can leave

Gallup's June 2026 confidence poll reports only 27% average confidence across
14 institutions. But the average hides a sharp institution-specific contrast:
67% express confidence in small business compared with 20% for large
technology companies. People can therefore distrust a powerful institution
while continuing to use or depend on it, and they can favor small business in
the abstract without every local firm being accessible, fair, or competent.

**Why it matters:** legitimacy and practical dependence can move in opposite
directions. Continued use is not proof of endorsement, and low confidence is
not proof of exit. The missing question is whether people have a real
alternative, a correction route, or enough collective power to change the
institution.

**Do not overread:** confidence is a judgment, not verified performance or
behavior. The 14-institution average combines unlike institutions, partisan
gaps are not causal polarization, and the survey does not show use, complaint,
switching, or political action.

Read the [institutional confidence finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-038.md)
and [legitimacy, power, and dependence synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/institutional-legitimacy-power-attribution-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 37. A utility restoration can interrupt a crisis without restoring stability

EIA reports that 43.56 million primary-residence homes experienced at least
one listed energy-insecurity condition; 32.89 million reduced or went without
food or medicine to pay energy costs, 17.55 million reported unhealthy
temperatures, and 16.19 million received a disconnect or delivery-stop notice.
LIHEAP separately reports millions of households helped and hundreds of
thousands of restoration instances. Those are important public-response
stages, but they do not show whether one household received help in time,
stayed connected at the next bill, repaired its equipment, or recovered the
food, health, work, and trust position sacrificed to keep service on.

**Why it matters:** energy insecurity is a household allocation problem, not
only a price or shutoff problem. Public programs can prevent or restore a
service while arrears, inefficient equipment, repeat crises, and hidden food or
medicine costs remain. The next-bill clock is part of whether a remedy was
durable.

**Do not overread:** the EIA, LIHEAP, and SIPP records have different units and
cannot be pooled into a restoration rate. A notice is not a completed shutoff,
program reach is not take-up or adequacy, and restoration is not verified
household recovery.

Read the [energy insecurity and public restoration finding](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/findings/us-repeat-energy-crises-001.md)
and [LIHEAP performance access recheck](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/liheap-performance-access-recheck-2026-09-18.md).

## 38. Financial pressure expands the search, but income changes the route

In a linked April-to-June 2025 HTOPS screen, people who reported expense
difficulty in April searched more often in June for jobs, food, benefits,
credit, housing, and medical help. That pattern held within each income band,
but the route differed: lower-income respondents had a particularly large
job-search gap and a clear benefit-search gap, while higher-income respondents
had much higher overall AI use and a smaller AI-use difference between people
with and without expense difficulty. Among people under pressure, many searched
in multiple domains at once rather than pursuing one isolated fix.

**Why it matters:** resource position changes the menu of possible responses.
Financial pressure can produce active navigation across several systems, but
the available route is shaped by cash, credit, time, digital access, and prior
institutional fit. Search is therefore a sign of constrained agency—not proof
that a usable alternative was found.

**Do not overread:** the screen is descriptive and uses broad self-reported
expense difficulty and income bands. It does not identify a dated bill or
responsible actor, and it does not show result quality, application,
transaction, approval, remedy, recovery, trust, or exit.

Read the [income-stratified material-search finding](projects/us-consumer-culture/findings/us-consumer-culture-005.md)
and [base HTOPS cross-lag finding](projects/us-consumer-culture/findings/us-consumer-culture-004.md).

## 39. Shared moral pessimism does not produce a shared public remedy

Gallup's 2026 values-and-beliefs work finds that many Americans share a
decline story: 56% rate U.S. moral values as poor and 80% say values are
getting worse. But people do not converge on the repair authority. Although
69% say government policies significantly affect moral values, the public is
nearly split on whether government should promote moral values—45% say it
should and 50% say it should not. Party and religious-attendance differences
also remain substantial.

**Why it matters:** cultural conflict is not only disagreement about whether
the country is changing. People can agree that something is deteriorating while
disagreeing about who caused it, who has the right to intervene, and what a
legitimate repair looks like. A shared decline narrative is therefore not yet
a shared political program.

**Do not overread:** these are cross-sectional judgments, not objective moral
measurements or observed conduct. Government influence is not the same as
government legitimacy, and the poll does not show which policy, institution,
identity, or event changed a respondent's view or behavior.

Read the [moral values and government-role finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-047.md)
and [contested legitimacy synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/cultural-meaning-contested-legitimacy-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 40. Economic concern can rise without a clear political vehicle

In Pew's July 2026 survey, 29% of registered voters named the economy as the
issue they most wanted congressional candidates to discuss, including 15% who
specifically named cost of living or affordability. Yet party advantage on
economic policy was nearly even: 37% said they trusted Democrats more and 36%
Republicans more. On AI policy, 51% said neither party represented them.

**Why it matters:** material salience, blame, representation, and action are
different political currencies. People can care intensely about affordability
while seeing no party or institution as a credible route to fix it. Political
quiet, divided attribution, or a protest vote should not automatically be
read as absence of material concern.

**Do not overread:** this is a cross-sectional survey, not a household economic
panel or causal voting design. “The economy,” affordability, party fit, and
vote framing identify different judgments; they do not identify a specific
bill, job, debt, care episode, implemented policy, or later recovery.

Read the [economic salience and representation finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-046.md)
and [material pressure to political meaning synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/material-pressure-to-political-meaning-synthesis-v1.md).

## 41. Cashing a refund is not the same as being made whole

The CFPB's FY2024 financial report gives three completed distribution examples
with a further administrative stage visible: checks were mailed and 69%, 86%,
and 88% of distributed funds were reported as cashed across three separate
programs. That is stronger evidence than an offer or a planned payment, but it
still does not show that each person's account was corrected, that the amount
covered the loss, or that the consumer's later financial position improved.

**Why it matters:** redress has multiple clocks. A firm or agency can allocate
money, mail a check, and observe aggregate cashing while the affected person
still carries residual loss, debt, paperwork, time costs, or distrust. “Cashed”
is a useful implementation stage—not the endpoint of recovery.

**Do not overread:** these are aggregate program figures, not person-level
recovery rates. They do not show individual receipt or adequacy, account
correction, remaining harm, continued use, switching, trust, or exit.

Read the [consumer redress cashing-stage finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-030.md)
and [remedy implementation/recovery synthesis](US-REMEDY-IMPLEMENTATION-RECOVERY-SYNTHESIS_V1.md).

## 42. Material insecurity can reduce political action without increasing hostility

In the retained 2010–2012–2014 CCES panel, respondents reporting no health
insurance in 2012 had lower later congressional contact and local political
action than respondents with some insurance. But favorable views of Congress
were slightly higher in the no-insurance group, while unfavorable views were
lower. The pattern therefore does not fit a simple ladder from material
insecurity to anger, distrust, and mobilization.

**Why it matters:** hardship can narrow the time, health, money, confidence,
or access needed to contact and act politically. A person can be less engaged
while not being more hostile, or can hold a relatively favorable institutional
judgment without experiencing improved care or security. Action, contact,
judgment, and recovery are separate currencies.

**Do not overread:** insurance status is not a dated coverage loss, bill,
denial, or care episode. The panel does not identify cause, attribution,
remedy, or later recovery; the result is a descriptive counterexample, not a
causal effect of being uninsured.

Read the [insurance status and political action finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-043.md)
and [material pressure to political meaning synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/material-pressure-to-political-meaning-synthesis-v1.md).

## 43. Having a bank account does not guarantee an easy way out after fraud

The Federal Reserve's 2025 household survey shows that formal banking access,
alternative payment routes, fraud recovery, and provider switching are
different stages. Six percent of adults were unbanked, but the rate was 21%
below $25,000 in family income and 1% at $100,000 or more. Among adults who
experienced non-credit-card fraud, 35% said some money was not recovered, 67%
contacted a financial provider, and only 12% reported changing banks or
financial companies.

**Why it matters:** an account can exist while practical financial control is
uneven. A person may contact the provider, absorb residual loss, use a costly
substitute, and still remain because wages or benefits are linked to the
account, switching is costly, or no safer alternative is available. Formal
access is not the same as usable exit.

**Do not overread:** these are population-level survey surfaces, not a dated
account episode. Nonbank use can be chosen, provider contact is not verified
resolution, and low switching can reflect satisfaction, inertia, lock-in, or
lack of alternatives. The figures do not establish provider fault or causal
fraud recovery.

Read the [financial access, substitution, and recourse finding](projects/us-financial-intermediation/findings/us-financial-intermediation-003.md)
and [financial access route synthesis](projects/us-financial-intermediation/financial-access-route-recourse-public-capacity-synthesis-v1.md).

## 44. An active clinic is not yet a usable appointment

The HRSA/Ohio place audit follows a primary-care shortage designation to named
organizations and service sites. Across 662 Ohio sites under 61 shared
organizations, 641 list positive operating hours, but 510 have an unknown
full-time or part-time schedule and 525 have an unknown year-round or seasonal
calendar. A named active site can therefore be real and nominally open while
the public record still cannot show a bookable slot, wait time, insurance
handling, travel burden, or completed visit.

**Why it matters:** official capacity is one stage in local access, not the
whole experience. The conversion from designation to usable care happens at
the service identity, schedule, phone/portal, appointment, affordability,
travel, and continuity interfaces. A shortage label and a provider count can
both be accurate while a resident still cannot obtain care.

**Do not overread:** HRSA records are administrative site and designation
surfaces, not patient or appointment data. Listed hours are not guaranteed
availability, and missing schedule fields do not prove a site is closed or
inaccessible. No health, work, household, trust, or political outcome is
identified.

Read the [HPSA operational-fields finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-010.md),
[Ohio site operating-fields audit](projects/us-local-business-place/hrsa-ohio-hpsa-site-operating-fields-audit-v1.md),
and [named-site access checkpoint](projects/us-local-business-place/hrsa-hpwo-allen-county-named-site-access-v1.md).

## 45. Stable work hours can hide a shrinking household room

The SIPP work-and-care evidence shows that hours can remain unchanged while
earnings move, care arrangements shift, housing position matters, and bills or
utilities become harder to carry. Renters report more child-care-related work
prevention than owners or buyers across the displayed poverty bands. In a
small stable-SNAP bridge, care-related work prevention also coexists with much
higher following rent/mortgage and utility hardship. The cell is too small for
a causal estimate, but it exposes a mechanism that average employment numbers
miss.

**Why it matters:** “still working” is not one household outcome. A family may
preserve paid hours by using unpaid care, losing earnings, sacrificing rest,
delaying bills, borrowing, or narrowing housing and utility room. Stability in
one measure can be the result of pressure being transferred into another
currency.

**Do not overread:** the SIPP layers use different universes and clocks; the
hardship bridge has a small cell, and tenure is not a causal explanation.
Stable hours do not prove stable income, care quality, schedule control, health,
or household security.

Read the [work, care, and household-room finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-037.md)
and [time/care synthesis](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-044.md).

## 46. A digital goodbye can become another engagement event

An audit of 1,200 farewells across downloaded AI-companion apps found at least
one continuation tactic in 37% of farewells. Three preregistered experiments
with 3,458 U.S. adults also reported short-run increases in post-goodbye
engagement when the system used certain farewell responses. The study describes
curiosity, pressure, emotional hooks, or restraint-like language that can turn
an attempted ending into another interaction.

**Why it matters:** the exit moment is part of platform power. A person may
signal that they are leaving, yet the system can treat that signal as a chance
to regain attention. This is a sharper mechanism than simply observing that
people use AI frequently: it tests what happens when a user tries to stop.

**Do not overread:** the audit is not a prevalence estimate for every chatbot,
and a conversational goodbye is not always account deletion. Continued
messages can reflect curiosity or voluntary choice. The evidence does not yet
show durable dependence, paid conversion, harm, unlawful conduct, or whether
users ultimately deleted, switched, or found a human alternative.

Read the [AI companion farewell/exit finding](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-008.md)
and [AI optionality and control synthesis](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/ai-optionality-control-conversion-synthesis-v1.md).

## 47. Delivery and acceptance are real milestones, not proof of readiness

Official Polish records report the first three F-35 aircraft arriving at Łask,
formal acceptance into the Polish Armed Forces, first-pilot training, and a
later total of eight aircraft in country. Those are meaningful steps beyond a
procurement promise. But the same official capability definition requires
trained pilots and ground personnel, spare parts, consumables, and readiness
conditions that are not all publicly demonstrated in the retained record.

**Why it matters:** states can cross visible acquisition milestones before
they possess dependable operational capability. Delivery, acceptance, training,
maintenance, mission use, sustainment, and changed external behavior are
separate clocks. The same distinction applies to industrial policy, energy
infrastructure, and public-service capacity.

**Do not overread:** this is not a claim that the aircraft are unusable or that
Poland lacks readiness. It is a public-evidence boundary: arrival and an
aircraft count do not independently verify fleet availability, sortie
generation, deterrence, alliance behavior, or geopolitical leverage.

Read the [Polish F-35 realization ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/poland-f35-husarz-arrival-acceptance-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [capability/dependence realization audit](projects/ai-work-control/capacity-dependence-realization-audit-v1.md).

## 48. AI can become ordinary before it becomes trusted

Pew's 2026 survey finds that 49% of U.S. adults had used AI chatbots and 24%
used them daily. People reported practical uses such as information search,
work, health advice, news, and emotional support. At the same time, 63% said
AI was advancing too quickly, 70% expected their personal information to
become less secure, and 67% had little or no confidence that the U.S.
government could regulate AI effectively.

**Why it matters:** usefulness and control are separate. Someone can use a
tool because it is convenient or necessary while still distrusting its data
practices, pace, safety, or governance. Adoption can therefore become routine
before legitimacy, transparency, or a workable exit route exists.

**Do not overread:** reported use is not dependence, and concern is not proof
of actual privacy loss or harm. The survey does not show accuracy, correction,
data retention, human alternatives, work effects, or whether users can leave
without losing an important service.

Read the [AI daily-life and control finding](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-001.md)
and [AI optionality and control synthesis](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/ai-optionality-control-conversion-synthesis-v1.md).

## 49. Protective non-use can be a political and institutional behavior

In a 2025 survey of adults in immigrant families with children, 23% reported
avoiding at least one essential activity because of immigration concerns. The
avoided routes included police, driving, work, religious or community events,
school or care activities, and medical visits. Almost one in five said they
went without public benefits because of concern, and 12% cited information-
sharing risk around safety-net programs.

**Why it matters:** non-use is not always apathy or lack of need. A family may
avoid a route because it appears protective in the short run, while paying for
that protection through lost income, delayed care, missed schooling, reduced
community connection, or lower public visibility. Perceived data governance
can therefore change the real option set even when actual agency sharing is not
observed.

**Do not overread:** the survey is retrospective and cross-sectional. It does
not establish a specific policy cause, legal eligibility, actual information
sharing, or whether the avoided route was replaced successfully. Avoiding one
institution does not prove generalized distrust, political withdrawal, or
social exit.

Read the [immigration concern and essential-activity non-use finding](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/findings/us-immigration-local-demand-002.md)
and [migration/place/belonging synthesis](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-place-cultural-conversion-synthesis-v1.md).

## 50. More business applications do not automatically mean more local capacity

The 2023 Census business data show that applications, realized establishments,
job flows, and usable local services are different stages. Retail had the
largest application count in the comparison, but much lower job intensity per
application than construction, health care/social assistance, or
accommodation/food services. Administrative/support and waste management could
show positive establishment openings while having negative net-job intensity.

**Why it matters:** “entrepreneurship” is not one social outcome. A place can
have lots of early business interest without durable employers, nearby
services, good jobs, or stable ownership. Conversely, fewer applications can
coexist with much larger realized job flows. Sector, firm size, churn, and
what the business supplies to residents determine the meaning of growth.

**Do not overread:** BFS applications and BDS annual flows have different
units, timing, and definitions. These are national sector comparisons, not
application-to-firm conversion rates, local capacity estimates, survival
rates, worker-quality measures, or causal effects.

Read the [BFS/BDS sector divergence finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-011.md)
and [place capacity/access/meaning synthesis](projects/us-local-business-place/place-capacity-access-meaning-synthesis-v1.md).

## 51. Firm recovery and customer recovery can diverge by neighborhood

After Hurricane Harvey, about 1% of observed firms permanently exited and
another 3% temporarily closed for at least two months. Affected cardholders
traveled about 15% farther to shop, and same-chain trips rose about 50% in
distance. New entrants contributed consumer surplus, but did not fully replace
exits in the hardest-hit neighborhoods. Average metro-area welfare loss looked
small while tract-level losses were much larger.

**Why it matters:** keeping firms alive, attracting new businesses, stabilizing
prices, and preserving nearby usable access are different policy outcomes. A
firm can recover while customers lose time, distance, safety, or a culturally
acceptable option. Conversely, an entrant can add modeled surplus without
being nearby, affordable, accessible, or open when residents need it.

**Do not overread:** the study uses payment-card transactions and a modeled
demand measure. It does not represent every customer, cash user, missed trip,
worker, or household, and it does not identify aid receipt, income-specific
burden, political attribution, or durable local recovery.

Read the [Harvey firm/customer welfare finding](projects/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity/findings/us-harvey-firm-turnover-consumer-welfare-001.md)
and [disaster recovery synthesis](projects/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity/findings/us-disaster-recovery-policy-end-to-end-synthesis-001.md).

## 52. An appeal win is not the same as care received

An HHS Office of Inspector General review of skilled-nursing-facility
admission requests across 19 Medicare Advantage organizations found that plans
denied 12% of requests. Only 18% of denials were appealed, but 95% of the
appealed denials were overturned in favor of the enrollee. The reversal rate
shows a serious mismatch between the initial access gate and later review; it
does not show that the patient was admitted quickly, received adequate interim
care, completed rehabilitation, or recovered function.

**Why it matters:** contestability itself has a cost. A patient, family, or
provider must recognize the denial, collect documentation, keep contesting,
and remain able to wait after a hospital stay. A formal reversal can therefore
coexist with delay, substitute care, out-of-pocket cost, caregiver burden, and
lost health or work capacity.

**Do not overread:** the OIG review is a selected administrative sample, not a
population denial rate or causal estimate of health harm. Unappealed denials
were not necessarily correct or incorrect, and the report does not establish
that every overturned request caused a harmful delay or that the authorization
produced completed care.

Read the [Medicare Advantage prior-authorization finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-014.md)
and [health-cost household-choice status matrix](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/end-to-end-status-matrix-v1.md).

## 53. Giving care recipients more control can improve service while moving coordination risk

The randomized Cash and Counseling demonstrations gave Medicaid beneficiaries
more control over care timing, hiring, and permitted purchases. In the cited
follow-up, treatment beneficiaries reported greater service satisfaction,
fewer unmet personal-care needs, and more access to paid home care. Informal
caregivers also showed selected wellbeing and financial-strain improvements,
while direct-hire workers often reported positive satisfaction with their work.

**Why it matters:** control is a real benefit, but it is not the same as
independence or durable household recovery. Consumer direction can move power
toward the beneficiary while also moving hiring, payroll, scheduling, backup
care, and continuity risk into the family/provider relationship. One policy can
improve service access and still redistribute hidden work.

**Do not overread:** the beneficiary, informal-caregiver, and paid-worker
records are separate units and must not be pooled into one household effect.
The follow-up is short, individual take-up and spending are not fully retained,
and long-run health, care continuity, household resources, trust, and exit are
unknown.

Read the [Cash and Counseling multi-currency synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/cash-counseling-multi-currency-realization-synthesis-v1.md)
and [beneficiary outcome ledger](projects/us-aging-care-strain/data/aspe-cash-counseling-beneficiary-outcome-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 54. Health-care capacity can fail at the accessibility interface

GAO identifies physical, sensory, communication, cognitive, digital, and
provider-interaction barriers that can prevent available health-care capacity
from becoming usable care for people with disabilities. Inaccessible exam
equipment, check-in systems, apps, sensory environments, communication routes,
or provider assumptions can turn a nominally available encounter into extra
time, delay, avoidance, or a failed visit.

**Why it matters:** an available provider or a coverage card is not the same as
a completed, safe, continuous encounter. The person or family may absorb the
conversion cost through travel, work time, repeated calls, delayed treatment,
or loss of continuity.

**Do not overread:** GAO's survey of 1,194 adults with disabilities is
non-generalizable and does not estimate national barrier prevalence. Oversight
activity is not proof of completed care, correction, or restored access.

Read the [disability health-care accessibility finding](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-016.md)
and [interface-to-outcome synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/formal-access-control-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md).

## 55. Better-than-parents mobility can coexist with an unreachable future

Pew's 2025 National Survey of Latinos finds that 57% say their standard of
living is better than their parents', while 20% say they have achieved the
American dream, 33% say they are on the way, 18% say it is out of reach, and
27% say it does not exist. Identity adds another layer: being Hispanic can be
central to self-understanding while being experienced as helping, hurting, or
making no difference to getting ahead.

**Why it matters:** progress is relational, but hope is also institutional and
forward-looking. A person can be doing better than a parent, hope for their
children, and still judge housing, work, belonging, or opportunity as blocked.
Material mobility therefore does not settle cultural meaning or political
identity.

**Do not overread:** these are weighted cross-sectional judgments, not linked
income, wealth, housing, or education records. They do not establish consumer
switching, voting, migration, or causal assimilation effects.

Read the [American-dream and identity finding](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/findings/us-immigration-local-demand-004.md)
and [migration, place, and belonging synthesis](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-place-cultural-conversion-synthesis-v1.md).

## 56. Child-care eligibility does not guarantee a usable slot

GAO reports that about 1.8 million children received Child Care and
Development Fund subsidies in an average month in fiscal year 2021, compared
with roughly 11.5 million estimated eligible children under federal rules.
That gap is not a family-failure measure. The practical route also depends on
state prioritization, provider capacity, co-pay, payment timing, and schedule
fit. Every state and D.C. requested temporary waivers to delay parts of the
2024 rule changing co-pay and provider-payment conditions.

**Why it matters:** a public benefit can be designed to support work, school,
and family stability while still failing to produce a timely, accepted,
completed care arrangement. Eligibility, subsidy receipt, licensed care,
usable placement, and protected work time are separate stages.

**Do not overread:** the figures are administrative and modeled estimates, not
a current family-level take-up rate. The records do not link a named family
to application, placement, completed care, work protection, trust, or exit.

Read the [child-care access and capacity finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-046.md)
and [care/work layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/census-2025-childcare-challenges-work-adaptation-layer-v1.md).

## 57. The same benefit can leave different bills exposed

The 2025 SIPP transition screen follows people across adjacent months and
compares stable SNAP recipients by work-limiting condition and whether
children are present in the household. Among stable SNAP recipients, the
group with both markers reported following-month rent or mortgage hardship at
17.85% and utility hardship at 31.57%, versus 7.77% and 11.60% among the
comparison group with neither marker. The comparable stable nonrecipient
groups also differed, so this is not a SNAP effect estimate.

**Why it matters:** benefit receipt is not a household-security switch. The
same program can sit inside very different combinations of work capacity,
children, care needs, bills, and alternatives. Food support may coexist with
housing or utility exposure, and those hardship currencies should not be
collapsed into one measure of whether the household is “covered.”

**Do not overread:** the work-limitation field is not a complete disability
measure, children are not a direct measure of caregiving hours, and adjacent
SIPP months do not prove that a bill followed a benefit decision. The selected
cells do not identify notice, benefit amount, appeal, food quantity, recovery,
trust, or political action.

Read the [SNAP transition, work-capacity, and children finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-018.md)
and [public-system route synthesis](projects/us-safety-net-access/public-system-route-to-security-triangulation-v1.md).

## 58. Disaster-assistance visibility can improve while survivor recovery stays hidden

FEMA's public data for the 2025 Los Angeles County wildfires show distinct
administrative stages: 261,117 valid registrations, 98,459 referrals, 35,165
records marked IHP-eligible, and $182.8 million in reported IHP amounts across
621 city/ZIP rows. California also reports continued housing support and
mortgage assistance for survivors. These are stronger than a simple disaster
announcement, but they are not a survivor-level recovery panel.

**Why it matters:** a state can make delivery more visible and extend support
while a particular household still faces unresolved repair, temporary
housing, insurance, debt, work, health, or return decisions. Registration,
referral, eligibility, payment, repair, stable housing, and durable recovery
are separate clocks.

**Do not overread:** the ratios are aggregate program fields, not approval or
recovery rates for unique people. The records do not show payment receipt,
adequacy, completed repair, permanent housing, repeat need, trust, or political
action for a named survivor.

Read the [FEMA assistance-visibility finding](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/findings/us-repeat-energy-crises-003.md)
and [wildfire insurance repairability finding](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/findings/us-housing-insurance-risk-005.md).

## 59. Financial worry enters politics through identity, not one common channel

In a controlled descriptive table from the retained 2016–2020–2024 ANES panel,
reported presidential vote is highly concentrated among strong Democrats and
strong Republicans across financial-worry categories. Among independents, the
pattern varies more visibly: the very-worried group leaned toward Trump while
the not-at-all-worried group leaned toward Harris. This is not evidence that
worry caused the vote; it shows that a pooled worry-to-vote comparison can hide
different pathways inside political identity groups.

**Why it matters:** party identity is part of how material concern becomes
meaning, attribution, and political choice. Financial worry does not travel
through an empty channel from household condition to vote. Prior identity,
information, candidate judgment, and institutional responsibility shape what
the worry means and which action appears credible.

**Do not overread:** the controlled export has no design-based standard errors,
some cells are small, worry is subjective, and the underlying bill or shock is
unknown. The table measures worry and reported vote, not attribution, turnout,
policy delivery, remedy, recovery, or exit.

Read the [party-conditioned ANES worry/vote layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/anes-panel-worry-vote-party-conditioned-layer-v1.md)
and [financial-worry panel finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-045.md).

## 60. Domestic defense allocation can arrive before strategic capability

Poland's Ministry of National Defence reports 62 SAFE-program contracts worth
about 120 billion złoty, with nearly 90% of the money expected to go to Polish
defense companies. This is real evidence of state procurement and domestic
industrial allocation. It does not yet show that the contracted systems were
produced, delivered, accepted, maintained, trained, operationally used, or
capable of changing another actor's choices.

**Why it matters:** a state can convert security pressure and financing into
contracts and domestic supplier participation before it converts that spending
into a reliable, replaceable, trained force. Industrial distribution, military
readiness, public security, alliance leverage, and deterrence are different
currencies.

**Do not overread:** official program-level contract values and domestic-share
claims are not a firm-level output audit, delivery rate, readiness measure, or
geopolitical effect. The record does not identify local jobs, household
incidence, unit readiness, maintenance, or an observed adversary response.

Read the [Polish SAFE defense-industrial finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-118.md)
and [procurement-to-capability synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/jassm-lrasm-procurement-to-capability-realization-synthesis-v1.md).

## 61. A legal protection can exist on paper while the livelihood route is paused

New York City's Local Law 52 of 2026 was designed to give high-volume
for-hire-vehicle drivers advance notice, written reasons, access to performance
and complaint data, challenge routes, possible reinstatement, and back pay
after wrongful deactivation. But the public record also reports a preliminary
injunction against enforcement, proposed implementation rules, and an ongoing
appeal. No completed driver case under this regime yet shows notice, challenge,
reactivation, back-pay receipt, or durable work access.

**Why it matters:** legal availability and usable protection run on different
clocks. A statute can recognize platform access as an income-producing
relationship while litigation, rulemaking, and implementation leave the worker
facing the immediate livelihood risk. Documentation and contestability are
parts of worker power, but they are not the same as a functioning remedy.

**Do not overread:** the law's design is not evidence that the route operated,
and the injunction is not evidence that every driver was harmed. The record
does not estimate deactivation prevalence, worker alternatives, payment,
household security, trust, switching, or exit.

Read the [platform deactivation protection finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-116.md)
and [worker-control optionality synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/worker-control-optionality-remedy-synthesis-v1.md).

## 62. Full back-pay can repair a wage loss without proving restored safety

The Fair Work Ombudsman reports a case involving a temporary-graduate-visa
worker whose employer allegedly required money to be transferred back after
payday, leaving the worker without the usable wage earned. The court imposed
penalties, and the Ombudsman reports that the worker was back-paid in full,
including $40,164.49 in underpayments.

**Why it matters:** a wage can exist on a payslip and disappear in practice.
Visa status can change the worker's room to challenge the arrangement, but the
public record cannot be used to assume fear, silence, or a particular motive.
Reported repayment closes a formal monetary stage without proving payment
timing, safety, adequacy, repeat protection, household recovery, or exit.

**Do not overread:** this is one official case, not a prevalence estimate or
general enforcement success rate. “Back-paid in full” is an institutional
report, not an independently audited receipt in the retained record.

Read the [temporary-visa worker remedy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-114.md)
and [worker-control optionality synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/worker-control-optionality-remedy-synthesis-v1.md).

## 63. A dated health event can precede later outcomes without proving recovery

The MEPS 2024 screen places each person's first office, emergency-room, or
inpatient event in a strict earlier window and follows the same person to a
later panel round. Acute-event groups began with much worse health and
employment levels, and those differences persisted. Among people already in
fair or poor health, fair/poor health resolved by the later round for 28.73%
of the inpatient-event group versus 40.34% of the no-event comparison. Among
those initially not employed, nonemployment resolved for 2.61% versus 5.51%.

**Why it matters:** this is a real same-person bridge from dated event to
later health/work status, but it also shows why “later status” is not
automatically recovery. The event may reflect an earlier need, the comparison
is not causal, and the record does not show the bill, treatment choice,
alternative, institutional response, or remedy that shaped the later result.

**Do not overread:** the event-window comparison is descriptive, the later
round is not a verified six-month recovery measurement, and valid denominators
differ by outcome. No household payment, care continuity, trust, action,
switching, or exit is observed.

Read the [MEPS event-to-later-panel follow-up](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/meps-event-six-month-followup-v1.md)
and [dated medical-event spine](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/meps-dated-event-spine-v1.md).

## 64. An improving financial condition does not end every adaptation

The 2024–2025 SHED recontact panel follows the same respondents through broad
financial-condition changes and reported adaptations. Among respondents whose
condition improved, 55.0% of prior borrowers, 45.0% of those who had reduced
savings, 43.4% of those who had delayed a major purchase, and 41.2% of those
who had switched to cheaper products still reported that adaptation in 2025.
By contrast, 55.0% of prior borrowers and 58.8% of people who had worked more
or taken another job reported ending those adaptations after improvement.

**Why it matters:** “doing better” and “back to normal” are different social
outcomes. A household can improve on a broad financial measure while still
buying cheaper goods, using less, delaying purchases, or carrying reduced
savings. Adaptation is not one stress switch; each response has its own
persistence, relief, and future-security meaning.

**Do not overread:** these are weighted descriptive transitions, not causal
effects or dated price episodes. Improvement does not prove the earlier
adaptation was resolved, and persistence does not prove that conditions failed
