to improve. The panel does not observe a bill, firm, remedy, trust change,
political action, or exit.

Read the [SHED financial-condition and adaptation persistence layer](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/shed-2024-2025-panel-persistence-layer-v1.md)
and [household adaptation and recovery record](records/us-household-adaptation-buffer-recovery-crosssource-2024-2025.json).

## 65. Job loss can reduce political action without producing one approval pattern

In the retained 2010–2012–2014 CCES panel, respondents reporting job loss had
a much higher later no-insurance share in 2012 than those without reported job
loss, 41.15% versus 12.72%, and lower local political action in 2014, 23.93%
versus 31.57%. Congressional contact was also slightly lower, 37.36% versus
39.14%, while favorable Congress approval was slightly higher, 13.86% versus
10.06%, and unfavorable approval was nearly the same.

**Why it matters:** work disruption, material security, institutional judgment,
and political action are separate currencies. A person can lose job-linked
security and become less politically active without expressing a simple rise in
hostility. Reduced action may reflect time, health, money, access, or issue
mismatch rather than satisfaction or trust.

**Do not overread:** the panel item recalls job loss over a two-year period and
does not identify the separation date, employer, cause, remedy, alternatives,
or income loss. The weighted contrasts are descriptive and do not establish a
causal job-loss effect, later recovery, or political withdrawal.

Read the [CCES job-loss political follow-up](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/cces-panel-job-loss-political-followup-v1.md)
and [material-status/political-action synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/material-status-political-action-divergence-synthesis-v1.md).

## 66. A remedy system can persist after distribution without proving recovery

The CFPB's FY2025 redress report shows later activity after earlier
distributions: $20,809 in direct payments to consumers who requested reissued
Premier Student Loan Center payments, plus later redistributions of $129,729
for U.S. Equity Advantage and $174,178 for SMART Payment Plan. This is a real
administrative stage beyond the first mailing or distribution.

**Why it matters:** the remedy has its own time path. Unclaimed or unresolved
funds can trigger reissue or redistribution while the affected person still
has to be located, informed, verified, and able to complete the route. A fund
remaining open does not show that the original consumer received adequate
correction or regained financial security.

**Do not overread:** the report gives aggregate later amounts, not recipient-
level receipt, timing, account correction, or adequacy. Redistributed money is
not automatically money returned to the same harmed people, and no trust,
switching, or exit outcome is observed.

Read the [CFPB redress persistence finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-031.md)
and [consumer redress cashing-stage finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-030.md).

## 67. Insurance restitution can reach several sides while recovery stays open

Virginia settlement summaries describe three different insurance failures
followed by reported restitution and prospective correction: about $2 million
to 2,959 Esurance consumers, about $2.5 million to 91,696 State Farm
consumers, and an Anthem/Carelon matter involving payments to 1,680 consumers,
586 providers, and the state. The cases therefore reach beyond an order into
reported money movement and system-control changes.

**Why it matters:** a single institutional error can distribute burden across
customers, providers, claims, premiums, and public systems. Aggregate
restitution and a corrective plan can coexist with unknown individual receipt,
corrected premiums or claims, restored coverage or care, provider retention,
trust, and switching.

**Do not overread:** these are institution-level settlement summaries, not a
representative consumer sample or a recipient-level payment file. A reported
corrective plan is not proof of lower future error, completed care, or restored
household security.

Read the [Virginia insurance-settlement remedy finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-029.md)
and [consumer recourse visibility/remedy synthesis](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/consumer-recourse-visibility-remedy-synthesis-v1.md).

## 68. A repeat refund can confirm prior receipt without proving later recovery

The FTC reports that ACRO Services first sent more than $3.8 million in
refunds, then sent 5,685 second-round checks totaling more than $6.2 million
to people who had accepted the first payment. The second distribution is
therefore conditioned on a real prior receipt stage, not merely an announced
fund or a broad eligible population.

**Why it matters:** reach can be strengthened without closing recovery. A
person can receive a second payment while the original debt, account position,
residual fees, and financial security remain unresolved. Recipient-conditioned
denominators should not be mistaken for the full harmed population or a
recovery rate.

**Do not overread:** the FTC reports checks sent, not second-check cashing,
debt correction, adequacy, trust, switching, or exit. Prior acceptance proves
one payment stage, not that the underlying consumer harm was repaired.

Read the [recipient-conditioned ACRO refund finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-032.md)
and [remedy implementation/recovery synthesis](US-REMEDY-IMPLEMENTATION-RECOVERY-SYNTHESIS_V1.md).

## 69. A work remedy can restore money without restoring the job—or restore the job only temporarily

Two public Fair Work Commission cases show different gaps between legal
remedy and durable work security. In Cartisano, back pay and continuity were
reported as paid, but the worker was not allowed to return while a medical and
risk assessment was used; the Full Bench held that the conditional arrangement
was not real and practical reinstatement. In Aitken, a union-supported return,
three months of back pay, and prior-service recognition were followed by a
later dismissal after a workplace safety conflict.

**Why it matters:** worker recovery has more than one currency. Money, access
to the workplace, status continuity, safety, and future employment can move in
different directions. A remedy can repair one part of the loss while leaving
the worker without usable work, or it can produce a real return that does not
last.

**Do not overread:** these are two adjudicated cases, not a worker-population
rate. The public records do not establish final payment details, household
recovery, later employment, health, trust, or exit, and the later dismissal in
Aitken is not proof that the earlier return agreement caused the conflict.

Read the [Cartisano implementation ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/fwc-cartisano-backpay-no-return-risk-assessment-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [Aitken durability ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/fwc-aitken-return-backpay-later-dismissal-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 70. Speaking up about pay can open an enforcement route while exposing the speaker to risk

The U.S. Department of Labor reported that an urgent-care operator failed to
pay workers correctly for required orientation, meetings, training, and some
off-the-clock work. The agency also reported that a worker who questioned the
pay practices was suspended. After its investigation, the Department reported
$113,199 in recovered back wages.

**Why it matters:** worker protection is not only the amount an agency later
reports. The route to enforcement may require a person to speak while still
depending on the employer for income, schedule, references, and continued
work. A public recovery can therefore coexist with an unknown personal cost of
voice, and silence cannot safely be read as satisfaction.

**Do not overread:** this is one agency-reported case, not a rate of
retaliation or wage theft. The public release does not identify the suspended
worker's payment, the suspension's duration, return or alternative work,
payroll correction for others, later trust, or willingness to speak again.

Read the [worker-voice and wage-remedy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-117.md)
and [worker control/optionality synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/worker-control-optionality-remedy-synthesis-v1.md).

## 71. An operating grid project can coexist with only a projected household benefit

Dominion reports that the Northern Virginia Wishing Star–Mars transmission
project was energized in January 2026 and completed construction in May. In
the same regional reliability context, the separate Golden–Mars project is
recorded only through route approval. Virginia's regulator also modeled a
change in the typical residential Rider T1 effect—from a projected $2.90
monthly increase to $0.94 under an amended cost-allocation method.

**Why it matters:** infrastructure has several clocks. A project can move from
approval to construction to operation while the household bill, reliability
gain, local compensation, and public acceptance remain unmeasured or only
projected. Operating capacity is not automatically an experienced benefit.

**Do not overread:** Wishing Star–Mars and Golden–Mars are separate projects;
the regulator's figures are projections, not observed bills. The public record
does not identify a data-center-specific household effect, outage change,
community compensation, or resident response.

Read the [Virginia infrastructure realization finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-106.md)
and [large-load governance ledger](projects/ai-work-control/virginia-large-load-governance-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 72. A formal compensation order can arrive after a worker has already exited on broken trust

In a Fair Work Commission case, one worker experienced persistent underpayment
and delayed wages, made repeated complaints, received Fair Work Ombudsman
assistance, resigned, and found new employment. The public record reports a
$4,000 partial payment and a later $27,424.88 gross compensation order. It
also records that the worker did not want to return to the original employer
because trust had broken down and the experience had caused emotional stress.

**Why it matters:** exit is not always free preference or successful mobility.
A worker can use a complaint route and later receive a formal remedy while the
original job, income timing, relationship, and willingness to return have
already been lost. The remedy can transfer liability back to the employer
without recreating the worker's earlier option set.

**Do not overread:** this is one adjudicated case, not a prevalence estimate.
The public record does not establish final payment receipt, residual loss,
quality of the new job, household recovery, longer-term trust, or collective
action.

Read the [worker-remedy and broken-trust finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-113.md)
and [strongest end-to-end chains](US-BROAD-STRONGEST-END-TO-END-CHAINS_V1.md).

## 73. A firm can repair its payroll system while individual recovery remains hidden

The Fair Work Ombudsman reports that 13 of 22 investigated aged-care providers
were non-compliant. Six residential-care providers reportedly back-paid more
than $2.17 million to 2,338 employees, and seven home-care providers more than
$3.13 million to 1,253 employees. In a named Southern Cross Care undertaking,
the provider reported back-paying 3,603 of 5,500 employees $10.14 million,
replacing its time-and-attendance system, adding independent audits and worker
feedback routes, and training staff.

**Why it matters:** this is more than a payout. A firm can change its internal
rules, monitoring, and worker-voice channels to reduce repeat harm. But firm
repair and worker recovery are different clocks: the public record can show a
new system while leaving each worker's payment date, net amount, adequacy,
household effect, and later trust or exit unknown.

**Do not overread:** the figures are investigation and undertaking reports, not
a sector-wide rate or worker-level receipt file. “Back-paid” is stronger than
an offer, but it does not prove that every worker was reached, paid adequately,
or protected from recurrence.

Read the [aged-care payroll-repair finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-115.md)
and [worker-control/optionality synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/worker-control-optionality-remedy-synthesis-v1.md).

## 74. Public land control can change an infrastructure plan before residents see the outcome

In Northern Virginia's Golden-Mars transmission project, the initially
preferred Route 4 required Loudoun County School Board consent. The School
Board did not consent to Dominion crossing its land, and the State Corporation
Commission later approved Route 3A instead.

**Why it matters:** infrastructure power is not held only by the utility or
the regulator. A public institution's control over land can change the approved
route, even when the larger transmission need remains. Public participation
can therefore alter a project decision—not merely register opposition—while
the distribution of property, health, payment, reliability, and acceptance
effects remains unresolved.

**Do not overread:** the route change does not show that Route 3A is better for
residents, that construction is complete, or that household burdens fell. The
public record does not yet link the route to parcel-level compensation,
energization, reliability, or resident response.

Read the [public-land-control infrastructure finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-105.md)
and [Virginia large-load governance ledger](projects/ai-work-control/virginia-large-load-governance-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 75. Public voice can be real without producing public agreement

In the Virginia Golden-Mars transmission case, the State Corporation
Commission reported more than 600 registered speakers and hundreds of pages of
written comments. Residents raised concerns about undergrounding, home value,
landscape, appearance, and possible health effects. The Commission later
approved the project, selected a route it judged to have the smallest community
impact, found undergrounding infeasible, and adopted mitigation recommendations.

**Why it matters:** participation is an institutional stage, not proof of
consent. People can gain a formal voice and still disagree with the decision,
carry unresolved costs, or lose confidence if they cannot see how their input
changed the result. Infrastructure legitimacy therefore has to be measured
after the hearing, not inferred from the existence of the hearing.

**Do not overread:** speaker and comment counts are not representative public
opinion and do not show which comments changed the decision. The record does
not establish construction, compensation, property or health effects, resident
acceptance, or later political action.

Read the [infrastructure public-response finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-104.md)
and [Virginia governance ledger](projects/ai-work-control/virginia-large-load-governance-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 76. The AI distribution problem starts before displacement

Three evidence layers point to different stages of the AI transition. An NBER
executive survey reports 69% active firm use, while 90% of executives report
no employment or productivity impact at their own firm over the prior three
years. The Federal Reserve's 2025 SHED reports AI use among 25% of workers,
with higher use among workers with graduate education and greater task control;
44% of workers said AI saved time and 52% of users said it improved quality.
An IMF model shows how wage inequality could fall while wealth inequality
rises under different adoption and ownership conditions.

**Why it matters:** the first distributional question may be who is allowed,
prepared, and trusted to use AI—not simply who loses a job. Access, education,
task control, training, permission, and ownership can shape who captures a
time or productivity gain before employment effects become visible.

**Do not overread:** these are different firms, workers, countries, periods,
and methods, not one pooled estimate. The evidence does not show that task
control causes AI use, that perceived benefits are objective gains, or that
the modeled inequality paths have already occurred in US households.

Read the [AI distribution finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-110.md)
and [worker AI access/control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-109.md).

## 77. Reinstatement can exist on paper while language, mobility, and family ties shape whether the job is usable

In a Fair Work Commission case, a Rohingya worker with limited English was
dismissed after an injury and workplace dispute. The public record says he
needed an interpreter, did not drive, lived about a 25-minute walk from the
workplace, and supported family in a refugee camp in Bangladesh. The Commission
ordered reinstatement, continuity, translation of the decision into Rohingya,
and $18,035.56 in lost pay.

**Why it matters:** cultural and material access are part of job quality. A
nearby job, an understandable process, and the ability to support family can
make the difference between a remedy that is usable and one that exists only in
legal language. Reinstatement and payment repair formal status; they do not
automatically restore communication, mobility, security, or belonging.

**Do not overread:** this is one adjudicated case, not a general result for
migrant, multilingual, Rohingya, or injured workers. The public record does not
show actual return, payment receipt, later job duration, changed communication,
household recovery, trust, or exit.

Read the [cultural-access worker-remedy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-112.md)
and [worker-control/optionality synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/worker-control-optionality-remedy-synthesis-v1.md).

## 78. A document barrier can be corrected by an agency and firm without proving durable work security

The Justice Department's Immigrant and Employee Rights Section reports two
FY2026 employment remedies. In one Alexandria case, a property-management
company offered reinstatement and paid $30,000 in back pay after an asylum-
I-94 document issue. In a New York City case, an employer paid $8,000 in front
and back pay after an E-Verify-related termination and changed its Form I-9 and
E-Verify procedures.

**Why it matters:** a status or verification rule can become a work-access and
income shock, then produce several different kinds of correction: money, an
offer of return, and a firm process change. These are meaningful institutional
stages, but the worker still needs an accepted job, usable documentation,
adequate income, and durable protection to regain real control.

**Do not overread:** these are selected agency case summaries, not a prevalence
rate. “Paid” is reported by the agency and does not show independent receipt,
adequacy, accepted reinstatement, later tenure, household recovery, trust, or
exit.

Read the [employment-document remedy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-107.md)
and [worker-control/optionality synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/worker-control-optionality-remedy-synthesis-v1.md).

## 79. Returning to platform work is not the same as recovering financially

In the Hotak matter, Uber deactivated a worker, then voluntarily restored
access. The adjudicated record describes stress, anxiety, feeling unsupported,
income loss, and financial hardship during the interruption. The worker later
resumed work and completed more than 150 trips. A subsequent formal order
required reactivation while the amount of lost remuneration remained for later
discussion.

**Why it matters:** this is a rare step beyond an account-status label. Actual
work activity shows that access was restored enough for the worker to act, but
it does not show that the worker recovered the lost income, had a free
alternative, or was protected from another deactivation. Continued use can
mean restored opportunity, dependence, or the absence of a workable substitute.

**Do not overread:** this is one Australian adjudicated case, not a platform-
worker rate. The record does not establish net pay, expenses, payment of lost
remuneration, durable access, household recovery, switching, or exit.

Read the [platform operational-return finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-092.md)
and [practical-exit cross-domain synthesis](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/practical-exit-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md).

## 80. Health-care friction does not produce one uniform health direction

Two separate MEPS 2024 longitudinal screens point in different directions.
Among people reporting prescription affordability or access delay in an earlier
round, 24.62% later reported improved perceived health and 19.12% reported
worsened health. Among people reporting denial or prior-authorization delay in
the other screen, 19.04% improved and 25.11% worsened. The comparison groups
also differed in their valid universes and timing.

**Why it matters:** “institutional friction” is too broad to be a complete
mechanism. Medication delay, authorization delay, illness severity, treatment
need, coverage, selection, and regression toward the mean can produce different
paths. The same broad label can contain people who recover, worsen, or remain
stable, so the route and episode matter.

The stricter event-linked follow-up adds a persistence clock. Among office
event respondents, fair/poor health resolved for 24.11% of the friction group
versus 37.57% of the no-friction group, while new fair/poor health appeared
for 10.02% versus 5.11%. The inpatient cells point in the same direction,
though they are small; the ER resolution contrast is much less distinct. Work
transitions are mixed across event families. This is evidence that friction can
remain visible on a later health clock, not evidence that denial caused the
outcome or that later health status is recovery.

**Do not overread:** these are separate weighted descriptive screens, not one
pooled causal estimate. They do not identify a claim, treatment completion,
payment, household substitution, remedy, trust, or exit, and perceived health
is not a clinical recovery measure.

Read the [route-specific health-friction finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-012.md)
and [MEPS event-field boundary](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-013.md),
as well as the [strict-window friction-to-later-panel audit](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/meps-friction-later-panel-v1.md).

## 81. Expense difficulty can carry forward into household pressure while institutional confidence moves unevenly

In linked April and June 2025 Census HTOPS files, 6,564 respondents could be
matched by `SCRAMID`. Respondents who reported difficulty paying usual expenses
in April later reported more food insufficiency, inability to pay an energy
bill, and recent household job loss in June than the no-difficulty group. Their
confidence was also lower in the presidency, military, police, Supreme Court,
public schools, and statistical agencies. Confidence in Congress was nearly
the same across the two groups.

**Why it matters:** material pressure can persist into several household
currencies while political judgment remains institution-specific. A household
may experience worsening food or energy pressure without translating that
experience into one generalized distrust of every institution. The responsible
actor, prior identity, issue, and available action route still matter.

**Do not overread:** this is a selected linked-sample descriptive association,
not a causal transition or a general population rate. The files do not supply
a dated bill, responsible actor, remedy, recovery, vote, complaint, organizing,
or exit outcome, and the confidence measures are not proof of trust change.

Read the [HTOPS expense-to-confidence finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-042.md)
and [material-pressure-to-political-meaning synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/material-pressure-to-political-meaning-synthesis-v1.md).

## 82. Retail sales can rise while real household consumption and firm health remain open

The Census Bureau's August 2026 advance release reports $773.9 billion in
seasonally adjusted retail and food-service sales, up 1.2% from July and 6.0%
from August 2025. These are current-dollar establishment sales. The release
does not show whether households bought more units, paid higher prices, traded
down in quality, used credit, or concentrated spending in larger channels.

**Why it matters:** market activity and household room are different
currencies. Sales can rise through price increases, borrowing, reduced savings,
lower-quality substitution, or spending by households with more resources. A
firm can also grow revenue while margins, labor conditions, local access, or
smaller competitors deteriorate.

**Do not overread:** the release is an aggregate, revisable establishment
estimate, not a household or firm panel. It does not identify real quantities,
payment/debt, margins, local survival, customer welfare, trust, political
attribution, or exit.

Read the [nominal-retail-activity finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-041.md)
and [consumer participation/trust/power synthesis](projects/us-consumer-culture/consumer-participation-trust-power-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 83. A high national median can coexist with several kinds of household insecurity

The Census 2025 CPS ASEC release reports real median household income of
$87,460, described as the highest in the series beginning in 1967. The same
release reports a 10.2% official poverty rate affecting 34.5 million people, a
13.4% child official-poverty rate, a 13.1% Supplemental Poverty Measure rate,
and 7.9% uninsured for the entire year—26.7 million people.

**Why it matters:** these measures run on different security clocks. A median
income can look strong while households still face poverty under another
definition, child-specific exposure, medical and geographic cost pressure, or
an entire year without insurance. National improvement does not tell us who
has liquid room, usable care, protected needs, or a recovery path.

**Do not overread:** the measures use different definitions and do not form a
common household trajectory. Median income is not disposable cash, poverty is
not a dated crisis, insurance is not care access, and none of these figures
identifies adaptation, trust, political action, or recovery for the same unit.

Read the [income, poverty, and insurance finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-040.md)
and [household financial-pressure synthesis](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/shed-2024-2025-panel-persistence-layer-v1.md).

## 84. Contact can remain while loneliness and support adequacy worsen

Two 2026 HTOPS analyses show that expense pressure can appear in different
social and institutional currencies. In the March release, frequent loneliness
rose from 1.8% among people reporting no difficulty with usual expenses to
30.2% among those reporting expenses very difficult. Low social or emotional
support rose from 14.0% to 40.9%, while frequent telephone or video contact was
comparatively flatter. In a separate July release, expense difficulty was
associated with lower confidence in federal statistical agencies and Congress,
but the gap was larger for statistical agencies.

**Why it matters:** contact is not the same as useful support, and confidence
is not one universal trust currency. People may remain in communication while
feeling lonely or unsupported; they may judge measurement institutions
differently from Congress. Cultural and political meaning depends on the kind
of relationship and institution being measured.

**Do not overread:** the March and July files are separate cross-sectional
snapshots, not a panel. The results do not establish that expense difficulty
caused loneliness or lower confidence, nor do they observe a named bill,
delivered help, remedy, recovery, vote, or exit.

Read the [material-pressure social-connection finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-033.md)
and [trust, meaning, and action layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/cross-source-trust-action-layer-v1.md).

## 85. A stable broad pressure measure can hide movement in food, energy, and political judgment

Corrected March, corrected May, and July 2026 Census HTOPS/HPS snapshots show
that household pressure does not move as one block. The share reporting any
difficulty paying usual expenses was 51.2% in March, 49.8% in May, and 49.8% in
July. Food insufficiency moved 6.5% to 8.3% to 7.4%; reducing or forgoing basic
needs to pay an energy bill moved 12.8% to 14.9% to 12.7%. Trust in federal
statistics moved 52.1% to 49.7% to 51.5%, while high confidence in Congress
moved 18.2% to 18.3% to 17.3%.

**Why it matters:** the broad question can look stable while narrower endpoints
change, and political judgments can move on their own clock. A household may
report similar overall expense difficulty while changing food or energy
behavior; a statistical-agency judgment can rise while confidence in Congress
falls. One national pressure index would hide those different currencies.

**Do not overread:** these are independent cross-sectional snapshots, not the
same households recovering or deteriorating. The measures have different
universes and reference periods and do not identify a dated price shock,
responsible actor, alternative, remedy, trust change, vote, or recovery.

Read the [three Census pulse snapshots finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-017.md)
and [material-pressure-to-political-meaning synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/material-pressure-to-political-meaning-synthesis-v1.md).

## 86. Fraud exposure becomes a household event only when loss is realized—and reported recovery is not restored security

In linked April-to-June 2025 HTOPS files, 6,564 respondents could be matched.
Retrospective scam exposure alone was not the same as later hardship; reported
monetary loss was the sharper stage. Among respondents reporting exposure and
loss, June food insufficiency was 23.82% and inability to pay an energy bill
was 43.81%. In the tiny group reporting loss, agency reporting, and recovery,
the corresponding figures were 26.57% and 78.80%.

**Why it matters:** a consumer story has several conversions: exposure, money
actually lost, reporting, possible recovery, and later household security. A
person can report that an agency recovered something while still showing severe
food or energy pressure. That does not prove the recovery failed; it shows why
reported recovery cannot be treated as a verified return to the prior position.

**Do not overread:** the recovery cell contains only 11 respondents and is not a
stable estimate. The survey recalls the prior twelve months, does not verify the
incident or payment, and does not identify the responsible actor, timing,
adequacy, trust change, switching, or exit.

Read the [fraud/loss follow-up finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-035.md)
and [consumer participation/trust/power synthesis](projects/us-consumer-culture/consumer-participation-trust-power-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 87. Availability is not use: the conversion cost sits at the interface

Across several separate evidence surfaces, formal capacity repeatedly fails to
guarantee usable opportunity. Airport transit can exist while reported use is
only 4% to 19% at reviewed airports. Health-care coverage or an appointment
can coexist with physical, communication, sensory, digital, or bias barriers.
Workforce programs can enroll people while interpretation, technology, outside
training, or guidance problems weaken completion. Medicaid or SNAP can coexist
with work without proving wage adequacy or security. Smartphones can provide
information and connection while users report excess time, sleep costs, and
incomplete attempts to cut back.

**Why it matters:** the decisive stage is interface fit—time, price,
information, physical usability, schedule, reliability, disclosure, and
alternatives. A route, benefit, program, device, or facility is not an outcome
because it exists. People often absorb failed conversion privately through
driving, waiting, family help, delayed care, lower disclosure, constrained work,
or continued dependence.

**Do not overread:** these domains use different units, samples, and methods;
they are not one pooled failure rate. The comparison identifies a recurring
mechanism, not a universal causal effect. The next test is person- or
organization-level evidence of attempted use, completed use, burden, remedy,
adaptation, and later durability.

Read the [availability-to-use conversion synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-034.md)
and [formal-access/control synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/formal-access-control-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md).

## 88. Economic concern can rise while political representation stays divided

In Pew's July 2026 survey, 29% of registered voters named the economy as the
issue they most wanted congressional candidates to discuss, including 15% who
specifically named cost of living or affordability. Yet party advantage on
economic policy was nearly even: 37% said they agreed more with Democrats and
36% with Republicans. On AI policy, 51% said neither party represented them.

**Why it matters:** people can care intensely about affordability without seeing
one clear political vehicle for fixing it. Material salience, blame, party
representation, attention, turnout, and policy delivery are different stages.
Economic concern does not automatically become a vote, a party alignment, or a
household remedy.

**Do not overread:** this is a cross-sectional survey. It does not identify a
respondent's particular bill, job, debt, care episode, responsible actor,
campaign contact, validated action, or later policy recovery.

Read the [economic attribution and representation finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-046.md)
and [material-to-political-meaning synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/material-pressure-to-political-meaning-synthesis-v1.md).

## 89. Moral decline diagnosis does not settle government authority

Gallup's May 2026 Values and Beliefs poll reports that 56% rate U.S. moral
values as poor and 80% say values are getting worse. At the same time, 69% say
government policies significantly affect moral values, while the public is
nearly divided over whether government should promote moral values: 45% say it
should and 50% say it should not.

**Why it matters:** people may share a sense that the country is declining
without agreeing about who caused it, who has authority to repair it, or what
repair should look like. Cultural decline is a shared diagnosis more often than
a shared political program.

**Do not overread:** these are cross-sectional judgments, not measures of
objective conduct or a single polarization mechanism. They do not establish
that a particular institutional encounter caused a belief, action, conflict,
or later change in legitimacy.

Read the [moral pessimism and government-role finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-047.md)
and [cultural-meaning synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/cultural-meaning-contested-legitimacy-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 90. Local goodwill can be larger than confidence in reciprocal support

In Pew's March 2025 American Trends Panel, 76% said they were extremely or
very likely to bring in a neighbor's mail or water plants, while 52% thought
neighbors would do the same for them. Sixty-seven percent would bring a meal
to a sick neighbor or friend, compared with 42% who expected that help. The
gap was smaller for lending emergency money: 50% would do it and 45% expected
it.

**Why it matters:** social resilience is not just whether people are kind. It
also depends on whether people believe help will be available, reciprocal, and
usable during a real shock. A community can contain substantial goodwill while
people still feel individually under-protected.

**Do not overread:** these are hypothetical willingness and expectation
questions, not records of an emergency, delivered aid, helper burden, or
recovery. The comparison does not prove that neighbors fail one another.

Read the [mutual-aid and reciprocal-support finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-044.md)
and [formal/informal support synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/formal-informal-support-capacity-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 91. A formal remedy may need a second route before it becomes usable

The CFPB Ombudsman's FY2024 report describes people contacting the office
because a redress check was not received, was not cashed, or had to be
reissued to a next of kin after the recipient died. It also records questions
about eligibility, payment legitimacy, and how to connect with third-party
administrators.

**Why it matters:** a legal entitlement or mailed payment is not automatically
a usable remedy. The affected person may still need to find the right office,
prove eligibility, verify the payment, correct an address, or obtain a reissue.
Administrative reachability is part of distribution, not a back-office detail.

**Do not overread:** these are examples of inquiry types, not a payment-
completion rate. Ombudsman contact does not prove successful reissue, cashing,
account correction, adequate recovery, or restored trust.

Read the [redress-reachability finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/cfpb-ombudsman-redress-reachability-finding-2026-09-19.md)
and [remedy implementation/recovery synthesis](US-REMEDY-IMPLEMENTATION-RECOVERY-SYNTHESIS_V1.md).

## 92. A local care shortage label does not tell us whether care is reachable

The local-place evidence combines county health estimates, HRSA shortage-area
designations, and operational fields for designated providers. Those layers
show different things: a county-wide modeled measure, a designated population
or geography, and sometimes a reported staffing field. They do not show that a
particular resident can obtain an appointment.

**Why it matters:** a provider count or shortage score is not the same as active
hours, new-patient availability, insurance acceptance, price, travel, language
access, wait time, or completed use. A place can look adequately supplied in an
administrative file while a resident still faces delay, substitution, or
foregone care.

**Do not overread:** county and designation units are not resident outcomes,
and missing staffing fields are not proof of zero capacity. The evidence does
not yet show a downstream health, work, trust, or political effect for the same
person.

Read the [local care-capacity finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-012.md)
and [place-capacity/access synthesis](projects/us-local-business-place/place-capacity-access-meaning-synthesis-v1.md).

## 93. Marketplace visibility can become a seller cost before consumer harm is measured

The FTC and 22 state attorneys general sued Amazon in August 2026, alleging
that Sponsored Products auctions used undisclosed surcharges. The complaint
describes more than one million brands and sellers, including more than
500,000 small and medium-sized businesses, as within the alleged scope. It
also alleges that advertisers paid their own winning bid close to 80% of the
time by 2024 and that higher advertising costs were largely passed to
consumers.

**Why it matters:** a platform can make visibility a cost-bearing dependency
for sellers. A seller may absorb the cost, raise prices, reduce bids, change
assortment, use another channel, or continue because visibility is essential.
The seller-side cost, consumer price, household burden, and firm exit are
separate stages—not one automatic effect.

**Do not overread:** these are allegations in a pending enforcement case, not
an adjudicated finding or a representative seller/consumer estimate. The
public record does not provide seller-level auction logs, margins, product
prices, purchases, alternative-channel use, or household outcomes.

Read the [Amazon marketplace-power finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-013.md)
and [consumer participation/trust/power synthesis](projects/us-consumer-culture/consumer-participation-trust-power-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 94. Households absorb income and price pressure through several currencies

The Federal Reserve's 2025 SHED reports that 30% of adults had income that
varied at least occasionally, and 11% said that variability made it difficult
to pay bills. When prices or budgets became harder to manage, respondents
reported switching to cheaper products (62%), using less or stopping use (60%),
delaying a major purchase (46%), reducing saving (41%), borrowing more (16%),
or working more/getting another job (17%). Twenty-three percent reported
outside help with a listed expense.

**Why it matters:** financial pressure does not produce one consumer response.
Households can protect an immediate bill by sacrificing quality, savings, rest,
care, health, independence, or future flexibility. Outside help and extra work
may prevent an immediate failure while moving the cost somewhere else.

**Do not overread:** these are overlapping self-reported categories, not a
dated bill-level episode. The survey does not identify which adaptation protected
which expense, its time or debt cost, whether a remedy arrived, or whether
financial room later returned.

Read the [income-timing and price-adaptation ledger](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/data/fed-shed-income-timing-price-adaptation-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [financial-pressure adaptation synthesis](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/financial-pressure-adaptation-recovery-synthesis-v1.md).

## 95. AI capacity and dependence can rise together

The World Bank's 2026 development report describes productive AI capability as
an adopt–adapt–advance pathway requiring infrastructure, skills, institutions,
data, and local fit. It also identifies concentration across hardware,
infrastructure, models, and applications, with risks involving dependency,
capital concentration, surveillance, public budgets, and regulatory capacity.

**Why it matters:** access to an imported or privately controlled AI system can
create useful capability without creating strategic independence. A system
becomes power only when an organization or state can use it, inspect it,
replace it, refuse it, or impose a cost—and another actor actually responds.

**Do not overread:** this is a framework, not a measured U.S. effect estimate.
It does not provide a supplier concentration measure, outage event, switching
cost, observed state response, improved public service, or household outcome.

Read the [AI capability/dependence finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-111.md)
and [capacity/dependence synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/capacity-without-exit-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md).

## 96. A remedy can reach every named worker without proving durable recovery

In an official Fair Work Ombudsman follow-up, ten truck drivers who had been
underpaid were reported to have received back-payments ranging from $8,012 to
$91,566 after an appeal was dismissed and roughly $468,000 held in trust was
released. This is stronger than a promise, an order, or an aggregate program
allocation: the public record names a small worker group and reports receipt.

**Why it matters:** remedy evidence has levels. Here, the route reaches a
reported worker-level payment endpoint, but it still does not show whether the
amount was adequate against each worker's loss, whether household security was
restored, or whether trust, later work, health, recurrence, or exit changed.

**Do not overread:** receipt is institutionally reported and not independently
audited in this artifact. Ten named workers are not a population success rate,
and payment is not the same as durable recovery.

Read the [Atkins worker-receipt ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/fwo-atkins-truck-driver-backpay-receipt-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [remedy implementation/recovery synthesis](US-REMEDY-IMPLEMENTATION-RECOVERY-SYNTHESIS_V1.md).

## 97. Formal financial access can broaden while the provider environment contracts

The World Bank's 2024 Global Findex estimates that 97.0% of U.S. adults had
an account, while mobile-phone ownership was 98.0% and smartphone ownership
among phone owners was 92.6%. At the same time, IMF provider-side data show
commercial banks falling from 4,344 to 3,912 and commercial-bank branches
excluding headquarters falling from 75,500 to 68,436 between 2020 and 2024.

**Why it matters:** a formal account and a connected device are doorways, not
proof of usable financial power. Digital access may substitute for a branch for
some people, while others still need cash, a human identity check, an
accessible interface, or help disputing a failed payment. Provider counts,
account ownership, financial room, fraud recovery, and switching are separate
currencies.

**Do not overread:** the Findex and IMF figures use different units, years, and
methods. Branch contraction does not prove worse welfare, and high account
ownership does not prove liquidity, affordable credit, safe payment, effective
recourse, or practical ability to leave a provider.

Read the [financial-access doorway/provider finding](projects/us-financial-intermediation/findings/us-financial-intermediation-001.md)
and [financial-access route synthesis](projects/us-financial-intermediation/findings/us-financial-intermediation-002.md).

## 98. Cost-related care delay is accompanied by a wider household cascade

In the 2024 MEPS same-respondent architecture, people reporting cost-related
medical-care delay were more likely to report using a provider payment plan for
a hypothetical $500 bill (31.63% versus 20.70% among non-delayers) or being
unable to pay it at all (21.70% versus 7.86%). In the companion financial-room
screen, care delayers also reported more medical debt, missed loan or credit
payments, debt-collector contact, rent pressure, and utility difficulty.

The work/time currency moved too: among care delayers, 23.41% reported
sacrificing savings, 33.24% sacrificing basic spending, 37.38% working when
health needed time off, and 19.89% being unable to afford the income loss from
taking time off. The corresponding shares among non-delayers were 8.57%,
9.84%, 12.99%, and 5.27%.

**Why it matters:** a care problem can spill into payment strategy, debt,
housing, savings, work, and rest. The household may protect one immediate need
by moving the cost into another currency. This is the kind of cascade the broad
atlas is trying to see, not just a medical-use statistic.

**Do not overread:** the care-delay and adaptation fields are not a dated bill
episode and may refer to different needs or times within the survey year.
These are weighted same-respondent associations, not proof that delay caused
the sacrifices, debt, or work response. Alternatives, claim decisions, appeal,
care completion, remedy, and later recovery remain open.

Read the [MEPS care-delay and adaptation analysis](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/meps-2024-care-delay-adaptation-v1.md)
and [financial-room/care-delay analysis](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/meps-2024-financial-room-care-delay-v1.md).

## 99. Insurance friction can coexist with household sacrifice before any appeal outcome is visible

In the 2024 MEPS same-respondent screen, people reporting an insurance denial
or prior-authorization delay also reported more household sacrifice than those
reporting no denial or delay: 24.50% versus 7.63% sacrificed savings, 26.09%
versus 9.51% sacrificed basic spending, and 31.23% versus 12.56% worked when
health needed time off.

**Why it matters:** an institutional decision can reach beyond the clinic. It
may be experienced through savings, consumption, work, leave, family care, or
health—not only through whether an authorization was eventually reversed. The
household may carry the cost while the formal appeal route is still invisible.

**Do not overread:** the denial field does not identify a claim, decision date,
appeal, resolution, or specific care episode. These are same-respondent
associations from a broad survey round, not proof that denial caused the
sacrifices or that an appeal would have repaired them.

Read the [MEPS care-delay/adaptation analysis](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/meps-2024-care-delay-adaptation-v1.md)
and [Medicare Advantage denial-to-appeal finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-014.md).

## 100. People can value the smartphone and still fail to regain control of their time

In a 2026 Pew American Trends Panel survey, 53% of adults who answered the
smartphone-time question said they spend too much time on their phone. Forty-
five percent said they had tried to cut back during the previous year. Among
those who tried, 25% described the effort as extremely or very successful, 52%
as somewhat successful, and 23% as not very or not at all successful. At the
same time, 73% said the phone helps them stay informed and 51% said it helps
them feel connected.

**Why it matters:** this is not simply a story of adoption or harm. The same
device can be useful infrastructure and a contested claim on attention. A
person may want less use but still need the phone for information, connection,
work, school, or coordination. Intention, attempted change, partial success,
and durable control are separate stages of consumer agency.

**Do not overread:** these are self-reports from a cross-sectional survey, not
logged minutes, clinical sleep measures, productivity records, or a causal test
of platform design. The survey does not show which app, notification, social
obligation, or work requirement made cutting back difficult, nor whether a
successful reduction improved later well-being.

Read the [smartphone time, self-control, and well-being layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-smartphone-time-control-wellbeing-layer-v1.md)
and [digital control synthesis](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-006.md).

## 101. A transit route can exist while the trip remains practically unusable

The 2026 GAO review found some bus or rail service at nearly every airport it
examined, but public-transit use among passengers at 12 airports ranged from
only 4% to 19%. At two large airports, 17% and 19% of surveyed employees used
transit, while GAO estimated about 4% of airport and airline employees
nationwide commuted by transit. Cost, travel time, familiarity, accessibility,
parking, and shift timing all shaped the practical choice.

**Why it matters:** a line on a map is not the same as a usable mobility
option. A passenger may need a reliable transfer; a worker may start before
dawn; a rider using a mobility aid may need a working elevator. Infrastructure
becomes real consumer and labor capacity only when it fits time, price,
physical access, knowledge, and dependable trip completion.

**Do not overread:** the airports and measures use different designs, and the
4%–19% range is not a national average or causal estimate. GAO does not show
which intervention increased ridership, how many trips were abandoned, or
whether transit changed household cost, staffing, congestion, or emissions.

Read the [GAO airport transit availability and adoption layer](projects/us-transport-mobility/gao-2026-airport-transit-availability-adoption-layer-v1.md)
and the [capacity/dependence synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/capacity-without-exit-cross-domain-synthesis-v1.md).

## 102. The same country can look powerful at home and unreliable abroad

Pew's 2026 global survey found that 57% of Americans said the United States
contributes to global peace and stability, compared with a median of 35% across
36 surveyed non-U.S. countries. Inside the United States, 82% of Republicans
and 35% of Democrats gave the positive peace-and-stability answer. Yet 83% of
Americans across parties said the U.S. interferes in other countries' affairs
at least a fair amount.

**Why it matters:** geopolitical power has several audiences. Domestic party
identity, foreign reputation, perceived respect, alliance trust, and material
capability can move on different clocks. A country can retain military or
financial capacity while losing cooperation or legitimacy—or keep favorable
domestic support while foreign publics become less willing to trust it.

**Do not overread:** these are public judgments, not measures of actual peace,
intervention, rights performance, alliance behavior, trade, aid acceptance, or
military capability. The country median gives each surveyed country equal
weight, and the survey does not show whether a particular policy caused the
opinions or changed later behavior.

Read the [U.S. global-role and international-reception layer](projects/ai-work-control/pew-2026-us-global-role-domestic-partisan-international-comparison-layer-v1.md)
and [capability/legitimacy clock synthesis](projects/ai-work-control/capability-legitimacy-clock-synthesis-v1.md).

## 103. Broad approval of diversity can coexist with disagreement about institutional action

In Pew's 2025 American Trends Panel work, 75% of adults said racial and
ethnic diversity is good for the country, but only 64% said it is important for
companies to promote diversity. The partisan gap widened as the question moved
from broad meaning to institutional action: 86% of Democrats and 66% of
Republicans viewed diversity as good for the country, while 87% of Democrats
and 40% of Republicans said company promotion is important. Only 42% overall
said diversity efforts make society fairer; 32% said they make society less
fair.

**Why it matters:** demographic change is not the same as cultural consent,
and approval of a diverse society is not the same as legitimacy for a specific
workplace practice. The largest disagreement appears around what firms should
do and what fairness means—not simply whether diversity exists.

**Do not overread:** these are cross-sectional judgments, not observed hiring,
pay, promotion, treatment, customer experience, or later political behavior.
They do not show whether a particular diversity program improved opportunity
or caused the fairness perceptions. Race, party, age, nativity, region, and
workplace exposure may shape the answers in different ways.

Read the [racial diversity, cultural meaning, and workplace legitimacy layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-racial-diversity-cultural-workplace-meaning-layer-v1.md)
and [cultural-meaning synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/cultural-meaning-contested-legitimacy-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 104. Identity can be a source of belonging and a reported barrier at the same time

In Pew's 2025 National Survey of Latinos, 61% of Hispanic adults said being
Hispanic is extremely or very important to how they think about themselves.
But 33% said being Hispanic hurts their ability to get ahead, compared with
26% who said it helps and 40% who said it makes no difference. Thirty-four
percent reported discrimination or unfair treatment because of their Hispanic
background in the prior year, while about six in ten said what happens to
Hispanics in the United States affects their own lives a great deal or a fair
amount.

**Why it matters:** belonging and exposure are not opposites. An identity can
provide connection, family meaning, and collective concern while also changing
how a person interprets work, public treatment, language, housing, services, or
politics. “Integration” is therefore not one scale from separation to
assimilation.

**Do not overread:** these are self-reports from a cross-sectional survey, not
measured discrimination, income mobility, political action, consumer behavior,
or a causal effect of identity. The reported differences by party, vote group,
generation, and nativity may also reflect place, age, income, reporting norms,
or other conditions.

Read the [Hispanic identity, advantage, and barrier layer](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/pew-2026-hispanic-identity-advantage-barrier-layer-v1.md)
and [migration/place/cultural-conversion synthesis](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-place-cultural-conversion-synthesis-v1.md).

## 105. Civic participation is not a ladder from trust to action

Pew's 2025 public-life study grouped people across 19 forms of political,
community, religious, and news participation. The most active group—called
Mobilizers—had the highest political interest, with 58% extremely or very
interested in politics, but only 22% said they trust the federal government
always or most of the time. Connectors reported higher trust in national and
local news than Mobilizers while being less politically interested. Outsiders
had the lowest engagement and lower news trust, but the survey does not show
why they were less involved.

**Why it matters:** attention, community participation, political action,
trust in information, and trust in government are different currencies. Low
institutional trust can coexist with organized action; low participation can
reflect barriers, information conditions, time, access, or choice rather than
one settled attitude toward public life.

**Do not overread:** these are cross-sectional analytical clusters, not social
identities, treatments, or a causal participation sequence. The study does not
show what caused cluster membership, whether action changed policy, or how
material conditions made participation possible. Many questions cover the
previous year, including the 2024 election period.

Read the [public-life engagement groups and action layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-public-life-engagement-groups-action-layer-v1.md)
and [cultural-meaning synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/cultural-meaning-contested-legitimacy-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 106. People can value press freedom while supporting limits on the press

Pew's 2026 press-freedom survey found that 73% of adults consider freedom of
the press extremely or very important to society's well-being. But 53% said
preventing inaccurate reporting should take priority even if it limits press
freedom, while 45% prioritized freedom even if some reporting is inaccurate.
Only 28% said the media is completely free to report the news. Republicans
were more likely to say the media is completely free and less likely to be
highly concerned about restrictions than Democrats.

**Why it matters:** public legitimacy is not one “trust in media” number.
People are balancing freedom, accuracy, security, authority, and perceived
political power. A shared principle can coexist with disagreement about the
acceptable risk, who should intervene, and whether the government or news
organizations hold too much power.

**Do not overread:** these are normative perceptions, not audits of accuracy,
censorship, ownership, platform moderation, or actual press independence. The
forced-choice question does not identify what respondents call inaccurate or
who they want to decide. The partisan reversal may reflect the political
context of the survey rather than a stable ideology-only effect.

Read the [press-freedom limits and public-information norms layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-press-freedom-limits-norms-layer-v1.md)
and [press-government relationship and media-legitimacy layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-press-government-media-legitimacy-layer-v1.md).

## 107. Family care can preserve continuity while shifting public costs into the household

GAO reports that about 2.4 million U.S. children lived in kinship families in
2023. These households were more likely than all families to live in poverty;
grandparents commonly headed them and were more likely to have a disability or
be out of the labor force. For every child in foster care, about 18 kinship
children were not in foster care, even though the formal status can change
which financial and service supports are available.

**Why it matters:** family is an operating care institution. A relative may
preserve a child's school, culture, and relationships while absorbing housing,
childcare, health, legal, transportation, and emotional work. A public story
about family “stepping up” can hide who leaves work, draws down savings, or
cannot obtain respite and formal help.

**Do not overread:** GAO does not measure caregiver hours, out-of-pocket cost,
placement stability, exhaustion, reunification, or whether foster-care status
improves outcomes. The national descriptive data and interviews from selected
communities answer different questions. The 18-to-1 comparison is a population
ratio, not proof that non-foster families need the same intervention.

Read the [kinship families and intergenerational care-support layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-kinship-families-intergenerational-care-support-layer-v1.md)
and [formal/informal support synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/formal-informal-support-capacity-cross-source-synthesis-v1.md).

## 108. Long-term-care coverage can grow while the care bundle remains financially fragmented

GAO identified at least $12 billion in Medicaid and traditional Medicare
spending for services provided in assisted-living facilities in 2024. Medicaid
programs in 44 states covered some assisted-living services as of March 2025,
and 29 of those states used home- and community-based-services waivers. But
Medicare, Medicaid, households, HUD, VA programs, and family members may pay
different parts of the bundle: daily assistance, health services, room and
board, transportation, coordination, and informal care.

**Why it matters:** a covered service is not the same as an affordable,
available place to live. A person can be eligible for one part of care while
still facing a waitlist, limited providers, uncovered room and board, family
coordination, or no workable alternative. Assisted living is simultaneously a
health, housing, consumer, family-work, and public-finance problem.

**Do not overread:** the $12 billion is a minimum identified total, not all
spending or resident welfare. State coverage does not prove a slot, admission,
provider participation, quality, or continuity. GAO does not measure total
out-of-pocket cost, unmet need, wait time, staffing, safety, or later setting
choice.

Read the [assisted-living spending, Medicaid coverage, and care-finance layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-assisted-living-spending-medicaid-coverage-layer-v1.md)
and [care-supply/work option-stack synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/care-supply-work-option-stack-synthesis-v1.md).

## 109. A public workforce route can exist while disability-related work outcomes remain weaker

In GAO's review of selected DOL workforce programs, participants reporting a
disability had lower post-exit employment than participants who did not report
one. In the Adult program, second-quarter employment was 60% versus 76%, and
median second-quarter earnings among those employed were $7,048 versus $8,872.
Only 33% of Adult participants reporting disabilities used training services,
compared with 47% of those not reporting a disability. GAO also found local
problems with interpreters, outside training accessibility, guidance use, and
aggregate monitoring.

**Why it matters:** legal eligibility, assistive technology, participation,
training, placement, earnings, and retention are different stages. A public
route can be formally open while the middle—disclosure, accommodation,
accessible training, completion, and placement—remains uneven. Missing
disability-type information can also reflect privacy or fear, not lack of need.

**Do not overread:** this is a selected-program comparison, not a causal
estimate that access barriers produced the employment or earnings gaps. GAO's
12 job-center visits were not nationally representative, 43% of participants
reporting a disability did not report a type, and earnings are not hourly
wages. The study does not identify accommodation receipt, abandonment,
retention, or later mobility for the same person.

Read the [disability access to workforce programs layer](projects/us-safety-net-access/gao-2026-disability-workforce-program-access-layer-v1.md)
and [public routes to work and security synthesis](projects/us-safety-net-access/accessible-work-security-optionality-synthesis-v1.md).

## 110. Working and receiving public protection can be part of the same labor system

GAO estimates that about 13.8 million Medicaid-enrolled adults and 10.6
million adults living in SNAP households worked at some point in 2024. About
88% of the working adults in each group were in the private sector, and the
groups were less likely than comparison wage earners to work full-time hours
for at least 50 weeks. In selected data from 11 states, 46 companies appeared
among the top employers of Medicaid enrollees or SNAP beneficiaries in at least
two states.

**Why it matters:** employment is not the same as material independence.
Public health and food protection can help households remain attached to work,
while wages, schedules, employer benefits, household needs, and eligibility
rules determine whether that attachment becomes security. Employers, families,
and public programs are operating in one labor system.

**Do not overread:** Medicaid enrollment is individual while SNAP is measured
at the household level. “Worked at some point” does not show adequate wages,
predictable schedules, or continuous benefits. The employer data are selected
state administrative data; top-employer overlap does not prove employer intent,
underpayment, or cost shifting.

Read the [working adults, Medicaid/SNAP, and employer-reliance layer](projects/us-safety-net-access/gao-2026-working-adults-medicaid-snap-employer-reliance-layer-v1.md)
and [bounded work/safety-net finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-014.md).

## 111. Disaster aid enters an information environment where urgency can make help harder to verify

GAO reports that disaster-assistance scams target survivors and helpers, while
the true prevalence and dollar loss remain unknown. Survivors may need to
share identity or property information, pay for urgent repairs, find temporary
housing, or respond quickly to an agency-like message. Shame, embarrassment,
limited awareness, and the belief that reporting will not help can suppress
complaints. Agencies use information booths, press releases, and emergency-app
notifications, but those channels do not prove that a warning was received,
understood, or acted upon.

**Why it matters:** public assistance does not arrive in an empty information
space. A person can face both a material disaster and a verification problem.
The practical route includes recognizing the legitimate actor, protecting
privacy, deciding whether to pause and check, reporting suspicious contact, and
still reaching real aid. More warnings do not automatically mean safer access.

**Do not overread:** GAO does not provide a representative rate of exposure,
successful victimization, loss, identity compromise, reporting, or recovery.
It does not show that education reduces losses or that scam exposure causes
distrust or avoidance of legitimate assistance. Differences by age, language,
disability, rurality, internet access, or housing status remain open.

Read the [disaster-assistance scams, information, and trust layer](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/gao-2026-disaster-assistance-scams-information-trust-layer-v1.md)
and [FEMA assistance-delivery and capacity layer](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/gao-2026-fema-disaster-assistance-delivery-capacity-layer-v1.md).

## 112. Disaster credit can keep a firm alive while increasing private debt

In the studied SBA approval-threshold design, disaster credit was associated
with a 13.2-percentage-point reduction in firm exit, a 0.170-log-point
increase in full-sample employment, and about $17,712 more non-SBA private
debt. The firm-level result shows a real survival and employment effect in
that design, but it does not show worker pay, job quality, owner security,
customer affordability, or whether the added borrowing improved or prolonged
the business's vulnerability.

**Why it matters:** public recovery policy can move several currencies at once.
A loan may preserve a local employer and nearby consumer option while creating
repayment pressure or shifting risk into private credit. Firm survival is not
the same as household recovery, and employment is not automatically a good
job.

**Do not overread:** these estimates come from one studied approval-threshold
design and are firm-level outcomes, not a universal disaster-loan effect. The
record does not establish worker incidence, customer welfare, owner
consumption, loan receipt outside the design, or durable security.

Read the [disaster recovery policy end-to-end synthesis](projects/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity/findings/us-disaster-recovery-policy-end-to-end-synthesis-001.md)
and [disaster-liquidity finding](projects/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity/findings/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity-001.md).

## 113. Expanding a small-business support boundary can shift opportunity toward larger firms

An NBER study using quasi-random timing in U.S. small-business program
eligibility found that larger firms gained access while the smallest firms,
especially younger, more productive, and financially constrained firms,
experienced lower revenue. Government contracts shifted toward larger firms;
exits increased, wages declined, and patenting fell. An earlier version of the
research reported that the smallest firms became about 0.5 percentage points
more likely to exit.

**Why it matters:** “more eligible firms” does not necessarily mean more
opportunity for the smallest firms. A rule can widen formal access while
redistributing contracts, revenue, survival, wages, and innovation toward
scale. Policy boundaries are part of firm power and local economic culture, not
just technical program design.

**Do not overread:** this is firm- and program-level causal evidence, not a
customer-welfare, owner-household, or universal small-business estimate. It
does not show whether larger-firm gains improved prices or service, whether
every worker lost security, or how local consumers and communities responded.
The current and earlier paper versions have distinct estimates and are kept
separate.

Read the [small-business support size-rule finding](projects/us-small-business-support-size-cutoff/findings/us-small-business-support-size-rule-001.md)
and [disaster-credit finding](projects/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity/findings/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity-001.md).

## 114. A small firm can report profit while losing revenue room and local security

The April 2026 New York Fed small-business release shows that firm health does
not move on one line. Reported profitability improved from the pandemic-period
low, but more firms reported lower than higher revenue, the share expecting
higher revenue fell 6 percentage points year over year, and the smallest firms
had weaker pricing power and more reports of weak sales. Employment growth
increased for small firms while larger-firm growth decreased but remained
positive. Reported debt per employee was about $70,000.

**Why it matters:** profit, revenue, pricing power, employment, debt, worker
room, customer prices, and local service continuity are separate currencies.
A firm can remain profitable by cutting costs or absorbing pressure while
owners, workers, or customers experience less security. “Small business is
doing well” is too broad to explain local economic life.

**Do not overread:** the survey is weighted but non-random, covers employer
firms with fewer than 500 employees, and uses different question universes and
reference periods. The profit measure is a diffusion index, not a margin;
employment growth is not job quality; debt per employee is not affordability;
and the release does not measure customer welfare or household outcomes.

Read the [small-business capacity layer](projects/us-local-business-place/nyfed-small-business-capacity-layer-v1.md)
and [firm/local-business finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-001.md).

## 115. Domestic production does not automatically create strategic independence

USGS reports 100% net import reliance for selected critical materials such as
arsenic, gallium, graphite, indium, and manganese, while the United States
produced about $112 billion in minerals in 2025. GAO finds that battery
recycling could reduce import reliance for some minerals in roughly two to
three years, but semiconductor substitution and recycling may not reduce
short-term dependence because purity and performance requirements are high.
Recycling also needs collection, processing, feedstock, yield, and a buyer for
the recovered material.

**Why it matters:** a mine, factory, recycling plan, or domestic production
number is only one stage of strategic capacity. The real question is whether a
country can obtain the required material at the needed purity, price, volume,
and speed—and switch suppliers or processes under pressure. Domestic output
can rise while dependence remains in refining, technology, ownership, or
upstream feedstock.

**Do not overread:** net import reliance is not a complete resilience score,
and the two-to-three-year recycling horizon is a technical possibility, not
realized capacity or reduced imports. The evidence does not show a specific
shortage, factory shutdown, consumer loss, defense delay, or geopolitical
concession caused by an export control.

Read the [critical-minerals substitution, recycling, and dependence layer](projects/ai-work-control/gao-2026-critical-minerals-substitution-recycling-dependence-layer-v1.md)
and [domestic capacity/dependence state-leverage bridge](projects/ai-work-control/domestic-capacity-dependence-state-leverage-cross-source-bridge-v1.md).

## 116. Disaster investment can produce visible projects without proving economic recovery

GAO reports that the Economic Development Administration awarded about $2.1
billion in disaster-recovery grants from fiscal years 2014 through 2024 and
coordinated 45 federal Economic Recovery Support Function missions from 2013
through 2025. Nearly 45% of award dollars went to utilities, roads, and other
public infrastructure, while another 36% funded business, medical, training,
and other facilities. But GAO found that EDA's measures could not establish
whether intended economic outcomes occurred. Among 292 construction and
revolving-loan grants with available data, 55% of grantees reported no jobs
created or retained and no private investment after three years; GAO found
those reports insufficiently reliable for estimating economic effects.

**Why it matters:** a community can receive an appropriation, a federal
mission, a completed-looking asset, or a grant report while firms, workers,
customers, and households still lack functioning services, jobs, housing,
credit, or a safe return. Public spending is an input and project category,
not the same thing as local recovery. This adds a community/state-capacity
stage to the disaster path between money being awarded and everyday economic
life working again.

**Do not overread:** the 55% figure is not a failure rate for all EDA grants
or all disaster projects. It is a bounded, grantee-reported result for 292
grants with available data, and the report does not provide a complete
place-level counterfactual linking awards to employment, business survival,
wages, household security, migration, or trust.

Read the [EDA disaster-economic recovery outcomes layer](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/gao-2026-eda-disaster-economic-recovery-outcomes-layer-v1.md)
and [structured EDA recovery ledger](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/data/gao-eda-disaster-economic-recovery-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 117. One national economy can contain several different lived economies

The New York Fed's December 2025 Economic Heterogeneity Indicators estimate
different price and earnings conditions across groups. The constructed
inflation rate was 0.57 percentage points above the national average in the
Northeast and 0.51 points below it in the South; it was also lower for
Hispanic households and households headed by someone under 25 in that release.
Earnings ratios were 78.7% for Black workers relative to white workers, 77.5%
for Hispanic workers relative to white workers, 57.4% for workers without a
college degree relative to workers with a degree, and 86.7% for rural workers
relative to urban workers. Since 2023, real consumption rose for high-income
households, was flat for middle-income households, and fell for low-income
households through November 2025.

**Why it matters:** a national inflation rate, wage statistic, or consumption
number can hide the actual menu of choices people face. Different baskets,
earnings positions, wealth, and local prices change whether a household can
absorb a rise, substitute, delay, borrow, or stop buying. This is a foundation
for understanding why the same economic message can produce different trust,
blame, adaptation, or political responses.

**Do not overread:** these are constructed group-level indicators, not official
demographic price indexes or a same-household causal estimate. The earnings
ratios describe employed workers and do not by themselves measure hours,
occupations, bargaining power, or access to work. The consumption series does
not show why a particular family changed spending or whether it carried debt,
went without, or found a substitute.

Read the [New York Fed economic heterogeneity layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/new-york-fed-economic-heterogeneity-layer-v1.md).

## 118. AI can show up in productivity accounts before people share the gain

BEA says the United States does not yet have a direct AI line in its national
accounts. Its early industry estimates are consistent with productivity and
input-saving effects, but they are not observed job or wage effects. A BEA
working paper linked 75,292 worker observations to state-industry data and
found that a one-percentage-point higher share of frequent AI users was
associated with roughly 0.4% to 0.6% higher employment and 0.1% to 0.2% higher
real output after 2020. Another analysis of 1,586 industry-year observations
reported changes in labor and capital shares. These results are descriptive,
and the employer-reported specification did not reproduce the same output
pattern.

**Why it matters:** AI can be measured as use, output, cost change, or labor
composition before we know whether workers receive higher pay or control,
firms lower prices, or households gain purchasing power. Productivity is a
middle-stage signal, not proof of shared prosperity. The social question is
