who captures the time, savings, ownership, and bargaining room created by the
technology.

**Do not overread:** the evidence is early and not a causal estimate of AI's
effect on employment, wages, prices, or households. Retrospective use measures,
local demand, management, intangible capital, timing, imputation, and other
factors may shape the associations. Lower industry price growth does not prove
that consumers paid less or that savings were shared.

Read the [BEA AI economic-accounts, utilization, and cost layer](projects/ai-work-control/bea-ai-economic-accounts-utilization-costs-layer-v1.md).

## 119. “Working class” is a cultural identity, not a clean income category

Pew reports that 60% of U.S. adults described themselves as working class in
2026, up from 54% in 2024. The label crossed conventional economic and work
boundaries: 50% of upper-income adults, 50% of college graduates, and 61% of
workers outside blue-collar occupations used it. It was more common among
people without a college degree (66%), blue-collar workers (77%), and
Republicans or Republican leaners (67%) than Democrats or Democratic leaners
(55%), but the identity was not reducible to any one of those categories.

**Why it matters:** income, financial stress, occupation, status, family
background, dignity, cultural solidarity, and political belonging can all be
compressed into the same phrase. “Working class” may describe how someone
understands their place in society rather than their measurable resources. That
helps explain why economic pressure does not translate into one predictable
political interpretation or consumer response.

**Do not overread:** this is self-description, not an official class measure.
The survey does not show whether the identity reflects hardship, pride, family
history, resentment, work ethic, or political signaling, and it does not prove
that the label causes voting, organizing, policy preferences, or switching.

Read the [Pew working-class identity and status layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-working-class-identity-status-meaning-layer-v1.md).

## 120. Two-party labels hide several different political coalitions

Pew's 2026 political typology groups 10,357 adults into nine clusters using 30
questions about government, economics, immigration, national identity, social
values, and foreign policy. The four strongly ideological partisan groups make
up a minority of adults: the right anchors are 21% and the left anchors 17%.
Meanwhile, the Unconventional Right is 23% and the Order and Opportunity Left
is 18%; each leans toward one party while differing from that party on issues
such as abortion, crime, immigration, or the safety net. The Tuned-Out Middle
is 9% and has exceptionally low political interest, while the Left-Out Left
combines financial stress with feeling politically ignored.

**Why it matters:** “Republican,” “Democrat,” “moderate,” or “nonvoter” is not
enough to explain how people interpret prices, migration, technology, public
services, religion, alliances, or government power. A person can share a
party label while disagreeing about the institution, value, or policy route.
Political intensity also matters: the loudest groups may not represent the
median household's material experience.

**Do not overread:** the typology summarizes patterned attitudes; it is not a
fixed personality taxonomy, a causal explanation, or a prediction of future
votes. Group membership does not prove turnout, switching, organizing,
consumer behavior, or policy action.

Read the [Pew political typology, values, and coalition layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/pew-2026-political-typology-values-coalitions-layer-v1.md).

## 121. Semiconductor funding can advance while strategic independence remains unsettled

GAO reports that Commerce oversees about $50 billion in semiconductor programs:
$39 billion for facilities and equipment incentives and $11 billion for
advanced microelectronics R&D. By July 2026, 49 projects across 24 companies
had been awarded support, and by April 2026 about $13.1 billion had been
disbursed. But 125 milestones were still in progress, nine were past their
anticipated dates, and GAO found that Commerce lacked a sufficiently detailed
plan and timeline for fully meeting statutory requirements for key R&D
institutions. Commerce had also canceled awards representing about $7.8
billion of the $11 billion R&D appropriation while programs were being revised.

**Why it matters:** industrial capacity has several clocks. Money can be
awarded and disbursed while facilities are still qualifying equipment, workers
are still being trained, governance vehicles are being redesigned, and
qualified chips or materials are not yet reaching customers. Domestic funding
is therefore not the same as operating output, resilient supply, bargaining
room, or reduced dependence on foreign suppliers.

**Do not overread:** these figures do not prove that the program failed or that
canceled awards were wasted. Disbursement and milestone status are not
production, yield, workforce retention, customer delivery, or import
substitution. The report does not provide a complete firm counterfactual or a
measured change in geopolitical leverage.

Read the [GAO CHIPS R&D capacity, governance, and dependence layer](projects/ai-work-control/gao-2026-chips-rd-capacity-governance-dependence-layer-v1.md)
and [strategic capability realization bridge](projects/ai-work-control/strategic-capability-realization-cross-case-bridge-v1.md).

## 122. Leaving a benefit program does not automatically mean becoming secure

In a weighted SIPP person-month screen, 76.20% of observed SNAP exit pairs
had a lower next-month income-to-poverty ratio. SNAP entry and exit groups
could also show rent or mortgage and utility hardship, while the recorded
reason was available for only part of the transitions. The same person is
therefore observed leaving the program and still moving into a weaker measured
resource position in the next month.

**Why it matters:** program participation, work, income, food security, bills,
and recovery run on different clocks. Exit may reflect improved income, a
recertification or time limit, non-collection, administrative friction, or
worsening need—not one clear story of independence. Continued receipt is also
not complete security when bills and other needs remain exposed.

**Do not overread:** this is a descriptive adjacent-month comparison, not a
causal estimate of SNAP's effect. The record does not identify the notice,
benefit amount, household decision sequence, alternatives, appeal, food
quantity, debt, or later recovery. About half of the transitions lack a
classified reason, and person-month records are not a full administrative
episode.

Read the [SNAP transition/material-security ledger](projects/us-safety-net-access/data/sipp-snap-transition-material-security-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [SNAP transition finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-007.md).

## 123. A work requirement can increase benefit exits without increasing work

Two linked-administrative-data studies in the SNAP policy scan report this
policy counterexample. In one studied state and rule setting, work
requirements increased exits by 23 percentage points after 18 months, reduced
overall participation among subject adults by 53%, and showed no employment
effect; homeless adults were disproportionately screened out. A separate study
of parents found that general work requirements made people much less likely to
receive SNAP without increasing work. The authors identify administrative
burden as an important mechanism in those settings.

A newer 2026 Census CES working paper using linked SNAP and employment data
from five states and a triple-differences design reports about a 7% reduction
in SNAP participation, no increase in labor supply, and disproportionate
screening of lower-income people. It broadens the design-specific evidence but
does not turn the result into a national estimate.

**Why it matters:** a policy can be described as labor activation while its
most visible result is loss of benefit access. Eligibility, reporting,
exemption paperwork, route completion, employment, food security, and durable
independence are separate stages. This helps explain why a lower caseload is
not automatically evidence of better work or household security.

**Do not overread:** these are design-specific studies of particular states,
rules, populations, and periods. The newer five-state result is a working-paper
estimate, not a universal national effect. None of the studies shows what
happened later to food, debt, health, housing, trust, or political action for
each person.

Read the [SNAP administrative-burden and practical-access scan](projects/us-safety-net-access/snap-administrative-burden-paper-scan-v1.md),
[Census CES working paper](https://www.census.gov/library/working-papers/2026/adrm/CES-WP-26-46.html),
and [canonical administrative-access record](records/us-snap-administrative-access-2026.json).

## 124. Child-care incentives can exist while families still lack a dependable slot

GAO reports that the Defense Department employed about 19,000 nonappropriated-
fund child-care workers serving nearly 172,000 children in FY2024. Recruiting
and retention problems contributed to lengthy waitlists and waits. The Air
Force, Army, and Marine Corps provided 4,955 recruitment bonuses or retention
allowances in 2024, and some services offered fee discounts, including a full
discount for a first child at a DOD center. But incentives are not hires,
retained workers, staffed slots, shorter waits, or reliable hours.

**Why it matters:** child care is both a household service and a labor system.
A subsidy can lower the listed price while a staffing shortage still blocks
access. A worker allowance can preserve a classroom without solving a family's
schedule, commute, quality, or waitlist problem. The real social outcome is a
dependable staffed slot that protects work, family time, readiness, and
household security.

**Do not overread:** this is a DOD institutional case, not a national
child-care estimate. GAO does not identify the causal effect of any incentive
on turnover, waitlists, quality, parent work, deployment, or family security.
The services' data are not fully comparable, and Navy incentives could not be
separated reliably.

Read the [GAO military child-care workforce and capacity layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-military-childcare-workforce-capacity-layer-v1.md).

## 125. Unpaid eldercare can function like a hidden second job

BLS estimates that 38.2 million people age 15 and older provided unpaid
eldercare in 2023–2024, including 23.6 million employed people and 18.4
million full-time workers. Among providers, eldercare averaged 1.07 hours per
day across all provider-days but 3.88 hours on days when care occurred. The
provider rate was highest among adults ages 55–64 at 24.1%, so this is not only
a retired-person issue.

**Why it matters:** care is social infrastructure, but its cost may appear as
travel, coordination, household work, less rest, schedule strain, or reduced
future flexibility rather than a care bill. A late-career worker may be
simultaneously supporting a parent or partner, protecting a job, and preparing
for retirement. Care also travels through wider kin networks, not just the
recipient's household.

**Do not overread:** ATUS establishes population scale, time use, and work
overlap; it does not prove that caregiving caused lost earnings, worse health,
less sleep, job loss, recipient harm, or political action. Care-day averages
and all-provider averages answer different questions, and no same-caregiver/
recipient longitudinal outcome is observed.

Read the [hidden second job eldercare synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/aging-care-hidden-second-job-synthesis-v1.md)
and [distributed eldercare time-network synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/atus-eldercare-network-time-capacity-synthesis-v1.md).

## 126. Caregiving and paid work can move on different gendered timelines

A linked SIPP–Social Security study follows reported family-caregiving onset
against long-run employment and earnings records. Relative to matched
comparisons, women's employment and earnings fell after caregiving began; the
NBER summary reports about a 2.7-percentage-point employment decline and
nearly $1,000 lower annual earnings in the first three years. Men's labor
supply and earnings were already declining several years before reported care
onset and fell further afterward. Women's employment gaps narrowed after
several years, while men's persistence looked different.

**Why it matters:** unpaid care does not produce one universal work story.
Care can transfer long-term-care needs into paid work, earnings, family
resources, and future security, but the timing and mechanism differ by gender
and prior labor trajectory. A caregiver's work change may be a sacrifice, a
buffered choice, a response to earlier disruption, or some combination.

**Do not overread:** this is a study-specific linked-record estimate, not a
universal causal caregiving effect. The published result does not observe the
recipient's need, care hours, paid alternatives, employer response, health,
wealth, household buffering, care quality, trust, political action, or exit.

Read the [linked caregiving/labor-supply ledger](projects/us-aging-care-strain/data/nber-caregiving-labor-supply-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [aging-care capacity bridge](projects/us-aging-care-strain/aging-care-system-capacity-bridge-v1.md).

## 127. Turning family care into paid work can leave unpaid care and retention risk

In the Cash and Counseling follow-up, directly hired workers were generally
satisfied with working conditions, supervision, and compensation; more than
40% were very satisfied with wages and fringe benefits across the studied
states. But many continued substantial unpaid care, often had less formal
training or benefits than agency workers, and faced uncertainty about longer-
term wellbeing and willingness to continue. The direct-hire route could convert
part of an existing family or friend care relationship into paid work without
turning the whole care arrangement into stable employment.

**Why it matters:** worker satisfaction, wage adequacy, benefits, paid hours,
unpaid carryover, training, backup care, and retention are different currencies.
Consumer direction can give a beneficiary and caregiver more control while
moving payroll, supervision, continuity, and replacement risk into the
beneficiary-worker relationship. Flexible paid care is not automatically a
durable care workforce.

**Do not overread:** this is a study-specific follow-up, not a universal wage
or worker-quality estimate. Worker and beneficiary records are not linked in
the compact public evidence, individual payment timing is not verified, and
the study does not establish long-run retention, care quality, household
security, trust, or exit.

Read the [Cash and Counseling paid-worker ledger](projects/us-aging-care-strain/data/aspe-cash-counseling-worker-experience-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [multi-currency care synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/cash-counseling-multi-currency-realization-synthesis-v1.md).

## 128. A small access redesign can change benefit participation before household recovery is known

In a field experiment involving about 65,000 Los Angeles SNAP applicants,
allowing applicants to request flexible interviews increased approvals by about
6 percentage points, doubled early approvals, and increased longer-term
participation by more than 2 percentage points. A separate administrative study
found that closing an enrollment office reduced tract SNAP participation by
about 7% to 9% over two years. These results show that interviews, offices,
travel, timing, and route design can change whether people reach a benefit.

**Why it matters:** access is not just a rule about eligibility. A modest
change in who controls the interview or whether an office is reachable can
change approval and continued participation. Administrative design therefore
redistributes time, paperwork, missed work, transport cost, and the ability to
persist before any food, housing, work, or political outcome is measured.

**Do not overread:** the estimates come from particular states, periods,
applicant groups, and policy designs. They do not show that every approved
person received adequate food support, that the route improved household
security, or that it changed trust, action, debt, health, or recovery.

Read the [SNAP administrative-burden and practical-access scan](projects/us-safety-net-access/snap-administrative-burden-paper-scan-v1.md).

## 129. A moderate national insurance average can hide severe local coverage pressure

GAO estimates that the average homeowners-insurance premium rose only 3% after
inflation from $2,743 in 2019 to $2,829 in 2024 dollars. But some southern
coastal areas rose at least 25% above inflation, and modeled premiums were
about 58% higher in high versus medium wind-risk areas and 8% higher in high
versus medium wildfire-risk areas. By August 2025, 35 states operated FAIR or
beach plans for homeowners unable to obtain private coverage; those plans
generally offered less coverage than private policies.

**Why it matters:** a national premium average can look manageable while a
particular place faces a very different cost, coverage, lender, and staying
decision. Public insurance backstops are not simply extra supply: they can
shift risk among households, insurers, states, and taxpayers while leaving
coverage limits and repair capacity unresolved.

**Do not overread:** the modeled premiums are not every household's bill, and
risk comparisons do not prove a specific disaster caused a premium change.
FAIR-plan presence is not enrollment or proof of private-market failure for
every household. The evidence does not yet join a property to a notice,
premium payment, claim, repair, move/stay choice, or later security.

Read the [GAO homeowners-insurance 2019–2024 layer](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/gao-homeowners-insurance-2019-2024-layer-v1.md).

## 130. A home can gain market value while its protection and recovery position worsen

The 2026Q2 FHFA purchase-only house-price index rose 2.13% nationally over one
year and 33.41% over five years, but one-year state movement ranged from
8.30% in Alaska to a 1.25% decline in New Mexico. California, Florida, and
Texas moved in different directions as well. These price changes do not show
whether an owner can afford insurance, produce a deductible, repair a damaged
home, refinance, sell, or move.

**Why it matters:** housing has separate clocks for asset value, protection
cost, and recovery capacity. An appreciating property may still be harder to
insure or repair; a flat or falling index may coexist with adequate coverage
and liquid resources. “Home values are up” is not the same as household
security or usable financial room.

**Do not overread:** FHFA is a repeat-sales index for covered single-family
properties, not a measure of every owner, renter, home, or transaction. It
does not establish that insurance pressure caused the state differences or
that price movement caused a move, default, repair delay, or political action.

Read the [FHFA house price, protection cost, and recovery capacity layer](projects/us-housing-insurance-affordability/fhfa-price-protection-recovery-layer-v1.md).

## 131. A changing place can have steady civic activity and uneven institutional trust

Across 620 counties in a 2024 contextual comparison, county population growth
and foreign-born share did not produce a simple trust gradient. Federal trust
was 27.846% in the lowest displayed joint cell, but 36.055% in a high-growth,
high-foreign-born-share cell and 39.542% in a low-growth, high-share cell.
Civic action varied less across the cells, from 34.629% to 42.015%.

**Why it matters:** a place can change demographically while people maintain
similar levels of civic participation and judge institutions differently.
Belonging, fairness, welcome, threat, language access, housing pressure, and
service capacity are not interchangeable. A resident may continue to act in
public life while feeling less safe using a particular public route.

The companion immigration-concern evidence makes that interface concrete:
nearly one in five families reported going without public benefits because of
immigration concerns, and 16% reported not applying for or stopping noncash
programs over green-card concerns. A nominally available benefit or clinic can
therefore coexist with protective non-use.

**Do not overread:** the county comparison is contextual and weighted; it does
not identify a respondent's migration history, direct service encounter, legal
status, language experience, or reason for local trust. Non-use is not
automatically distrust or ineligibility. The evidence does not show that
population change caused trust, civic action, or non-use.

Read the [place conditions and cultural response synthesis](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/migration-place-cultural-conversion-synthesis-v1.md).

## 132. Health-care coverage can exist while the encounter remains unusable

GAO's review of disability access identifies physical, sensory, communication,
cognitive, digital, and provider-interaction barriers that can keep a covered
person or an available provider from producing a completed, safe, continuous
care encounter. Examples include inaccessible exam equipment, check-in kiosks
and apps, sensory environments, communication needs, and provider assumptions
or denials.

**Why it matters:** a health-care system is experienced at the interface. The
person or family may pay with extra coordination, travel, waiting, disclosure,
missed work, or avoided care even when coverage, a provider, or an online route
exists. That makes accessibility part of distributional power, not a cosmetic
feature of service design.

**Do not overread:** GAO reviewed 12 HHS population-health surveys and found
that they did not collect national-level data on most accessibility barriers;
its survey of 1,194 adults with disabilities is nongeneralizable. Oversight,
recommendations, or amended requirements do not prove accommodation receipt,
completed care, correction, health, work, trust, or provider switching.

Read the [disability health-care accessibility ledger](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-016.md).

## 133. Financial pressure can create more searching without creating more choice

In a linked April-to-June 2025 HTOPS sample of 6,564 respondents, people who
said ordinary expenses were difficult searched more for jobs, benefits, credit,
food, housing, and medical help. Job search was 40.6% for the difficulty group
versus 21.8% for the comparison group; benefit search was 19.6% versus 11.1%,
and credit search was 20.3% versus 11.4%.

**Why it matters:** search is often treated as evidence of choice. Under
pressure it may instead be work needed to construct a possible choice. The
household can spend more time, attention, privacy, emotional energy, and
institutional effort navigating several systems at once, while the usable
result remains uncertain. This is a consumer and cultural experience before
it becomes a purchase, benefit receipt, job, or political opinion.

The pattern also varies by resources. Expense difficulty was associated with
more search within every displayed income band, while AI use did not simply
replace human navigation. In the pooled comparison, broad AI use was 53.9%
among the difficulty group and 58.5% among the comparison group; confidence in
Congress was nearly identical at 17.2% and 16.8%.

**Do not overread:** this is a descriptive same-respondent screen, not a
causal estimate. It does not show which result people found, how long or how
much the search cost, whether an application or transaction was completed,
whether a remedy arrived, or whether security, trust, switching, or exit
changed. Searching can reflect agency, desperation, preparation, or several at
once; not searching has multiple possible meanings too.

Read the [material-pressure and option-search synthesis](projects/us-consumer-culture/material-pressure-option-search-cultural-agency-synthesis-v1.md).

## 134. Material insecurity can reduce political action without producing one political meaning

In a retained 2010–2012–2014 CCES panel, people who reported earlier job loss
had lower later congressional contact than the comparison group, 37.36% versus
39.14%, and lower local political action, 23.93% versus 31.57%. People who
reported no health insurance also showed lower later contact, 31.75% versus
40.29%, and lower local action, 21.81% versus 32.48%.

But the approval pattern did not move in the same simple direction. Favorable
Congress approval was 13.86% after reported job loss versus 10.06% in the
comparison group, and 11.51% for the uninsured group versus 10.23% in its
comparison group.

**Why it matters:** hardship can reduce the capacity to contact, organize, or
participate without producing one predictable view of government. Time, money,
confidence, exhaustion, prior party identity, attribution, ideology, and the
particular institution being judged can shape different political currencies
in different ways. Lower action should not automatically be read as agreement,
apathy, or anti-government feeling.

**Do not overread:** this is a descriptive same-panel comparison, not a causal
transition estimate. Job loss covers a prior two-year period and does not show
the separation date, alternatives, remedy, or recovery; insurance status is not
a dated coverage-loss or medical-bill event. The panel does not close trust
change, institutional response, household recovery, switching, or exit.

Read the [material status and political action synthesis](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/material-status-political-action-divergence-synthesis-v1.md).

## 135. A named clinic and long opening hours still do not prove usable care

In Allen County, Ohio, HRSA records connect a primary-care shortage
designation to Health Partners of Western Ohio and, through the same
organization identifier, to the named North Lima Community Health Center. The
site is listed as active, permanent, and open 72 hours per week. Its public
page lists medical, oral-health, behavioral-health, pharmacy, social-service,
and substance-use offerings.

**Why it matters:** this is stronger evidence than a county shortage label, but
it still stops before lived access. The public materials show a call or portal
handoff, not a verified appointment slot, wait time, service-specific schedule,
insurance acceptance for a person, transportation feasibility, or completed
care. The HRSA file and current site page also list different phone numbers,
and the login-gated portal exposes no public booking availability.

A place can therefore have a designated provider, a named active site, listed
hours, and an affordability promise while a patient still faces uncertainty
about whether the route will work. The conversion cost may become calling,
waiting, travel, paperwork, payment uncertainty, or giving up.

**Do not overread:** organization-level statements are not a same-person
appointment or outcome record. “72 hours” does not identify which service is
available when, and the evidence does not establish completed treatment,
continuity, health, work, household, trust, or switching outcomes.

Read the [named-site care-access checkpoint](projects/us-local-business-place/hrsa-hpwo-allen-county-named-site-access-v1.md).

## 136. Disaster insurance can move money before it makes a home livable again

California's wildfire claims tracker reported 41,727 claims for the Eaton and
Palisades fires and 40,482 claims partially paid, with $25.2 billion paid as of
July 6, 2026. The tracker combines home, business, living-expense, auto,
debris-removal, and other claims, so these are processing and payment stages,
not a household repair rate.

At the same time, a State Farm investigation reviewed 220 claims and found 398
violations in 114 claims, including delayed investigation, underpayment,
reassigned adjusters, and smoke-damage delays or denials. California then
enacted AB 1795 standards for investigating, testing, and restoring
smoke-damaged homes. The law shifts some uncertainty toward insurers: it
requires necessary testing and sampling costs, inspection within 30 days after
claim notice or access, health-and-safety repair estimates, continued
additional-living-expense protection until safe restoration, and contractor
choice.

**Why it matters:** money can start moving while the home is still unsafe,
unrepaired, or difficult to inhabit. A regulator can identify failure and a
state can enact a standard while implementation, compliance, temporary
housing, health risk, deductible/debt burden, and safe return remain open. The
customer's problem can shift from proving loss to financing and coordinating
repair.

**Do not overread:** “partially paid” is not final settlement, adequate
compensation, completed repair, or stable housing. The claims data do not
identify unique properties or households, and the sampled State Farm review is
not a statewide violation rate. The new legal standard does not show that a
particular household received testing, adequate payment, continued living
expenses, completed repair, or a safe and affordable return. Public records do
not show who returned, moved, borrowed, or experienced lasting health or work
effects.

Read the [California wildfire insurance implementation finding](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/findings/us-housing-insurance-risk-005.md).

## 137. Semiconductor milestones and R&D governance move on different clocks

GAO reports that Commerce oversaw about $50 billion in semiconductor-related
programs. By July 2026, 49 projects across 24 companies had been awarded, and
$13.1 billion had been disbursed as of April 2026. Yet 125 milestones remained
in progress, 9 were past anticipated dates, and the workforce awards did not
show worker retention or a continuous skilled-labor pipeline.

The research side moved on a different clock: Commerce canceled awards
representing about $7.8 billion of the $11 billion appropriated for advanced
microelectronics R&D, while the NSTC operating model was being reset. GAO
warned that weak R&D implementation could extend dependence on foreign
suppliers or cede technological leadership.

**Why it matters:** public money, project awards, disbursements, workforce
activity, and institutional redesign are all real stages—but none alone proves
qualified chips, stable customers, retained workers, resilient supply, or
reduced foreign dependence. Domestic capacity is therefore not one number. It
has facility, materials, packaging, research, workforce, governance, customer,
and geopolitical clocks.

**Do not overread:** disbursement is not a production or completion rate, and
the delayed-milestone count uses anticipated dates rather than necessarily
contract deadlines. Canceled R&D awards do not by themselves prove failure or
waste, and GAO does not establish a causal effect on output, jobs, wages,
prices, defense supply, or geopolitical leverage.

Read the [CHIPS R&D capacity and dependence layer](projects/ai-work-control/gao-2026-chips-rd-capacity-governance-dependence-layer-v1.md).

## 138. AI access can spread faster than the local capability to adapt or control it

The World Bank's 2026 AI report separates three stages: adopting existing
tools, adapting them to local language, institutions, data, and needs, and
advancing the underlying models and infrastructure. It also identifies a
trade-off in concentration: a small number of firms and countries control much
of the frontier, which can make powerful tools affordable to outsiders while
creating dependence on providers, chips, data centers, and capital.

**Why it matters:** a tool can be available without being socially usable or
strategically controllable. A public agency, firm, or community may gain a
capability quickly while lacking staff who can evaluate it, procurement terms
that preserve switching, data protections, local-language fit, or the ability
to continue service when the provider changes price or rules. “AI adoption” is
therefore not the same as productivity, public capacity, sovereignty, or
worker benefit.

This also changes the cultural question. Imported capability may be useful and
still reshape whose norms, data rules, labor practices, and institutional
judgments govern everyday decisions. The outcome depends on complements and
alternatives, not on whether the system is simply domestic or foreign.

**Do not overread:** the report supplies a framework and comparative evidence,
not a US causal estimate or a measured provider-switching rate. It does not
establish improved citizen outcomes, equal access, changed US leverage, or a
specific agency's completed adaptation.

Read the [World Bank AI capability, complements, and governance layer](projects/ai-work-control/world-bank-wdr2026-ai-capability-governance-layer-v1.md).

## 139. A country can retain material power while losing agreement about what that power means

Pew's 2026 survey of 42,151 adults in 36 countries found a median 35% saying
the United States contributes to global peace and stability, compared with 57%
of U.S. adults. Inside the United States, the same measure was 82% among
Republicans and 35% among Democrats. At the same time, 83% of Americans across
parties said the United States interferes in other countries' affairs at least
a fair amount.

**Why it matters:** geopolitical power has a legitimacy and reception layer.
Military, financial, or technological capability can remain substantial while
domestic partisans, foreign publics, and institutions interpret the country's
role differently. Agreement that a country acts does not mean agreement that
its conduct is legitimate, peaceful, respectful, or worth supporting.

That difference can matter through alliance trust, aid acceptance, markets,
talent, media and cultural reputation, or domestic permission for policy. But
those are possible routes, not outcomes established by this survey.

**Do not overread:** public opinion is not a measure of actual intervention,
peace outcomes, diplomatic consultation, alliance behavior, trade, or changed
state leverage. The 36-country result is a median with equal country weighting,
and the survey does not identify which event caused any judgment or whether a
judgment changed later behavior.

Read the [U.S. global-role and international-reception layer](projects/ai-work-control/pew-2026-us-global-role-domestic-partisan-international-comparison-layer-v1.md).

## 140. Stable benefit status can coexist with more child-care help and more work prevention

In a public SIPP person-level comparison, November-to-December stable-SNAP
records had lower reported paid child-care use than stable-no-SNAP records,
23.9% versus 31.0%. But the stable-SNAP group had more reported child-care
assistance, 14.6% versus 5.2%, and a higher share saying child-care
arrangements prevented work or more work, 7.4% versus 3.4%.

**Why it matters:** benefit participation is not a simple security switch.
Some households may have more assistance because they have greater care need,
less ability to buy paid care, different eligibility, or a different work and
income mix. The same public system can therefore coexist with support, lower
paid-care use, and a continuing constraint on work.

This makes the household-capacity chain more precise:

```text
benefit status
  -> care assistance or paid-care route
  -> work or work-more constraint
  -> [open] care quality, earnings, child outcome, and durable security
```

**Do not overread:** the comparison is descriptive, not a SNAP effect estimate.
The SNAP status and annual child-care fields use different reference periods
and universes, and the groups differ in need, work, income, and household
composition. It does not show notice, benefit amount, care adequacy, employer
response, appeal, later earnings, trust, or exit.

Read the [SIPP SNAP stable-state and child-care bridge](projects/us-safety-net-access/sipp-snap-stable-state-childcare-bridge-v1.md).

## 141. A stable average day can hide unequal care-time sacrifice

The 2024–2025 American Time Use Survey shows only modest year-to-year movement
in average minutes spent on household work, care, paid work, travel, and
socializing. But a selected 2025 SIPP group of reference parents who said
child-care arrangements prevented work or more work reported very different
time-loss amounts by resource endpoint: 8.43 hours (SE 1.70) below 1× the
poverty line versus 24.40 hours (SE 7.25) at 4× poverty or above.

**Why it matters:** a stable population average does not mean equal time
capacity. Households can keep visible systems functioning by using unpaid
family care, reducing work, absorbing provider costs, borrowing, delaying bills,
or giving up rest and social connection. Care time, work prevention, lost
earnings, and household hardship are related currencies, but they are not one
measure of resilience.

The direction should not be read as a simple poverty ranking. The SIPP result
is conditional on reporting work prevention and a valid time-loss field, and
lower-resource respondents more often reported days while higher-resource
respondents more often reported hours. The selected groups may use different
care arrangements, work schedules, or reporting frames.

**Do not overread:** the time-loss screen is not a prevalence estimate for all
parents and does not establish lost earnings, a causal care effect, or harm.
ATUS means and SIPP time-loss records are different units and are not pooled;
the evidence does not show provider failure, care quality, later recovery,
trust, or political participation.

Read the [material, time, and care synthesis](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-044.md).

## 142. A benefit can be issued while control over it is stolen

SNAP is intended to supplement household income for food, but GAO reports that
EBT benefits can be taken through skimming, cloned cards, phishing, algorithmic
attacks, or unauthorized terminals. During the statutory replacement period
from October 2022 through December 20, 2024, states reported replacing more
than $320 million in stolen benefits for nearly 679,000 households.

**Why it matters:** formal receipt is not the same as usable protection. A
household can have an active benefit balance and still lose the ability to buy
food. The recovery route may require locking a card, changing a PIN, monitoring
an account, recognizing an unauthorized transaction, and filing a claim. The
system can therefore shift security work and the risk of a food gap onto the
recipient even while replacement authority and fraud controls exist.

This is a consumer-power problem as well as a public-benefit problem. New chip
cards, transaction blocking, monitoring, and mobile-payment pilots may reduce
theft, but a control can also add steps or false positives for people who need
accessible, online, or out-of-state use.

**Do not overread:** the $320 million and nearly 679,000 households are
approved replacement claims, not the full theft population or a recovery rate.
Some people may not know how to file, claims can be denied, and the public
record does not show claim effort, replacement speed, interim food support,
next-month stability, trust, or continued use for the same household.

Read the [SNAP EBT control-and-recovery finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-019.md).

## 143. A bank-system transition can create customer harm before the repair route is visible

In June 2026, the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau reported that Bilt's
move to a new bank partner caused payment problems for some customers,
including overdraft, late, and insufficient-funds fees. Bilt contacted
potentially affected customers and offered reimbursement. The Bureau later
reported that Bilt was reviewing requests and would reimburse more than 500
newly identified customers by June 4, while reporting that technical issues had
been resolved.

**Why it matters:** an internal architecture decision for a firm can become a
failed payment, fee, late-payment risk, or loss of liquidity for a customer.
Firm-level repair and proactive outreach are real institutional actions, but
they do not prove that every affected customer was reached, reimbursed, or
returned to the same financial position. A system can be repaired while the
customer's money, credit position, time, and trust remain unsettled.

**Do not overread:** the CFPB account does not identify the affected-account
denominator, each fee or failure, contact success, individual reimbursement,
account correction, repeat-failure rate, or later switching. “To reimburse” and
agency-reported process completion are not customer-level receipt or adequacy.

Read the [Bilt bank-partner transition and consumer-recourse finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-033.md).

## 144. Shared moral pessimism does not create a shared public remedy

Gallup's May 2026 Values and Beliefs poll found that 56% rated U.S. moral
values as poor and 80% said values were getting worse. At the same time, 69%
said government policies significantly affect moral values, while the public
was nearly split over whether government should promote moral values: 45% said
it should and 50% said it should not.

**Why it matters:** people can share a decline story without sharing a theory
of cause, authority, or repair. “The country is getting worse” does not tell us
whether people want government action, family or religious action, market
change, local organizing, or no formal intervention. Cultural concern is not
yet a shared political program.

This helps explain why broad moral or social agreement can coexist with
conflict over policy, legitimacy, and institutional power. The meaning of the
same concern is filtered through party, religion, identity, and the issue being
judged.

**Do not overread:** these are cross-sectional judgments, not objective measures
of conduct or a causal polarization estimate. Believing government influences
values is not the same as believing government should intervene, and the poll
does not show later action, policy implementation, trust, or conflict.

Read the [moral pessimism and government-role finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-047.md).

## 145. Firm AI adoption can precede measured impact while expectations already diverge

An NBER executive survey reports that 69% of firms were actively using AI,
while 90% of executives reported no employment or productivity impact at their
own firm over the previous three years. At the same time, executives expected
average next-three-year productivity growth of 1.4%, output growth of 0.8%, and
employment decline of 0.7% at their firms. Employees' expected employment
change was positive at 0.5%.

**Why it matters:** adoption is not the same as realized displacement, but
expectations can begin reallocating power before the measured outcome arrives.
Managers may plan around future productivity or headcount while workers
anticipate a different employment future. The social question starts after the
adoption statistic: who controls implementation, who receives the gain, and
who absorbs changes in tasks, training, monitoring, pay, hours, or security?

**Do not overread:** the survey covers executives in four countries and is not
a US worker panel. Expectations are not realized outcomes or a causal estimate;
the public record does not join firms to workers, tasks, wages, bargaining,
household security, retention, switching, or exit. This is a transition signal,
not evidence that AI has already displaced workers.

Read the [firm AI adoption and expectation finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-108.md).

## 146. Infrastructure rules can assign risk before anyone sees the realized bill

Virginia's State Corporation Commission describes a safeguard package for
large-load customers such as data centers: separate rates tied to their costs,
contracts requiring customers to take and pay for service for at least 14
years, minimum payment of 85% of transmission and distribution costs even when
actual use is lower, collateral of up to 60% of minimum charges for qualifying
new customers, and Commission review of the interconnection process.

**Why it matters:** the policy is trying to decide who carries uncertainty
when projected demand does not become ordinary, continuous demand. This is a
state-capacity and distributional-power question, not simply a question of
how many data centers exist. A rule can protect existing customers in
principle by shifting demand, credit, and direct-connection risk toward the
large customer that creates it.

**Do not overread:** these are described obligations and possible safeguards,
not evidence of a signed contract, collateral posting, minimum-charge payment,
interconnection approval, energized load, enforcement, or household bill
change. The public record does not yet show whether the rules prevented cost
shifting, improved reliability, or produced durable public legitimacy.

Read the [Virginia large-load risk-allocation finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-103.md).

## 147. A defense schedule can have two official clocks without proving delivery

Polish Ministry of National Defence pages describe two planning windows for the
JASSM-ER acquisition. The May 2024 contract announcement says deliveries will
occur in 2026–2030; a December 2024 modernization briefing describes arrival
in 2028–2030 and connects the timing to supply-chain alignment with the
readiness of Poland's expanded fighter fleet.

**Why it matters:** acquisition intent, supply-chain timing, and fleet
readiness can be coordinated without the public record yet showing a physical
missile, accepted lot, trained unit, or usable inventory. A schedule is its own
realization state. It should not be collapsed into “capability acquired,” and
the difference between planning windows should not automatically be called a
delay or failure.

The strategic chain remains:

```text
contract and schedule
  -> shipment
  -> acceptance and unit receipt
  -> training, maintenance, and fielding
  -> operational use and external response [open]
```

**Do not overread:** the public pages do not identify an order-linked lot,
accepted quantity, shipment, training completion, inventory change, or external
deterrence effect. A separate family-level delivery record cannot resolve the
current order's status.

Read the [JASSM-ER schedule and realization finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-102.md) and the [latest official-source delivery recheck](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-081.md).

## 148. A later refund can be targeted to people who already cleared the first access hurdle

The FTC's 2022 refund report says Yellowstone Capital mailed 7,731 first-round
payments and that 3,557 businesses had cashed a payment by the reporting date:
46% of the eligible group. The FTC's June 2026 page then says that a third
round of 3,759 checks was sent to small businesses that had cashed a previous
check.

**Why it matters:** the later payment is not reaching a random cross-section of
everyone harmed. It is conditioned on an earlier successful route. That may be
sensible administration, but people who lacked notice, had an old address,
could not use the payment channel, or did not complete the first route may
disappear from later rounds. A refund system can return money to some
recipients while selecting for the businesses that were able to navigate the
first payment process.

**Do not overread:** 46% is a first-round cashing count divided by the eligible
count, not a recovery rate. The 2026 figure is checks sent to prior cashers,
not third-check cashing. The public record does not show delivery, reissue,
account correction, remaining loss, continued operation, trust, or exit for
the same businesses.

Read the [Yellowstone refund reachability finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-035.md).

## 149. An ownership promise can change worker meaning before it changes worker wealth or power

In one U.S. building-materials supplier, workers who received concrete
explanations connecting daily behavior to the value of their stock-appreciation
rights were reported to be 13 percentage points less likely to leave over six
months than workers who received broader values-based messaging. The difference
became larger around Hurricane Helene.

**Why it matters:** a contingent financial claim does not become meaningful
through ownership language alone. It can become culturally salient when workers
can see how present work might affect a future value. That may strengthen
identification and short-run retention during disruption, linking firm design
to worker meaning and behavior.

**Do not overread:** this is one company and a short window. Both groups were
inside the same ownership program, so the result compares communication styles,
not ownership with no ownership. Retention is not payout, wealth, safety,
decision power, or durable security; the study does not show whether the claim
vested, was paid, or gave workers governance control.

Read the [employee-ownership meaning finding](projects/us-employee-ownership-meaning/findings/us-employee-ownership-meaning-001.md).

## 150. The same economic message can arrive as information or partisan threat

In a survey experiment with 5,205 U.S. adults, people who saw the Federal
Reserve as an in-group reported more trust, wanted more information from it,
and gave its communication more weight when updating inflation expectations.
People who saw it as an out-group were less trusting and interpreted the same
institution through a different identity filter.

**Why it matters:** institutional communication does not enter an empty mind.
Perceived alignment is a gate between a public message and what people believe
it means. An economic announcement can therefore be treated as useful
guidance, partisan evidence, or a threat before it reaches decisions about
spending, saving, borrowing, or political responsibility.

**Do not overread:** perceived alignment is not proof that the Fed is actually
partisan. The experiment measures short-run information demand and expectation
updating, not actual spending, refinancing, voting, protest, or durable trust.
The study is not a same-household chain from message to economic behavior to
political action.

Read the [Federal Reserve partisan-trust finding](projects/us-fed-partisan-trust/findings/us-fed-partisan-trust-001.md).

## 151. Digital life is not one platform trend

The evidence separates several digital lives. Adults report broad but uneven
use of platforms; teens combine routine social-platform use with growing
chatbot use; adults use chatbots for information, work, health, and advice
while expressing concern about privacy and regulation; and news engagement
contains distinct attention, trust, and civic-action styles.

**Why it matters:** “digital exposure” is too broad to explain culture or
consumer behavior. A teen using a chatbot for schoolwork, an adult using one
for medical advice, and a voter encountering news through a social platform
face different risks, alternatives, and power relations. Frequency is not
reliance, reach is not influence, and usefulness is not control.

**Do not overread:** these are separate surveys with different dates, samples,
questions, and valid bases. They do not show that platforms caused beliefs,
dependence, privacy loss, political action, or harm. The missing end-to-end
record is a person-level path from need to digital encounter, time and trust,
correction or complaint, human alternative, and successful exit.

Read the [digital-life cross-source finding](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-005.md).

## 152. Automatic saving can build future assets while changing short-term borrowing

The Oregon Auto-IRA evidence adds a useful counterexample to one-dimensional
financial-policy stories. The study reports increases in retirement-plan
ownership and IRA assets, alongside increases in checking or savings measures.
The available summary also reports a roughly two-percentage-point increase in
credit-card-debt incidence and about $90 higher credit-card balances.

**Why it matters:** a policy can improve future asset ownership while changing
how households manage current liquidity. “More saving” is not automatically
the same as more emergency room today, and “more debt” does not by itself show
that the policy harmed the household. The real question is who absorbs the
short-term adjustment and whether the future asset is accessible, adequate,
and durable.

**Do not overread:** the results come through a reported secondary summary of a
state-policy comparison, not a new independent analysis here. Higher balances
do not reveal fees, withdrawals, take-home pay, interest, delinquency, useful
asset purchases, or retirement timing. The evidence does not establish a
single net-benefit score or political response.

Read the [Auto-IRA balance-sheet finding](projects/us-auto-ira-household-balance-sheets/findings/us-auto-ira-household-balance-sheets-001.md).

## 153. Digital health convenience can carry a privacy, billing, and exit bundle

The FTC and two states sued Hims & Hers in July 2026, alleging that the
telehealth provider shared sensitive health information with advertising
platforms despite privacy assurances, enrolled or charged consumers around
prescription subscriptions, and made cancellation difficult. The case is
pending.

**Why it matters:** digital health joins several currencies in one ordinary
consumer route: a private health need, online intake, data disclosure,
recurring payment, prescription or refill timing, and the ability to leave.
Convenience may reduce travel and waiting while also making privacy, billing,
treatment continuity, and exit more consequential than in an ordinary
subscription.

**Do not overread:** the complaint is an allegation, not a final finding. The
public record does not show which individuals were affected, actual data use,
charge incidence, treatment harm, refunds, deletion, successful cancellation,
or durable trust. A released complaint file shows institutional response, not
a customer denominator or verified remedy.

Read the [digital-health privacy and exit finding](projects/us-privacy-trust-data-sharing/findings/us-privacy-trust-data-sharing-001.md).

## 154. A business revenue shock can reach the owner household without a one-for-one spending collapse

Linked business and owner-household account data show a roughly 40% decline
in business revenue during the first two months after the March 2020 national
emergency. Each dollar of lost business revenue was associated with about a
1.6-cent decline in owner consumption, and the muted pass-through persisted
through 2021.

**Why it matters:** a firm and a household can share one financial environment
without moving in lockstep. Stable-looking household consumption may reflect
buffers, public support, reduced spending opportunities, borrowing, delayed
payments, or business disinvestment—not absence of strain. The result connects
firm capacity, household security, and consumer behavior while keeping workers,
customers, and firm survival as separate outcomes.

**Do not overread:** the retained summary does not identify each owner's cash
buffers, debt, subsidy timing, business survival, worker outcomes, or later
recovery. The COVID shock and policy environment are not the same as a natural
disaster-loan episode. Consumption stability is not proof of welfare, and the
1.6-cent estimate is not a universal household rule.

Read the [owner-household business-shock finding](projects/us-owner-household-business-tradeoff/findings/us-owner-business-revenue-consumption-covid-001.md).

## 155. Material pressure changes what people search for more than whether they use AI

In the June 2025 Census household file, people reporting greater expense
difficulty were much more likely to search online for jobs, food, benefits,
housing, medical help, or credit. For example, job searching rose from 20.7%
among those reporting no difficulty to 57.0% among those reporting very great
difficulty. Broad AI use, by contrast, was 59.0% in the first group and 58.4%
in the last, with no monotonic gradient.

**Why it matters:** when room gets tight, search becomes a practical
navigation behavior across several systems. People may be building an option
menu before they buy, apply, switch, or exit. This is an effort and
option-discovery signal, not proof of digital power or recovery.

**Do not overread:** this is a same-round association, not a causal panel. The
file does not show who searched, whether the result was accurate or usable,
whether an application or transaction was completed, or whether help restored
security. Search can rise because need is greater; it does not mean the need
was solved, and AI use is not a proxy for adaptation.

Read the [material-pressure and consumer-search finding](projects/us-consumer-culture/findings/us-consumer-culture-003.md).

## 156. Later retirement can mean more choice—or that immediate room is still insufficient

The Auto-IRA retirement-timing evidence reports that after state adoption,
early retirement before age 65 fell by about 1.2 percentage points, retirement
past age 67 rose by about 3 points, early Social Security claiming fell by
about 1 point, and claiming at age 70 rose by about 1.8 points.

**Why it matters:** automatic saving may affect not only whether people have an
account, but when they leave work and claim benefits. Delaying retirement or
claiming can reflect a larger future benefit and expanded choice, but it can
also reflect inadequate current savings, health constraints, care duties, job
conditions, or the need to keep earning. “Later” is not automatically “better.”

**Do not overread:** this is a staggered state-policy comparison, not proof of
the mechanism for every worker. The evidence does not show that extra saving
caused the timing change, and the full-controls model reports no significant
change in total retirement assets. It does not measure job quality, health,
care, debt cost, household needs, or whether the later benefit outweighed the
cost of working longer.

Read the [Auto-IRA retirement-timing finding](projects/us-auto-ira-household-balance-sheets/findings/us-auto-ira-retirement-claiming-001.md).

## 157. Worker power is a stack, not one outcome

The worker evidence separates several channels that are often collapsed into
the word “empowerment”: a financial stake, understandable information,
representative voice, technical control, an appeal or recourse route, actual
enforcement, household security, and the ability to leave or switch. A worker
can gain one channel without gaining the others.

**Why it matters:** a stock-appreciation right can change identification while
remaining contingent; a works council can exist without a documented veto; a
software override can be published without being safe to use; reactivation can
restore access while lost pay remains unpaid; and a monetary order can exist
without household recovery. The broad question is not “did empowerment rise?”
but “who received money, information, contestability, time, and actual choice?”

**Do not overread:** this is a mechanism comparison across different firms,
jurisdictions, and units, not a labor-market estimate. Retention is not wealth,
consultation is not bargaining power, an override is not practical refusal,
reactivation is not recovery, and an award is not receipt or durable security.

Read the [worker-power channels synthesis](projects/us-employee-ownership-meaning/findings/us-employee-ownership-meaning-002.md).

## 158. A shared checkout price can hide different payment-system burdens

Merchant payment data and an incidence model estimate that interchange fees
and card rewards can redistribute value between cash/debit users and
credit-card users. The paper estimates roughly $30 billion a year in gross
redistribution, reduced by about 25% after consumer sorting and merchant-fee
differences, with about $9.2 billion modeled as moving from households below
$150,000 toward households above that threshold.

**Why it matters:** the visible price is not always the full distributional
object. The same nominal price can coexist with different rewards, credit
access, fraud protection, convenience, liquidity, and merchant choices. Who
can use the rewarding payment rail—and who cannot—may shape the effective
price and financial room even when the checkout price looks identical.

**Do not overread:** these are modeled incidence estimates based on merchant
and payment-platform data, not observed household losses on bills. Rewards may
pay for credit, convenience, or fraud protection; merchant sorting and
negotiated fees can change the pattern. The evidence does not show household
stress, debt, distrust, or political action caused by the payment mechanism.

Read the [payment-rewards redistribution finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-003.md).

## 159. Formal bank access can coexist with unequal substitution and limited switching

The Federal Reserve's 2025 household survey reports that 6% of adults were
unbanked, with a much higher rate below $25,000 of family income than at
$100,000 or more. Among unbanked adults, 28% used nonbank check-cashing or
money-order routes, compared with 11% among banked adults. Among people
reporting non-credit-card fraud, 35% said some money was not recovered, 67%
contacted a financial provider, and 12% changed banks or financial companies.

**Why it matters:** an account doorway is not the same as usable financial
control. A person may have an account yet face overdraft cost, fraud exposure,
recovery work, or a weak alternative. Switching is a real endpoint, but not
switching can mean satisfaction, inertia, lock-in, lack of alternatives, or a
different response—not necessarily that the provider worked well.

**Do not overread:** these are survey-reported population measures, not a
same-account episode. Nonbank use is not automatically exclusion or harm, and
contacting a provider is not verified correction. The data do not show the
amount or timing of recovery, provider liability, alternative quality, trust,
or why each person stayed or left.

Read the [financial access, substitution, and recourse finding](projects/us-financial-intermediation/findings/us-financial-intermediation-003.md).

## 160. Aggregate refund acceptance is not the same as individual recovery

The Amazon Prime settlement creates a deeper remedy record than an enforcement
announcement alone. The FTC reports a $1.5 billion customer-refund pool and a
$1 billion civil penalty. A court filing reports that, as of September 9,
2026, $845.2 million had been issued and $235.5 million accepted across the
automatic-payment and claims-process phases. A revised order expands automatic
eligibility for some customers and raises the possible maximum payment.

**Why it matters:** the remedy now has visible legal eligibility,
distribution, and acceptance stages, but aggregate money still does not tell
us whether a specific customer got paid, how long it took, whether the amount
was adequate, or whether the customer continued, switched, or recovered lost
household room. A refund can repair a charge while leaving the time, attention,
and dependence created by the subscription route unchanged.

**Do not overread:** issued and accepted dollars are not eligible-customer
counts, a receipt rate, or a recovery rate. The public record does not join
enrollment intent, cancellation effort, claim completion, individual receipt,
remaining loss, continued use, switching, trust, or household recovery.

Read the [Amazon Prime refund implementation finding](projects/us-subscription-inattention/findings/us-subscription-inattention-002.md).

## 161. Import reliance is not the same as usable substitution or strategic autonomy

GAO and USGS evidence shows why critical-mineral dependence must be measured
at the mineral–application–process level. A material may be replaceable in one
battery use but difficult to substitute in a semiconductor because purity,
performance, energy density, volume, price, and qualification requirements
differ. Battery recycling may offer a two- to three-year reduction path under
the right collection and processing conditions, while near-term semiconductor
substitution remains more constrained.

**Why it matters:** a policy, research award, or technical possibility is not
yet a working supply alternative. The real infrastructure chain is import
reliance → shipment, purity, price, and inventory exposure → qualified
substitute, recycling, or redesign → operating output → supplier switching and
external leverage. Domestic research can improve strategic room without yet
reducing dependence.

**Do not overread:** the evidence does not identify a particular shortage,
export cutoff, firm loss, consumer price, worker burden, or geopolitical
concession. It does not establish operating recycling output, substitute
qualification, or national mineral independence. A downstream domestic factory
can still depend on imported material.

Read the [critical-minerals substitution and autonomy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-100.md).

## 162. Defense spending can build industrial allocation before it becomes usable strategic capability

Poland's Ministry of National Defence reports that its SAFE program has
produced 62 contracts worth about 120 billion złoty, with nearly 90% of the
money expected to go to Polish defense companies. The same program material
describes equipment and delivery schedules extending into later years.

**Why it matters:** a state can turn security pressure and external financing
into contracts and domestic supplier allocation before it has delivered,
accepted, trained, maintained, and operationally used the equipment. Domestic
industrial participation is an economic and political outcome, but it is not
yet proof of usable force, replaceability, deterrence, or changed foreign
behavior.

**Do not overread:** the official figures are program-level claims. They do
not show firm-level production, shipment, acceptance, workforce effects,
maintenance, unit readiness, or an observed change in another actor's choices.
The next test is one identifier-bearing contract followed through delivery,
training, maintenance, operational use, and an external or alliance response.

Read the [defense-industrial realization finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-118.md).

## 163. A platform-work protection can create a right to challenge deactivation before it becomes a usable remedy

New York City's Local Law 52 is designed to give high-volume for-hire-vehicle
drivers advance notice, reasons, relevant performance and complaint data, an
informal resolution route, and possible reinstatement or back pay after a
wrongful deactivation. But a federal court preliminarily enjoined the law in
July 2026, and the city's implementation rules remain proposed while an appeal
is pending.

**Why it matters:** platform access is not only a customer-service issue when
it is an income-producing relationship. Documentation and contestability can
turn an opaque platform decision into something a worker can challenge. But a
formal right is not yet practical protection: the legal clock can move while
the worker's income clock is already running.

**Do not overread:** the law's design does not show notice, usable data,
completed challenge, reactivation, back-pay receipt, continued work, or
household recovery for any driver. The next test is a post-law driver episode
followed from deactivation through challenge, platform response, payment or
reactivation, and later use or exit.

Read the [platform deactivation protection finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-116.md).

## 164. An active clinic with listed hours is still not the same as usable care

In an Ohio HRSA crosswalk, 61 organizations shared identifiers between a
primary-care shortage file and a service-site file. Those organizations covered
662 service-site rows; 641 listed positive hours, with a median of 40 hours per
week. But 510 sites had unknown full- or part-time schedules, and the named
North Lima site had conflicting public phone numbers and no publicly visible
appointment slots or wait times.

**Why it matters:** the route from shortage to care has several separate
steps: a designated need, a named organization, an active site, listed hours,
a working contact route, an available appointment, completed care, and
continuity. “Active” and “72 hours” establish nominal capacity, not that a
particular person can book, afford, reach, complete, or continue care.

**Do not overread:** this is an administrative site-access audit, not a wait-
time, appointment, utilization, or health-outcome estimate. It does not show
that the phone discrepancy blocked anyone or that the site lacked capacity.
The next test is a person- or appointment-level episode linking need, contact,
wait, travel, payment or coverage, completed care, and continuity.

Read the [named-site care-access checkpoint](projects/us-local-business-place/hrsa-hpwo-allen-county-named-site-access-v1.md).

## 165. Affordability can become politically salient without producing a shared representative

In a July 2026 Pew survey, 29% of registered voters named the economy as the
issue they most wanted congressional candidates to discuss, including 15% who
specifically named cost of living or affordability. Yet party advantage on
economic policy was nearly even: 37% said they agreed more with Democrats and
36% with Republicans. On AI policy, 51% said neither party represented them.

**Why it matters:** material pressure does not automatically create one
political coalition. Economic concern, blame or leader attribution, party fit,
campaign attention, and actual action are different political currencies. A
public can agree that affordability matters while disagreeing about who is
responsible or which institution can repair it.

**Do not overread:** this is a cross-sectional survey, not a household price,
bill, job, or debt panel, and it does not establish turnout, contact, policy
delivery, or recovery. The next test is a repeated or event-linked design
joining material exposure, attribution, campaign contact, validated action,
implemented policy, and later household outcome.

Read the [economic attribution and representation finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-046.md).

## 166. People may be willing to help locally without feeling protected by local reciprocity

In Pew's March 2025 American Trends Panel, 76% of adults said they were very
or extremely likely to bring a neighbor's mail or water plants, while 52%
expected neighbors to do the same. Sixty-seven percent said they would bring a
meal to a sick neighbor or friend, compared with 42% who expected that help.
For emergency loans to a friend, willingness was 50% and expected help was
45%.

**Why it matters:** social resilience is not just goodwill. It also depends on
whether people believe support will be available, reciprocal, and usable when
something goes wrong. A community can contain substantial willingness to give
while many people still feel uncertain about being protected themselves.

**Do not overread:** these are hypothetical, cross-sectional answers, not
verified aid deliveries or evidence that neighbors fail one another. The data
do not show who requested help, what it cost the helper, whether the support
was adequate, or whether it changed later trust or civic action. The next test
is a place-linked or repeated episode following help requested, delivered,
burden, adequacy, recovery, and later movement.

Read the [local reciprocity and mutual-aid finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-044.md).

## 167. AI access and ownership shape distribution before displacement

The current evidence separates three layers. An NBER executive survey reports
active firm AI use alongside little reported past employment or productivity
impact and different expected future employment changes for employers and
employees. The Federal Reserve's 2025 household survey reports worker AI use
is higher among people with more education and greater task control. An IMF
model shows that wage inequality could fall while wealth inequality rises when
task complementarity, capital returns, and firm adoption interact.

**Why it matters:** the social distribution of AI begins with access,
permission, task control, learning capacity, and ownership—not only with who
eventually loses a job. A worker may be exposed to a tool without being allowed
to use it, trained to use it, paid for the productivity gain, or able to contest
how it evaluates them.

**Do not overread:** these are different units, periods, countries, and
methods, not one pooled US result. The evidence does not yet show which
mechanism dominates in actual firms or households. The decisive next test is a
matched firm-worker episode linking adoption, task change, permission,
training, monitoring, pay, voice, household effect, and later retention or
exit.

Read the [AI firm-worker distribution finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-110.md).

## 168. Benefit receipt and household security can diverge when work capacity and children differ

In the 2025 SIPP analysis, people who remained on SNAP while reporting a
work-limiting condition and children in the household had following-month
rent or mortgage hardship of 17.85% and utility hardship of 31.57%. The stable-
SNAP group with neither marker reported 7.77% and 11.60% respectively.

**Why it matters:** formal benefit receipt is not the same as a secured
household. The same program status can sit alongside very different remaining
rent and utility exposure depending on work capacity and household demands.
Food support, housing room, utilities, care responsibilities, and ability to
work are separate currencies of security.

**Do not overread:** this is a selected, descriptive same-person comparison,
not a SNAP effect or a national hardship rate. The data do not identify notice,
benefit amount, appeal, food quantity, care obtained, or later recovery. The
next test is a same-episode record linking benefit timing and amount to bills,
work or care trade-offs, correction, and later household security.

Read the [SNAP work-capacity and children finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-018.md).

## 169. An issued benefit can become practical dispossession when account control is stolen

SNAP benefits are meant to support food purchases, but GAO documents theft
through skimming, cloned cards, phishing, algorithmic attacks, and unauthorized
terminals. A recipient can therefore have an active benefit balance while being
unable to use it for food. During the statutory replacement period from
October 2022 through December 2024, states reported replacing more than $320
million in stolen benefits for nearly 679,000 households.

**Why it matters:** formal receipt is not the same as control. The recovery
route may require a recipient to notice the loss, lock a card, change a PIN,
file a claim, and wait for replacement while finding food or cash elsewhere.
Fraud prevention is therefore also a time, access, and usability issue—not
only a technical-security issue.

**Do not overread:** the replacement totals are approved claims, not a theft
rate or household recovery rate. They do not show claim effort, replacement
timing, an interim food gap, next-month stability, or restored trust for any
household. The next test is a same-episode route from transaction loss through
claim, replacement, interim substitution, and later food security.

Read the [SNAP EBT control and recovery finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-019.md).

## 170. Progress relative to parents does not guarantee belief in an attainable future

Pew's 2025 National Survey of Latinos finds that 57% say their standard of
living is better than their parents', and 53% expect their children's standard
of living to be better than theirs. Yet only 20% say they have achieved the
American dream, while 18% say it is out of reach and 27% say it does not exist.
Identity adds another split: 61% say being Hispanic is very or extremely
important to how they think about themselves, while 33% say it hurts their
ability to get ahead at least a little, 26% say it helps, and 40% say it makes
no difference.

**Why it matters:** progress is relational, while opportunity is judged in the
present and future. A person can be doing better than their parents, hope for
their children, and still experience housing, work, institutions, or identity
as exclusionary or difficult to control. Identity centrality and identity
valence are also different: belonging can be important without having one
fixed political or consumer meaning.

**Do not overread:** these are cross-sectional reported judgments, not linked
income, wealth, housing, discrimination, or later-action records. They do not
establish mobility, assimilation, voting, switching, or institutional remedy.
The next test is a dated work, housing, school, health, or service encounter
linked to interpretation, action, and later mobility or belonging.

Read the [Latino mobility, American-dream, and identity finding](projects/us-immigration-local-demand/findings/us-immigration-local-demand-004.md).

## 171. Moral pessimism can coexist with contested legitimacy

Gallup's May 2026 Values and Beliefs poll reports that 56% rate U.S. moral
values as poor and 80% say values are getting worse. At the same time, 69%
say government policies significantly affect moral values, while the public
is nearly divided over whether government should promote moral values: 45% say
it should and 50% say it should not.

**Why it matters:** people can share a decline story without sharing an
authority, cause, or repair plan. “The country is getting worse” is not yet a
common political program. Cultural legitimacy depends on who is allowed to
define the problem, which issues count as evidence, and whether intervention
feels like protection or coercion.

**Do not overread:** these are cross-sectional judgments, not measures of
objective conduct or a single polarization mechanism. Government influence is
not the same as government legitimacy, and moral concern is not observed
policy action. The next test is an event-linked design joining an institutional
encounter to identity, attribution, issue meaning, action, implementation, and
later legitimacy or trust.

Read the [moral-values and public-remedy finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-047.md).

## 172. Material insecurity can reduce political action without producing one uniform judgment

In a retained 2010–2012–2014 CCES panel, respondents reporting no health
insurance in 2012 were less likely in 2014 to report congressional contact
(31.75% versus 40.29%) or local political action (21.81% versus 32.48%) than
respondents reporting some insurance. But favorable Congress approval was
slightly higher in the no-insurance group (11.51% versus 10.23%), while
