unfavorable approval was lower (74.77% versus 83.07%).

**Why it matters:** insecurity does not create one automatic ladder from
hardship to distrust to withdrawal. A person may have less time, health,
money, access, or issue fit for contact and action while holding a different or
even less hostile institutional judgment. Participation, approval, trust, and
recovery are separate political currencies.

**Do not overread:** insurance status is not a dated coverage loss, bill,
denial, or care episode, and this descriptive panel does not establish cause.
It does not show attribution, remedy, later recovery, or why someone acted or
did not act. The next test is a dated coverage or care episode linked to
alternatives, institutional response, later trust, action, and exit.

Read the [insurance status and political action finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-043.md).

## 173. Expense pressure expands search across income groups, but not in the same way

In a same-respondent April-to-June 2025 Census HTOPS link, people reporting
expense difficulty in April searched more in June for jobs, food, benefits,
credit, housing, medical help, or other options across every household-income
band. The route differed: below $50,000, job search was 39.8% with difficulty
versus 16.3% without it, while at $100,000 or more it was 37.0% versus 25.1%.
AI use was much higher in the top-income group overall, but expense difficulty
barely changed it there (71.5% versus 71.7%).

**Why it matters:** material pressure produces option-building effort across
the distribution, but resources shape which options are searched. Searching
also appears multi-front: people under pressure often look across several
systems at once. AI is not a simple substitute for this navigation; it may
coexist with more searching because need, access, or engagement is greater.

**Do not overread:** this is a descriptive same-respondent association, not a
causal panel estimate. Search does not prove a usable alternative, completed
application, accurate answer, transaction, remedy, or recovery, and the design
does not identify whether AI caused or solved any search burden.

Read the [income-stratified material-search finding](projects/us-consumer-culture/findings/us-consumer-culture-005.md).

## 174. Financial worry enters politics through judgment and identity, not a simple ladder

In a retained 2016–2020–2024 ANES panel, 70.3% of the extremely financially
worried group placed themselves in “some of the time” or “never” trusting the
federal government, compared with 51.9% of the group that was not at all
worried. In a post-election cross-tab, the extremely worried group reported
55.4% voting for Trump and 36.9% for Harris, while the not-at-all-worried group
reported 32.0% and 66.5%.

**Why it matters:** material pressure can become political through
interpretation. The same worry may be filtered through party identity,
information, candidate preference, and beliefs about who is responsible. It is
not automatically an engagement ladder, withdrawal ladder, or single voting
response.

**Do not overread:** worry is subjective and the panel does not identify a
dated bill, price, employer, household shock, responsible actor, or practical
alternative. The vote is self-reported, and no remedy, policy response, later
trust, recovery, organizing, or exit is observed. The next test is an
event-linked record joining burden, attribution, alternatives, action,
institutional response, and later material outcome.

Read the [ANES financial-worry, trust, and vote finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-045.md).

## 175. National health surveys can miss the accessibility interface

GAO identifies physical, sensory, communication, cognitive, digital, and
provider-interaction barriers that can keep health-care capacity from becoming
usable care for people with disabilities. Examples include inaccessible exam
equipment, check-in kiosks and apps, sensory environments, communication needs,
and provider assumptions or denials. GAO also found that none of 12 reviewed
HHS population-health surveys collected national-level data on most of these
accessibility barriers.

**Why it matters:** coverage, an available provider, and a digital route are
not automatically a completed, safe, continuous encounter. When the interface
fails, the person or family may absorb the conversion cost through extra time,
delayed or avoided care, disrupted work, and family strain. Oversight capacity
is not the same as corrected access.

**Do not overread:** the GAO survey is nongeneralizable and does not provide a
national prevalence or causal health estimate. A complaint or oversight action
does not prove correction or restored care, and low complaint volume does not
prove low harm. The next test is a privacy-preserving encounter record linking
need, accommodation, receipt, completion, delay, continuity, family/work time,
complaint, correction, and later health.

Read the [disability health-care accessibility finding](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-016.md).

## 176. Unpaid eldercare is a distributed time network, not only a household burden

BLS estimates that 38.2 million people age 15 and older provided unpaid
eldercare in 2023–24, including 23.6 million employed providers and 18.4
million full-time workers. Providers spent an average of 1.07 hours per day
when averaged across all providers and days, but 3.88 hours on days when they
actually provided care. The provider rate was highest among people ages 55–64
(24.1%), so eldercare overlaps with late-career work, retirement planning, and
care for other family members.

**Why it matters:** care is social infrastructure carried through households,
wider kin, and community networks. The cost can appear in travel, household
work, schedule flexibility, rest, and social availability rather than a care
invoice. A population average and a care-day average answer different
questions: spread is not intensity, and neither alone proves lost earnings or
recipient harm.

**Do not overread:** the ATUS estimates do not follow a caregiver and recipient
longitudinally, identify displaced work or sleep, measure paid-care alternatives,
or establish causality. They do not show whether care was shared, whether the
recipient was safe, or whether work and health later changed. The next test is a
same-provider, same-recipient episode linking care frequency and travel to work,
earnings, sleep, health, replacement care, and recipient continuity.

Read the [unpaid eldercare hidden-second-job synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/aging-care-hidden-second-job-synthesis-v1.md).

## 177. Child-care disruption is converted into work, time, and housing strain

In the retained child-care evidence, households reporting inadequate-care
problems lost an average of 65.8 days of work time. Reported responses included
taking leave, cutting hours, quitting, stopping a job search, and working while
supervising children. About 30.4 million households reported a child age 13 or
younger; 23.9% used paid care, and payer households spent about $10,520 on
average, or 5.6% of income.

**Why it matters:** care is not one price. Households with more room may buy
care or leave; others transfer the problem into unpaid time, reduced work,
non-search, supervision while working, or later rent and housing strain. A
benefit or subsidy can lower a nominal price while a family still lacks a
dependable staffed slot.

**Do not overread:** the work-loss figure is a reported adaptation measure, not
a causal estimate of days lost because of care. Categories can overlap, and the
evidence does not link one household to a specific provider, wait, employer
response, completed care, or later recovery. The next test is a same-household
care episode joining provider availability, price or subsidy, schedule,
informal support, work hours, and later care/work outcome.

Read the [care supply, household adaptation, and work option-stack synthesis](projects/us-aging-care-strain/care-supply-work-option-stack-synthesis-v1.md).

## 178. Long-term-care coverage can pay for one part of a care bundle while leaving housing and family burden exposed

GAO identified at least $12 billion in Medicaid and traditional Medicare
spending for services provided in assisted-living facilities in 2024, including
at least $3.5 billion in federal Medicaid spending and $8.5 billion in
traditional Medicare spending. Medicaid programs in 44 states covered some
assisted-living services for older adults and people with disabilities, while
most residents still used personal resources and other support for parts of
the arrangement.

**Why it matters:** “covered” is not one thing in long-term care. Medicaid may
pay for daily assistance or therapy, Medicare for a health service or hospice,
and a household for room and board; HUD, VA, or family members may fill other
gaps. A waiver can make a service formally available while enrollment limits,
provider supply, geography, or family capacity still determine whether a safe,
affordable placement exists.

**Do not overread:** the $12 billion is a minimum and not total resident cost,
quality, access, or welfare. The 44-state count is policy coverage, not a
resident access rate. The report does not show wait time, authorized slots,
provider participation, out-of-pocket cost, unpaid care, resident safety, or
family work consequences. The next test is a state- or facility-level episode
linking need, search, authorization, wait, admission, payer mix, room/board,
family labor, continuity, and later setting choice.

Read the [assisted-living care-finance finding](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-assisted-living-spending-medicaid-coverage-layer-v1.md).

## 179. Business applications are an intention signal, not durable local capacity

The 2023 national Census bridge shows retail with about 971,630 business
applications and 77,369 establishment openings, while health care and social
assistance had about 325,200 applications and 98,281 openings. Health care and
social assistance produced about 881,400 net jobs, compared with 52,232 in
retail. Agriculture, information, and administrative/support services had
negative net job creation despite recorded applications.

**Why it matters:** “business activity” is not one outcome. Applications can
signal interest before a firm opens, survives, hires, provides a service, or
changes local prices and access. Sector structure matters: a place can see
many applications without durable employers, while a lower-application sector
can generate much greater realized employment or essential capacity.

**Do not overread:** the files use different constructions, timing, and
aggregate units. Applications per opening are not a conversion rate, and net
job creation is not job quality, bargaining power, service access, ownership,
or local welfare. The next test is a state- or county-sector episode joining
applications to identifiable firms, entry, exit, employer stock, service,
wages, prices, and resident outcomes.

Read the [BFS–BDS complete sector profile](projects/us-local-business-place/bfs-bds-complete-sector-profile-v1.md).

## 180. Sector growth can coexist with heavy turnover, so net jobs do not mean stability

The 2023 Census BDS sector file shows health care and social assistance with
98,281 establishment openings and 70,254 closings alongside 881,400 net new
jobs. Accommodation and food services had 78,150 openings and 64,285 closings
alongside 791,588 net new jobs. Retail had 77,369 openings and 69,636 closings
but only 52,232 net new jobs.

**Why it matters:** growth can coexist with substantial entry and exit. A
sector can add jobs while workers, owners, and customers repeatedly encounter
new firms, closures, changed schedules, altered prices, or disrupted service
relationships. “The sector grew” does not tell us whether a particular place,
worker, or household experienced stability.

**Do not overread:** these are national annual aggregates, not identifiable
firm or worker episodes. Net job creation is not job quality, bargaining power,
service reliability, ownership, or resident welfare, and establishment flows
are not identical to firm births and deaths. The next test is a state- or
county-sector panel linking entry and exit to service, wage, price, and local
outcomes.

Read the [realized firm entry, exit, and sector-turnover layer](projects/us-local-business-place/bds-realized-entry-exit-sector-layer-v1.md).

## 181. Household adaptation is a stack of currencies, and improvement does not erase every adaptation

In a same-respondent 2024–2025 SHED panel, reported price adaptations often
persisted: among prior adopters, 76.6% still switched to cheaper products,
73.6% still used less or stopped products, 65.6% still delayed a major
purchase, 60.2% still reported reduced savings, and 53.2% still reported
increased borrowing. Among respondents whose broad financial condition
improved, more than half of prior borrowers and extra workers no longer
reported those adaptations, but most prior cheaper-product and reduced-use
adopters still did.

**Why it matters:** households do not experience pressure as one stress score.
They shift across consumption, savings, debt, work, timing, and emergency
buffers. An improving headline condition can coexist with continued trade-down
or reduced use, while a former borrower or extra worker may stop that response.
Recovery is therefore partial and currency-specific, not a single return to a
prior baseline.

**Do not overread:** these are weighted descriptive self-reports, not causal
effects or dated bill records. The panel does not identify why conditions or
adaptations changed, whether the household recovered, or how trust, care,
housing, work quality, and political action changed. The next test is a
same-household episode joining price or shock timing to adaptation, reversal,
remaining loss, and later security.

Read the [SHED financial-condition and adaptation persistence layer](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/shed-2024-2025-panel-persistence-layer-v1.md).

## 182. Smartphones can be useful infrastructure and still feel difficult to control

Pew's 2026 survey finds that 53% of adults say they spend too much time on
their smartphone, while 73% say it helps them stay informed and 51% say it
helps them feel connected. Forty-five percent tried to cut back in the prior
year. Among those who tried, 25% described the attempt as extremely or very
successful, 52% as somewhat successful, and 23% as not very or not at all
successful. Younger adults reported more perceived excess, more near-constant
use, and greater sleep concerns.

**Why it matters:** adoption, utility, concern, attempted self-regulation,
successful change, and durable control are different stages. The same device
can provide information and connection while also competing with sleep,
productivity, or the user's sense of agency. Incomplete cutback success is
consistent with dependence or utility trade-offs, but does not prove either.

**Do not overread:** these are self-reports, not logged minutes, app behavior,
clinical sleep, measured productivity, or a causal health estimate. The survey
does not identify notifications, work or school obligations, social pressure,
replacement options, privacy choices, switching, or later civic action. The
next test is a repeated-use record linking exposure, obligation, cutback
attempt, observed behavior, sleep/time displacement, and later switching or
non-use.

Read the [smartphone time, self-control, and well-being layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-smartphone-time-control-wellbeing-layer-v1.md).

## 183. Civic engagement is a set of combinations, not one ladder

Pew's 2025 public-life study grouped people across 19 measures into four
descriptive styles: Mobilizers, active across politics, civic life, news, and
religion; Connectors, broadly involved but less politically active; Spectators,
who follow news with less direct participation; and Outsiders, less involved
across most measured activities. Mobilizers had the highest political interest
(58%), but federal-government trust was low across all four groups, ranging
from 22% among Mobilizers to 17% among Outsiders. Connectors reported the
highest national and local news trust in the comparison while not being the
most politically interested group.

**Why it matters:** political interest, direct action, community participation,
religious attendance, news attention, source trust, and government trust are
different participation currencies. Low institutional trust does not
necessarily mean withdrawal, and low engagement does not reveal whether the
cause is indifference, time, access, information, health, or exclusion.

**Do not overread:** these are one-time analytical clusters, not permanent
identities, causal treatments, or a joined respondent history. The study does
not establish why people entered a group, what material conditions enabled
participation, or whether any style changed policy, trust, or later action. The
next test is a dated information or institutional episode linked to
interpretation, contact or organizing, vote, non-use, and later response.

Read the [public-life engagement groups layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-public-life-engagement-groups-action-layer-v1.md).

## 184. Press freedom can be widely valued while the power to correct information remains contested

Pew's 2026 survey finds that 73% of adults say press freedom is extremely or
very important to society's well-being. Yet 53% prioritize preventing
inaccurate reporting even if that limits press freedom, while 45% prioritize
freedom even if some reporting is inaccurate. Only 28% say the media is
completely free to report the news, and 46% are highly concerned about possible
restrictions.

**Why it matters:** the public is not simply divided into people for or against
the press. People can support press freedom as a principle while accepting
limits when accuracy or national security is invoked. The deeper contest is
which risk deserves protection, who decides what is inaccurate, and whether
government, editors, platforms, or audiences hold corrective authority.

**Do not overread:** these are normative perceptions and a forced-choice trade-
off, not a legal audit of press independence, a measure of actual accuracy, or
evidence of censorship or media effects. They do not show source exposure,
sharing, trust change, platform moderation, subscription switching, or political
action. The next test is a defined information controversy linked to source
choice, perceived accuracy, trust, correction, institutional response, and
later behavior.

Read the [press-freedom limits and public-information norms layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-2026-press-freedom-limits-norms-layer-v1.md).

## 185. An attempted exit can become an engagement event when an AI companion answers the goodbye

A behavioral audit of 1,200 farewells across the most-downloaded AI companion
apps found at least one continuation tactic in 37% of farewells. The reported
tactics included questions that delay departure, curiosity or fear-of-missing-
out hooks, pressure to respond, emotional neglect, ignoring the goodbye, or
language implying restraint. Three preregistered experiments with 3,458 U.S.
adults reported short-run post-goodbye engagement increases of up to 16 times
under experimentally varied farewell responses.

**Why it matters:** the system receives a clear signal at a possible exit
moment and can answer in a way that changes the next interaction. That gives
the platform/attention lane a product-level mechanism between use and exit.
The key question is not merely whether the conversation lasts longer, but
whether the person can still leave with understanding, control, and a workable
alternative.

**Do not overread:** a chat goodbye is not necessarily account deletion, and
longer interaction can reflect curiosity or voluntary continuation. The audit
is not a prevalence estimate for every chatbot, and the experiments do not show
six-month retention, payment, mental-health effects, durable dependence,
unlawful conduct, or successful remedy. The next test is a version-stamped
exit audit followed through deletion, return, payment, switching, human help,
complaint, and later well-being.

Read the [AI companion farewell and exit finding](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-008.md).

## 186. Consumer harm can become visible before recovery and remedy do

FTC, Federal Reserve, and CFPB records observe different moments in the
consumer-harm route. FTC's 2024 Sentinel data contain 2.60 million fraud
reports, with 38% indicating a loss and a $497 median reported loss among loss
reports. The Federal Reserve's 2024 SHED estimates 21% of adults experienced
some financial fraud, about $63 billion in net consumer loss and $21 billion
recovered, and about 30% of non-credit-card fraud experiencers spent at least
10 hours dealing with it. CFPB's 2025 published records more often carry an
explanation or non-monetary-relief label than a monetary-relief label.

**Why it matters:** a loss can become socially legible before it becomes
personally repaired. A complaint, explanation, or published response may help
oversight without proving account correction, money returned, restored access,
or a workable exit. Recovery effort itself consumes time, attention, and
credibility, and recovery capacity can be unequally distributed.

**Do not overread:** these administrative and survey surfaces are not one
numerator, denominator, or person's longitudinal chain. Reported fraud is not
all incidents, complaint labels are not verified remedy rates, and the data do
not show responsible-firm causation, repeat contact, switching, or trust change.
The next test is a case-linked route from originating contact and loss through
recovery attempts, verified correction or compensation, repeat effort, exit,
and later trust.

Read the [digital consumer harm and recourse finding](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-007.md).

## 187. Energy assistance can restore service without restoring household stability

The 2024 Residential Energy Consumption Survey reports 43.56 million homes
with at least one listed energy-insecurity condition. That includes 32.89
million homes that reduced or went without food or medicine to pay energy
costs, 17.55 million with unhealthy temperatures, and 16.19 million receiving
a disconnect or delivery-stop notice. LIHEAP reports millions of households
helped and hundreds of thousands of restoration instances. These are different
units and stages, so they do not show that the same household received help,
was reconnected in time, and stayed stable at the next bill.

**Why it matters:** keeping service on can require sacrificing food, medicine,
comfort, savings, or time. A public program may prevent or restore a loss while
arrears, broken equipment, high energy burden, and the next crisis remain. The
real question is not only whether assistance reached someone, but whether it
created durable room to live safely.

**Do not overread:** a disconnect notice is not a completed shutoff; program
reach is not take-up or adequacy; restoration occurrences are not unique
households or restoration rates; and a following hardship does not prove the
utility caused it. The source layers do not yet provide one privacy-safe
household chain from bill or notice through aid, reconnection, next bill, and
repeat crisis.

Read the [energy insecurity and public restoration finding](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/findings/us-repeat-energy-crises-002.md).

## 188. A shortage label means different things depending on who or what is designated

The 2025 HRSA primary-care file contains geographic, population-based,
facility/site, census-tract, county, low-income, and migrant/seasonal HPSA
components. In a screen of 2,685 counties with both HPSA and CDC PLACES data,
counties with population-based or low-income components had higher median
modeled food, housing, utility, mental-distress, and uninsured measures than
counties with geographic components. The categories overlap, and the
migrant/seasonal group was a counterexample with lower medians on several
measures.

**Why it matters:** “this county is a shortage area” is not one exposure. A
designation may refer to a geography, a named site, a specific population, or
a narrower tract. The county average can therefore be mismatched to the
people or facility the designation is meant to describe. Classification is
part of the cultural and political meaning of shortage: it shapes who is seen,
who is counted, and which remedy appears relevant.

**Do not overread:** these are overlapping, unweighted county medians, not
resident-level access or health outcomes. The result does not establish that
HPSA designation caused the PLACES measures, that a designated site is open or
accepting patients, or that lower modeled need means better care. The next test
is a component-boundary and service-event ledger linking designated population,
provider hours, staffing, appointment, travel, affordability, completed care,
and continuity.

Read the [HPSA component-type and place-need finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-009.md).

## 189. A monetary-policy signal can move expectations without showing who changes spending

Two recent household-expectations studies separate three stages that are often
collapsed. One measures how people interpret a communicated rate decision; a
second measures what they say they would do with spending or a portfolio; and
the missing third is what households actually do after a contract, income, or
asset-price channel changes. The studies find expectation movement and small,
heterogeneous intended-spending responses, but they do not provide one
realized borrower/saver household chain.

**Why it matters:** “rates went down” or “rates went up” is not one household
experience. A variable-rate borrower, depositor, fixed-rate homeowner, renter,
and low-liquidity family can hear the same announcement but face different
costs, timing, and options. Financial literacy, awareness, existing debt, and
cash margin shape whether a policy message becomes a purchase, delay, saving,
borrowing, extra work, or a judgment about economic competence and fairness.

**Do not overread:** a hypothetical response is not a transaction, lower
expected inflation is not lower current prices, and a small average spending
response is not absence of burden. The studies use different rate changes,
samples, designs, and outcomes; they do not establish realized household
welfare, trust, voting, or collective action. The next test is a valid common
unit linking policy awareness, contract exposure, debt or deposits, actual
transactions, work/care response, and later attribution or political action.

Read the [monetary-policy expectations-to-adaptation finding](projects/us-household-monetary-policy/findings/us-household-monetary-policy-003.md).

## 190. Stable recorded hours can hide different earnings movement across household contexts

The retained 2025 SIPP slice follows the same person from one month to the
next. Across resource bands, hours were mostly unchanged, but earnings moved
in both directions more often. In the low-resource cell, people in households
with children had earnings unchanged for 33.3% of valid pairs, compared with
42.1% for people with no children recorded; among SNAP recipients the figure
was 40.8%, compared with 37.3% for no-SNAP pairs. These are descriptive
subgroup patterns, not a SNAP effect.

**Why it matters:** “work stayed stable” can mean hours stayed stable while
pay, job mix, days worked, reporting, schedule quality, or unpaid household
substitution changed. Children and public-benefit context alter the composition
behind the same hours statistic. This is a useful counterexample to treating
employment, benefit receipt, or stable hours as a single measure of household
security or independence.

**Do not overread:** children present is not a dated child-care failure, and
SNAP receipt is not a treatment assignment or measure of adequacy. “Same” means
the recorded value matched across adjacent months, not that pay was sufficient
or the schedule was desired. The panel does not identify the employer, care
event, bill, remedy, trust, political action, or later exit. The next test is a
dated work/care or payment episode with alternatives, schedule control,
institutional response, and later household security.

Read the [SIPP resource, work-direction, children, and SNAP finding](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/findings/us-household-calendar-integration-045.md).

## 191. Local capacity and mobility constraints do not carry one political response

In a same-county context comparison, nominal health-sector capacity and the
share of households without vehicles did not produce one simple pattern of
trust, civic action, or reported voting. The high-capacity/low-zero-vehicle
cell had the lowest federal-trust share, 34.77%, but the highest civic-action
share, 40.90%. The low-capacity/high-zero-vehicle cell had the lowest
civic-action share, 33.99%, while reported voting was high in every cell.

**Why it matters:** a place can have visible providers and mobility constraint
without residents forming one predictable political reaction. Trust, action,
and voting are different currencies, and their meaning depends on service
quality, population change, local history, housing, party composition,
respondent selection, and whether people actually experienced a failed or
successful route. Capacity is a sampling context—not a political outcome.

**Do not overread:** this is a county-context comparison, not a respondent's
service episode and not a causal estimate. It does not show which clinic or
business anyone used, what trip succeeded, who was blamed, or whether an
observed action followed that experience. The next test is a matched-place
episode linking availability, price, hours, wait, alternatives, actual use,
belonging/fairness, attribution, and later action or institutional response.

Read the [local capacity, mobility, trust, and action finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-005.md).

## 192. A national shock can be sharper for a region's households and firms

The New York Fed's April 2026 regional indicators show the Second District
with estimated inflation of 5.0% versus 3.7% nationally, including 9.0%
transportation inflation. Estimated inflation was 1.0 percentage point above
the regional average for Hispanic households and 0.8 points above it for
Black households; lower-income households were 0.53 points above while
high-income households were 0.62 points below. In the regional small-business
survey, profitability was 8 points versus 13 nationally, and the share
expecting employment generation fell 16 points.

**Why it matters:** national averages can hide a local stack of pressure. A
regional transport and price burden can narrow household room at the same time
that firms report weaker revenue, profitability, or employment expectations.
That does not yet prove a household-to-firm causal chain, but it changes the
questions about local demand, services, jobs, place attachment, and political
meaning: whose basket is under pressure, and which firms are losing room to
respond?

**Do not overread:** the regional inflation measure is derived rather than an
official demographic CPI, and the firm survey is weighted but non-random. The
data do not identify an individual fuel bill, local establishment outcome,
household-firm link, worker security, trust, or political response. The next
test is a place-keyed ledger joining price and transport exposure, actual
purchases, firm revenue/employment, service availability, and later household
or political outcomes.

Read the [regional household-and-firm capacity finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-002.md).

## 193. Local capacity is useful only when people can reach an alternative

The county option-stack screen places employer and health-sector presence beside
HPSA designation, rurality, vehicle access, commute modes, housing, wages, and
rent. The high-capacity/high-zero-vehicle cell had more visible health-sector
establishments but also a higher vehicle-constraint context and high HPSA
representation. The low-capacity/low-zero-vehicle cell shows the opposite
counterexample. Neither cell is automatically more accessible.

**Why it matters:** a business or clinic on a county map is only one part of a
usable option. People may need a vehicle, transit, a neighboring county,
affordable prices, accepted insurance, workable hours, language access, or
enough time away from work and care. Capacity, mobility, affordability, and
service completion are different currencies. This is how firm and place
conditions enter household life without assuming that establishment density is
consumer welfare.

**Do not overread:** the screen combines separate county and survey layers and
does not follow a resident to a completed service. Establishments are not
staffed hours, appointments, quality, or use; zero-vehicle share is not
transport failure; and HPSA representation is not a county-wide shortage for
every resident. The next test is one place-period ledger joining capacity,
neighboring alternatives, provider hours, price, travel, attempted and
completed use, unmet need, and later work/care or political outcomes.

Read the [local capacity and reachable-alternative finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-003.md).

## 194. A newer county health vintage can move several measures without creating one local-health trend

The CDC PLACES 2025 release can be compared with the 2024 release for 2,885
common counties, but the measures do not move together. Routine-checkup
estimates rose in the full set and in both low- and high-capacity groups;
lack-of-insurance estimates also rose in both groups. Transportation insecurity
fell modestly, mental distress was nearly flat overall, and food, housing, and
utility insecurity moved differently by group. The same-county comparison
therefore shows measurement movement across several surfaces, not one
improvement or deterioration for the same residents.

**Why it matters:** local institutions and communities often talk about “the
health of a place” as if it were one shared object. In practice, access, need,
insurance, mental distress, food, housing, utility pressure, and routine use
can move on different clocks. A favorable movement in one measure can coexist
with worsening protection elsewhere, and a release change can alter the story
through survey, model, population, geography, or missingness differences.

**Do not overread:** this is not a before/after causal estimate and does not
follow the same people. PLACES vintages use different input years and modeled
surfaces; some social-need measures are available for different county sets.
The result does not show that local capacity caused any movement or that a
county without an HPSA component is adequately served. The next test is a
version-controlled place panel joined to provider hours, insurance, prices,
appointments, completed care, and resident follow-up.

Read the [PLACES vintage and local-capacity comparison](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-008.md).

## 195. Political identity changes the meaning of the same place context

In the county-keyed 2024 CES comparison, federal trust and civic action varied
more sharply across broad party groups than across the four local capacity and
vehicle-constraint cells alone. Democratic-leaning respondents reported higher
federal trust in every displayed cell; independents reported the lowest trust
and civic action. Civic action still varied within party and place, while
reported voting remained high across the cells.

**Why it matters:** a place condition does not enter politics as raw geography.
People interpret the same providers, mobility constraints, and local change
through identity, prior institutional meaning, information, and perceived
responsibility. Trust, action, and voting are not interchangeable endpoints.
This is why a local capacity map cannot by itself predict political withdrawal,
support, or demand for reform.

**Do not overread:** the party grouping is descriptive, not a causal
adjustment. The county cells do not show which respondent used a business,
clinic, transit route, or public system, what happened, who was blamed, or why
someone acted. Small other/unknown cells, survey selection, county context, and
absence of complex-survey standard errors remain limits. The next test is an
event-linked local episode with service experience, attribution, identity,
trust, action, and institutional response.

Read the [political identity and local-capacity context finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-006.md).

## 196. More visible health capacity can coexist with more social need and no routine-care difference

Across 3,072 matched counties, counties in the lowest quartile of visible
health/social-assistance establishments had higher median modeled
transportation, food, housing, utility, mental-distress, and uninsured
measures than counties in the highest quartile. But routine-checkup medians
were nearly the same: 75.8% in the lowest-capacity group and 75.2% in the
highest. Counties with more establishments therefore showed a different social
need profile without a simple “more capacity means more care” result.

**Why it matters:** a hospital, clinic, or health-sector establishment is a
stock measure, not necessarily an accessible primary-care slot. Large systems,
administrative sites, neighboring providers, telehealth, price, insurance,
waits, work schedules, and quality can all intervene. The broader pattern is
that visible institutional capacity and lived social need can coexist; the
meaning of “underserved” cannot be inferred from one map or one outcome.

**Do not overread:** these are unweighted county medians and modeled PLACES
estimates, not patient episodes or causal effects. HPSA components can be
partial-county or population-specific, and the data do not show who attempted
care, waited, used alternatives, or completed treatment. The next test is a
place-period ledger linking provider capacity to hours, insurance acceptance,
appointments, travel, delayed or foregone care, and later health/work effects.

Read the [county service capacity and modeled social-need finding](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-007.md).

## 197. Housing security is split between making the payment and keeping the risk covered

The housing evidence shows two pressures at once: households report rent
arrears, missing homeowners insurance, and premium difficulty, while
California's FAIR Plan residual-market pool grows and place-level voluntary
nonrenewal varies with income, modeled risk, and how those categories are
defined. These are different layers. A public backstop can expand without
showing that coverage is affordable, adequate, renewable, or usable after a
loss.

**Why it matters:** keeping a home is not only a rent or mortgage problem. A
household may make the payment but face rising premiums, a deductible, reduced
limits, nonrenewal, or residual-market exposure. Conversely, an insured home
can remain financially fragile if the policy cannot fund repair. Payment room,
coverage room, claim room, repair room, and the decision to stay or move are
separate currencies.

**Do not overread:** rent arrears are not eviction, an uninsured owner is not
necessarily unable to pay, FAIR Plan growth is not proof of private-market
withdrawal, and a policy count is not adequate coverage or successful claims.
The household surveys, ZIP market data, and public-backstop stocks are not one
panel. The next test is a property/household-year ledger joining payment,
premium, deductible, policy status, hazard, claim, repair, assistance, and
move/stay outcome.

Read the [housing payment, coverage, and mobility finding](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/findings/us-housing-insurance-risk-003.md).

## 198. California insurance-risk patterns can change when the risk definition changes

In a 2022 California ZIP-level screen, a $64,000 income cutoff and a modeled
high/very-high wildfire-exposure cutoff produced a modest low-income/high-risk
nonrenewal gradient: 12.72% versus 10.13% in the high-income/low-risk cell.
When the income cutoff changed to $50,000, low-income/high-risk ZIPs had
12.03% nonrenewal while low-income/low-risk ZIPs had 13.16%. The stronger
finding is therefore sensitivity, not a stable universal gradient.

**Why it matters:** risk categories are not neutral containers. Income measure,
modeled hazard, ZIP-to-ZCTA assignment, insurer mix, regulation, mitigation,
property type, and timing can change the apparent pattern. A market story can
look like a clean class-by-hazard relationship until the denominator and
classification choices are tested. This matters culturally and politically
because fragile patterns can become claims about who is being abandoned.

**Do not overread:** these are unweighted ZIP-level renewal decisions, not
household or property transitions. Nonrenewal does not show replacement
coverage, affordability, claim success, repair, sale, or displacement. FAIR Plan
share is a separate backstop measure, not the same denominator. The next test
is a pre-specified multi-cut-point property/year panel with premiums,
deductibles, policy status, hazard, mitigation, claims, repairs, resources,
and move/stay outcomes.

Read the [California insurance nonrenewal sensitivity finding](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/findings/us-housing-insurance-risk-002.md).

## 199. A product recall can move safety work and proof onto the consumer

Current CPSC notices show that a safety intervention may require consumers to
stop using a product, register with the firm, destroy or dispose of it, upload
photographic proof, and then wait for a refund, replacement, credit, or repair.
One sauna notice also says an earlier repair was ineffective and offers a new
professional repair. The notice establishes an institutional warning and a
remedy design; it does not show that an affected consumer completed the route,
received the remedy, or became safe.

**Why it matters:** a nominal “full refund” can carry a time, documentation,
disposal, connectivity, transport, replacement, and safety cost before money
arrives. Proof requirements may protect firms from false claims while also
excluding people who lack a camera, safe disposal route, time, language access,
or an easy interface. Remedy access is therefore part of consumer safety, not
an administrative detail after the recall.

**Do not overread:** recalled units are not exposed households, a remedy offer
is not receipt, and a complaint or uploaded photo is not verified safe
restoration. The public notices do not show awareness, completion, residual
cost, repeat failure, trust, or exit. The next test is a product-level ledger
from hazard and notice through consumer contact, required proof, disposal or
repair, payment receipt, safety verification, repeat contact, and later use or
switching.

Read the [CPSC recall-remedy burden finding](projects/us-marketplace-product-safety/findings/us-product-recall-response-002.md).

## 200. Recurring payment can turn inattention into a consumer-exit problem

The FTC's Shutterstock case alleges that renewal terms were not clearly
disclosed, some consumers were charged without express informed consent, and
cancellation was difficult; the proposed settlement includes $35 million in
consumer relief and simpler cancellation rules. The FTC's amended Uber One
case gives a different service context—rides and food delivery—and reports
that some users could face as many as 23 screens and 32 actions to cancel.
These are case allegations and proposed controls, not a prevalence estimate.

**Why it matters:** three states must remain separate: an account renewed, the
customer did not want the charge, and the customer tried but could not exit
without material time, money, access, or support cost. Automatic renewal can
also be useful when it preserves a service the customer knowingly wants. The
consumer problem is therefore not “subscriptions are bad”; it is whether
attention, consent, monitoring, and exit are distributed fairly enough for
choice to remain real.

**Do not overread:** the cases do not show how many customers encountered the
flow, how many canceled, what savings they received, whether refunds arrived,
or whether anyone switched. The proposed remedy does not prove implementation,
receipt, or recurrence prevention. The next test is an account-level episode
linking plan terms, notice, intent, renewal, attempted cancellation, realized
charge, relief, continued use, switching, and later household room.

Read the [recurring-payment and practical-exit finding](projects/us-subscription-inattention/findings/us-subscription-inattention-001.md).

## 201. A free platform still has a time price

Two field experiments on Facebook and Instagram study demand for time-intensive
digital goods. The research treats ad load as a user cost, finds relatively
low user response to changes in that cost, and shows that a person's time
share and time cost influence which online or offline activities can substitute
for the platform. A possible platform separation is modeled, not observed as
a policy result.

**Why it matters:** a platform can be free in money while charging minutes,
attention, interruption, or foregone alternatives. Those minutes may be valued
connection or leisure, or they may crowd out sleep, care, work, or a preferred
activity. Low response to ad load can mean value, habit, limited substitutes,
or tolerable ads; continued use is not proof of satisfaction or free choice.

**Do not overread:** the evidence does not show household welfare, care or
sleep displacement, durable dependence, successful switching, or realized
competition effects. Time spent is not automatically harm, and a modeled
de-merger is not an observed outcome. The next test is a consented time-use
panel linking platform/version, ad load, purpose, active minutes, offline
substitute, work/care/sleep context, accessibility, switching, and later
perceived benefit or burden.

Read the [time-intensive platform-consumption finding](projects/us-time-intensive-platform-consumption/findings/us-time-intensive-platform-consumption-001.md).

## 202. Recovery progress does not settle whether relief feels reachable or fair

In a KFF/Episcopal Health Foundation follow-up survey across 24 heavily
impacted Texas Gulf Coast counties after Hurricane Harvey, 58% reported
property, vehicle, or household job/hour/income loss. Nearly a year later, 70%
of affected residents said life was largely or almost back to normal, but 30%
still said it was disrupted and 42% said they were not getting the help they
needed. Only about one in five knew of federal long-term recovery funding; 54%
of all residents were confident relief money benefited those who needed it
most, while 42% were not confident.

**Why it matters:** recovery has a material clock and a legitimacy clock. A
community can report broad progress while a large minority remains disrupted,
under-helped, or unsure who controls relief. “Back to normal” is not the same
as durable housing, health, work, or financial recovery; aid confidence is not
receipt; and knowing a public-input route exists is not participation. These
judgments shape preparedness, trust, and later demands even when the actual
causal path is not observed.

**Do not overread:** this is a retrospective regional survey, not a linked aid
application or payment panel. It does not show eligibility, approval, amount,
delay, receipt, attribution, voting, or later exit for the same resident. The
next test is a lawful person-level recovery ledger linking damage, aid route,
payment, housing/work/health status, perceived adequacy and fairness, and later
public action.

Read the [Harvey recovery, aid reachability, and legitimacy finding](projects/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity/findings/us-harvey-recovery-aid-legitimacy-001.md).

## 203. Financial recovery, health, and care do not move on one schedule

The Federal Reserve's 2024–2025 SHED recontact panel shows that financial
condition, self-rated health, and unpaid adult-care responsibility move
together only imperfectly. Among respondents whose financial condition
worsened, 18.8% reported worse health, 69.0% unchanged health, and 12.2%
improved health. Among those whose financial condition improved, 16.5%
reported worse health, 68.8% unchanged health, and 14.6% improved health.
Adult-care entry was 10.6% on the worsened financial path, 6.5% on the stable
path, and 7.5% on the improved path.

**Why it matters:** an improving money category does not mean that health,
family care, work time, or household coordination recovered at the same time.
Likewise, financial worsening does not determine one uniform health outcome.
People may borrow, work more, use family help, reduce consumption, or absorb a
new care role, and those adjustments can protect one currency while worsening
another. Recovery is therefore a multi-clock experience, not a single upward
or downward household score.

**Do not overread:** this is a weighted same-respondent descriptive panel, not
a causal financial-shock study. Care entry and exit are separate universes and
do not measure hours, intensity, recipient outcome, or schedule control. The
panel does not identify a dated bill, employer, insurer, remedy, trust change,
political action, or later exit. The next test is a dated financial or care
event linked to hours, coverage, debt, family support, health, work, and later
security for the same person or household.

Read the [SHED financial recovery, health, and care path finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-002.md).

## 204. A larger public card-offer surface does not mean cheaper credit for households

CFPB Terms of Credit Card Plans files show more reported product rows and
named institutions in the second half of 2025 than in the second half of 2024.
The median reported purchase APR among numeric product rows fell from 25.74%
to 24.49%, but rows marked as having credit-tier APR variation rose from 346
to 396; the median annual fee remained $95. This is a change in the visible
offer surface, not evidence that the same borrower received cheaper credit.

**Why it matters:** formal choice can expand while practical choice stays
unequal. More products can reflect issuer participation, naming, missingness,
or promotional mix. A lower product-row median can coexist with high assigned
APRs for credit-constrained borrowers, rejected applicants, fees, or terms that
are impossible to use safely. The household experiences eligibility, assigned
terms, balance, payment, liquidity, and exit—not the public spreadsheet alone.

**Do not overread:** the files do not link products over time, weight offers by
accounts or approvals, or measure APR and fees actually paid. Household
borrowing and delayed purchases are not attributable to these offers without
timing and account linkage. The next test is a lawful borrower/product ledger
joining approval, credit tier, realized APR and fees, balance, payment,
liquidity, complaint, and switching or exit.

Read the [credit-card offer-surface and household-access finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-004.md).

## 205. Aggregate credit growth and household hardship are related layers, not the same trend

The New York Fed's 2026 Q2 household-debt snapshot reports $18.8 trillion in
US household debt, including $1.26 trillion in credit-card balances, with 4.7%
of outstanding debt in some stage of delinquency. In the Federal Reserve's
2025 SHED linked-credit sample, average card balances rose $748 (11%) from
2023 to 2025; the increase was $2,530 (37%) among respondents who said they
were finding it difficult to get by, versus $59 (1%) among those living
comfortably.

**Why it matters:** the national credit picture and lived household pressure
can move together without being the same story. Aggregate growth may reflect
prices, purchases, access, or distress. A hardship-linked balance increase may
be temporary smoothing, a payment problem, or something else. The consumer
experience sits in the missing middle: assigned terms, payment, liquidity,
care/work tradeoffs, and whether the household can exit safely.

**Do not overread:** these are an aggregate credit-record snapshot, a survey,
and a consent-based linked subgroup—not a common denominator or causal borrower
panel. They do not show that hardship caused the balance increase, that rates
caused hardship, or what happened next to repayment, care, trust, or politics.
The next test is a lawful borrower-level design linking rate, balance, payment,
delinquency, spending, care/work, complaint, remedy, and later security.

Read the [aggregate-credit and household-hardship finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-005.md).

## 206. Utility hardship and credit use reveal unequal financial escape routes

In a 2025 Census SIPP person-record/month diagnostic, 35.21% of rows
reporting utility-payment difficulty carried a credit or store-card balance,
compared with 26.90% of rows without utility difficulty. Savings ownership
moved in the opposite direction: 46.44% among utility-difficulty rows versus
64.90% among rows without that difficulty. Inside the hardship group,
balance-carrying rose from 22.35% below 1.0 times the poverty line to 44.75%
at 4.0 times or more, while savings ownership rose from 29.33% to 61.34%.

**Why it matters:** needing credit does not mean having equal access to it.
Some households preserve cash by carrying a balance; others face the same bill
pressure with less savings and less usable revolving credit. The consumer
choice is therefore not simply “borrow or do not borrow.” It can be whether a
household can keep service on, absorb a fee, delay another purchase, or avoid
an even harsher tradeoff.

**Do not overread:** these are person-record/month co-occurrences, not household
prevalence or causal timing. Carrying a balance can be ordinary use, smoothing,
or repayment strain; savings ownership does not reveal the balance or liquidity.
The next test is an adjacent-month household design linking utility difficulty
to balance, savings, food/housing hardship, work, restoration, and repayment.

Read the [utility hardship, credit, and savings finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-006.md).

## 207. Aggregate income growth does not identify household financial room

BEA's July 2026 Personal Income and Outlays release reported increases in
personal income, disposable income, consumption, and outlays, alongside a
$712.0 billion aggregate personal-saving level and a 3.0% personal saving
rate. Services spending rose $86.2 billion while goods spending fell $49.9
billion. Those national flows describe the economic environment, but they do
not show which households gained liquid room, faced higher essential costs,
borrowed to maintain consumption, or delayed purchases.

**Why it matters:** a strong-looking national consumer signal can coexist with
very different lived realities. Services growth may include housing or medical
costs rather than discretionary choice; a saving rate is not an emergency-fund
distribution; and aggregate PCE cannot tell whether a household substituted,
cut quantity, used credit, or simply could not buy. Consumer culture is shaped
by that unequal room to choose, not by the national total alone.

**Do not overread:** this is a dated aggregate macro snapshot, not a household
panel or causal explanation. It does not identify prices, debt service,
benefits, buffers, product terms, remedy, trust, or political response for the
same people. The next test is a distributional same-unit bridge from income or
benefit change through price/access, spending or saving response, work/care
consequences, and later switching, remedy, or security.

Read the [aggregate income and household-room finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-007.md).

## 208. Low unemployment did not prevent consumer sentiment from falling

From January 2024 to July 2026, the University of Michigan consumer-sentiment
index fell from 79.0 to 55.2, while the CPI index rose 7.5% and the civilian
unemployment rate remained relatively low, moving from 3.7% to 4.1%. Sentiment
also reached a low of 52.9 in December 2025 before partially recovering to
55.2 in July 2026. The co-movement is a timing contrast, not a causal estimate.

**Why it matters:** employment can look stable while people feel less secure.
Public economic meaning includes expectations, fixed costs, political
interpretation, housing or insurance changes, debt, and the buffer available
for the next shock. A low unemployment rate therefore cannot stand in for
consumer confidence, affordability, fairness, or a sense of future control.

**Do not overread:** this is a simple aggregate time-series comparison. It does
not identify which people carried the price increase, whether sentiment changes
caused spending or political action, or how expectations, partisanship, and
household exposure interacted. The next test is a linked person or household
panel joining price and labor exposure to perceived room, confidence, blame,
trust, action, and later security.

Read the [employment, prices, and consumer-sentiment finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-012.md).

## 209. Insurance complaint recovery is not the same as keeping a home repairable

New York's 2025 Department of Financial Services record reports 39,443
insurance complaints received, 39,064 closed, and more than $121 million in
reported recovery for consumers and providers. In the combined property-casualty
and service-contract category, 2,869 complaints were closed, 498 were labeled
positive consumer outcomes, and reported recovery was about $17.95 million.

**Why it matters:** a complaint route makes institutional recourse visible, but
its administrative success is not the same as housing security. A recovered
amount may settle a disputed fee without funding a full repair; a closed case
may clarify a nonrenewal without producing replacement coverage. The household
still has to live with repairability, deductibles, debt, temporary housing,
care/work disruption, and the choice to stay, move, or sell.

**Do not overread:** these are agency-level totals and mixed categories, not a
homeowners-claim denominator or recovery rate. “Closed,” “positive outcome,”
and reported recovery do not identify individual receipt, adequacy, completed
repair, renewed coverage, trust, or displacement. The next test is a lawful
claim-level ledger linking complaint, payment, repair, coverage continuity,
remaining loss, and later housing choice.

Read the [insurance complaint and housing-recourse finding](projects/us-housing-insurance-risk/findings/us-housing-insurance-risk-004.md).

## 210. A shortage database can contain operational detail without being a capacity ledger

The current HRSA primary-care HPSA file contains 20,905 designated rows across
2,888 counties. A score is populated for every row, but FTE and shortage fields
are populated for only 16,582 rows, and estimated underserved population is
populated for 16,039. The facility/site category is especially incomplete:
FTE is available for only 543 of 4,866 rows, and the downloaded file does not
provide the same operational fields uniformly across designation types.

**Why it matters:** missing administrative detail is not the same as zero
capacity. A shortage file can look precise while the fields needed to infer
staffing, underserved population, or service scale are conditional on what was
designated and how it was recorded. That affects who appears underserved, how
local leaders interpret the problem, and which remedy seems measurable.

**Do not overread:** rows and counties overlap, so they cannot be summed into a
national provider or patient denominator. A score, FTE, or shortage value is
not an appointment, active hour, insurance acceptance, travel time, quality,
or completed care. The next test is a type-specific data-quality and service
ledger joining designation, staffing, hours, appointment, travel, price,
completed care, and continuity.

Read the [HPSA operational-field audit](projects/us-local-business-place/findings/us-local-business-place-010.md).

## 211. Upstream price pressure is a transmission surface, not automatic consumer pass-through

The BLS August 2026 Producer Price Index reports final-demand prices up 0.4%
month over month and 5.4% year over year. Final-demand goods rose 1.1%,
services 0.1%, and energy 4.2%; diesel fuel rose 24.1% in the detailed product
indexes. These are seller-side and intermediate-stage movements, not a direct
measure of what a household paid or what a firm kept as margin.

**Why it matters:** the same upstream shock can be absorbed by a firm's margin,
passed through later, offset by a contract or hedge, met through substitution,
or concentrated in a supply chain a household never uses. The consumer-facing
experience depends on product exposure, timing, competition, inventory,
quality, quantity, and alternatives. This is where firm power and consumer
choice meet.

**Do not overread:** PPI and CPI use different baskets and stages. The release
does not identify a named firm's cost, retail price, household burden, margin,
purchase, substitution, political response, or recovery. The next test is a
product- or sector-level ledger linking upstream price, contract or margin,
retail exposure, household response, and later firm or consumer exit.

Read the [producer-price and firm-to-consumer transmission finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-009.md).

## 212. Financial improvement does not automatically end the adaptations that protected the household

In a recontacted 2024–2025 Federal Reserve SHED panel, reported adaptations
persisted more often among respondents whose financial condition worsened than
among those whose condition improved. Among prior adopters, the 2025 persistence
shares for worsened versus improved paths were 48.1% versus 41.2% for working
more or getting another job, 65.1% versus 45.0% for increased borrowing, and
84.8% versus 66.1% for using less or stopping use.

**Why it matters:** a household can report financial improvement while still
working extra, carrying borrowing, or cutting use. Those adaptations may have
prevented an immediate failure, but they can continue as hidden costs in time,
quality, health, mobility, care, or future flexibility. “Better financial
condition” is therefore not the same as restored room or released pressure.

**Do not overread:** these are weighted same-respondent descriptive persistence
shares among prior adopters, not a causal shock study or a combined stress
index. The panel does not identify the dated trigger, adaptation cost, remedy,
schedule control, care intensity, trust, political action, or later exit. The
next test is a dated financial event linked to hours, debt service, use quality,
care/work consequences, and durable recovery for the same household.

Read the [financial-condition adaptation-persistence finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-011.md).

## 213. Income, outside help, and money left over are different kinds of household room

The Federal Reserve's 2025 SHED shows that household security is built from
several non-equivalent supports. Thirty percent of adults reported income
varying at least occasionally, and 11% said that variation made paying bills
difficult. Twenty-three percent received help with a listed expense. Only 41%
said money was left over always or often; that share was 19% among adults below
$25,000 in family income and 59% at $100,000 or more.

**Why it matters:** income can arrive at the wrong time, outside help can be
temporary or inadequate, and a household can keep current consumption while
having no budget margin. A national income number or a receipt measure misses
the cultural experience of dependence, timing risk, and whether there is room
to absorb the next bill without borrowing, cutting use, or asking for help.

The age-conditioned table makes the social infrastructure visible: 47% of
adults ages 18–29 received help with at least one listed expense, including
phone bills, general costs, housing, cars, medical expenses, and education.
That is not simply “family resilience”; it is a practical payment and access
route whose amount, adequacy, reciprocity, stigma, and durability are not
observed.

**Do not overread:** these are overlapping self-reports, not a dated bill,
liquid-balance measure, or causal estimate. The survey does not identify the
amount or adequacy of outside help, the source of income variation, the expense
protected, or later recovery, trust, or political action. The next test is a
same-household time-and-money ledger linking income timing, assistance, bills,
adaptation, and later room.

Read the [income timing, outside help, and budget-margin finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-013.md).

## 214. A dated health event is not yet a household recovery episode

The retained 2024 MEPS staged event ledger contains 18,457 privacy-minimized
person/event rows. Every row has a hashed episode identifier and a month-
precision first-event date, with round-level context for payment, coverage,
health, employment, bills, debt, collectors, denials, and care delay. The field
audit finds no event-specific alternative, choice, or verified remedy, and
later round fields remain context rather than an event-specific recovery result.

**Why it matters:** a dated event is a real improvement over an annual hardship
rate, but it still does not show how a person or household paid, substituted,
appealed, delayed care, protected work, or recovered. This is the missing middle
between health exposure and social meaning: the event may be time-ordered while
the decision, responsible institution, remedy, and household consequence remain
unseen.

**Do not overread:** round-level payment is not the event's amount due or
payment completion; a denial flag is not a dated decision or appeal; later
health is not verified recovery; and person linkage is not household-payer or
caregiver linkage. Missing alternatives or remedies cannot be coded as no
alternative or failed remedy. The next test needs a lawful event-linked record
with need, alternatives, effort, payment, response, correction, and later
durability.

Read the [MEPS staged-event field-availability finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-013.md).

## 215. Recorded medical payment is not a monotonic measure of household burden

In a strict month-ordered MEPS screen, first emergency-room and inpatient
events were grouped by recorded self/family payment. The zero-payment acute
event groups had worse baseline and follow-up perceived health than the $100+
groups: for emergency-room events, fair/poor follow-up health was 30.34% at
$0 versus 17.81% at $100+; for inpatient events it was 36.08% versus 25.34%.
Yet the emergency-room $100+ group had more reported follow-up bill problems
(18.11% versus 14.28%).

**Why it matters:** “paid nothing” is not the same as “experienced no burden,”
and a larger recorded payment is not a complete household affordability
measure. Insurance, public coverage, payer mix, severity, prior health,
facility/professional charges, debt, and ability to pay can sort people into
different payment bands. A zero-payment household may still have worse health
or less financial room, while a high-payment household may have more resources
or more severe medical debt.

**Do not overread:** payment is an event-family field, not the complete bill or
household obligation; the event-window groups are selected and some cells are
small. Later bill, health, debt, and care-delay fields are broader round context
and do not establish that the event or payment caused them. The next test needs
bill/claim identity, payer and obligation timing, alternatives, treatment
continuity, remedy, and later household recovery for the same episode.

Read the [MEPS event-payment and later-context finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-004.md).

## 216. Month ordering sharpens a health-event comparison without proving recovery

The 2024 MEPS files allow a bounded screen that places each person's first
office, emergency-room, or inpatient event before, in, or after the R4/2
reference-period endpoint month. The timing groups occupy different health,
employment, and bill contexts: for example, inpatient events before the
endpoint had 34.61% fair/poor health and 58.29% not employed, while same-month
events had 41.19% fair/poor health and 73.55% not employed. Emergency-room
before-event records had 14.50% bill problems versus 6.97% for after-event
records.

**Why it matters:** “later” is not automatically a post-event outcome. An
after-endpoint event describes context before the event, a same-month event has
ambiguous ordering, and a before-endpoint event may reflect pre-existing need,
severity, coverage, or selection. Month ordering improves the design while
preserving the possibility that the event is a marker rather than the cause.

**Do not overread:** event months are not event days, reference periods can
overlap the event, and the comparison is descriptive. It does not link the
event to a bill or claim, alternative, care decision, remedy, household
adaptation, trust, or exit. The next test needs a dated need/bill, coverage and
alternatives, care decision, protected or sacrificed outcome, and follow-up
remedy for the same unit.

Read the [month-ordered MEPS event-follow-up finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-005.md).

## 217. Acute care events can mark prior need before they mark later household burden

In a strict month-ordered MEPS comparison, people with an emergency-room event
between the R3/1 and R4/2 endpoints already had 24.29% fair/poor health at
baseline, compared with 11.01% among people without an ER event in that window.
The inpatient contrast was 28.99% versus 11.27%. At the later endpoint, fair/poor
health was 27.05% versus 9.66% for ER and 33.67% versus 10.00% for inpatient;
bill problems were also higher. Office events were a counterexample, with
similar or lower health and bill-problem shares than the no-office-event group.

**Why it matters:** a later health or bill difference around an acute event may
reflect pre-existing illness, severity, age, coverage, access, or employment
selection rather than the event causing the outcome. The event is still useful
for locating a high-need population, but utilization is not automatically a
shock and later hardship is not automatically its consequence.

**Do not overread:** this is a same-person, month-ordered descriptive comparison
with selected event groups, not a causal estimate. The files do not identify
the initiating need, feasible alternative, bill or claim, care decision,
payment timing, remedy, trust, or exit. The next test needs a dated need or
obligation and a comparable non-event or pre-event design with documented
alternatives and follow-up outcomes.

Read the [MEPS acute-event selection finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-003.md).

## 218. Institutional friction appears across care channels before its remedy is visible

In the 2024 MEPS person/event screen, people reporting insurance denial or
prior-authorization delay had higher same-round care delay, medical debt, and
bill-problem shares across office, emergency-room, and inpatient event groups.
For example, ER-event respondents with denial/delay versus no denial/delay
reported care delay at 18.99% versus 7.67%, medical debt at 27.10% versus
19.06%, and bill problems at 25.24% versus 9.16%. Among privately covered
first-event respondents, the care-delay contrast remained visible across all
three channels.

The stricter inter-round recheck sharpens the same mechanism. Among first
office-event respondents, denial/delay was accompanied by 15.62% care delay,
27.31% medical debt, and 22.01% collector contact, versus 6.25%, 14.58%, and
10.55% without denial/delay. The corresponding ER figures were 22.30%,
32.06%, and 30.58%, versus 6.16%, 18.84%, and 14.80%; inpatient cells point in
the same direction but are smaller. The contrast remained visible among people
with any private coverage, and was generally larger among people not confident
they could pay an unexpected expense. This makes practical room a conditioning
surface rather than treating coverage as a complete protection variable.

**Why it matters:** institutional friction is not only an administrative detail
at one care gate. It can sit alongside delayed care, debt, collections, and
bill stress across different kinds of use. The pattern makes the practical
burden visible while still leaving open whether the friction caused it, whether
complex illness produced both, and whether a person had a usable appeal or
alternative.

**Do not overread:** `EQDENY53` is an annual denial/delay indicator, not a claim
ID, decision date, appeal, response, or resolution. Event groups are selected,
outcomes are same-round context, and coverage is not plan generosity or liquid
room. The next test needs dated denial, appeal effort, response, payment, care
completion, remedy, and later household or institutional outcomes for the same
case.

Read the [MEPS cross-channel institutional-friction finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-006.md)
and the [strict inter-round friction-cascade audit](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/meps-dated-friction-cascade-v1.md).

## 219. Formal workforce access can fail before employment recovery

In a GAO review of selected Department of Labor workforce programs, 7% of
participants reported a disability, and 43% of those participants did not
report a disability type. In the Adult program, 33% of participants reporting
disabilities used training services, compared with 47% of participants
reporting no disability. After exit, second-quarter employment was 60% for
participants reporting disabilities versus 76% for those reporting none; among
those employed, median second-quarter earnings were $7,048 versus $8,872.

**Why it matters:** formal eligibility, a job center, assistive technology,
and a monitoring system do not automatically become usable intake, completed
training, placement, or durable work. The review also found selected local
problems with interpreters, inaccessible training outside job centers, and
monitoring that was not routinely analyzed in aggregate. This puts disability
access in the same broader atlas frame as health, care, public benefits, and
work: institutional capacity is experienced at the interface, where the person
may have to absorb interpretation, transport, technology, and disclosure costs.

**Do not overread:** the participant comparison is descriptive, the 12 visited
job centers are not representative, and missing disability type is not proof
of institutional failure. The data do not establish that inaccessible services
caused the employment or earnings gap, nor do they show accommodation receipt,
service completion, retention, household security, trust, or exit. The next
test needs a voluntary participant-level route linking accommodation request
and receipt, service completion, placement, retention, wages/hours, correction,
and later household security.

Read the [disability workforce-access finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-015.md).

## 220. Working and relying on public benefits can coexist in the same labor system

GAO estimates that 13.8 million adults ages 19–64 enrolled in Medicaid and
10.6 million adults ages 19–64 living in SNAP-receiving households worked at
some point in 2024. About two-thirds of the working adults in each group
worked at least 35 hours per week, and 88% of wage-earning adults in both
groups worked in the private sector. A separate selected-state employer
analysis identified 46 companies that appeared among the top 25 employers of
Medicaid enrollees or SNAP beneficiaries in at least two states; 17 were among
the 50 largest Fortune 500 companies by employee count.

**Why it matters:** “working” and “using a safety net” are not opposite
states. Public support can protect food or health while people remain in paid
work, and the pattern raises a firm–state question: who bears the cost of
health, food, scheduling, and income instability when a large private employer
and a public program occupy the same household system? This is a structural
labor-and-consumer insight, not a claim that employers deliberately shifted
costs.

**Do not overread:** “worked at some point” is not stable employment, adequate
pay, predictable scheduling, or continuous coverage. SNAP is measured at the
household level, and the selected employer data are not a national ranking or
per-worker incidence estimate. The evidence does not identify wages, hours,
benefit churn, employer intent, alternatives, household adequacy, trust, or
exit. The next test needs a state-year worker–household–employer ledger linking
wages, hours, schedule predictability, employer benefits, Medicaid/SNAP status,
benefit amounts, household need, and later security or exit.

Read the [working adults and public-benefit/employer concentration finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-017.md).

## 221. A public complaint database can show routing without showing consumer power

In a bounded CFPB Consumer Complaint Database probe, the same credit-card,
monetary-relief, date-limited query returned 21,425 matching records with or
without the public `has_narrative=true` filter, and the same capped set of 25
records. The response exposed administrative routing and response fields, but
did not provide complaint narrative text or a usable narrative-presence field.

**Why it matters:** a public record can make institutional activity visible
without making the consumer's lived route visible. A label such as `Closed with
monetary relief` can establish an administrative response category, but it does
not establish that a particular person received money, corrected an account,
found an alternative, trusted the provider, or switched. This is a useful
cross-domain warning: measurement architecture itself can privilege the
institution's clock over the affected person's clock.

**Do not overread:** the missing narrative in this API response does not mean
consumers lacked narratives, and the probe is not a claim about all CFPB data
or all complaints. The next test needs a lawful consented or account-level
follow-up linking the problem, attempted alternatives, institutional response,
verified remedy, repeat effort, and later switching or exit for the same case.
