Read the [CFPB public complaint narrative-filter finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-028.md).

## 222. A completed back-pay process can repair assessed loss without restoring the missed opportunity

The Justice Department reported that an $18.25 million Apple back-pay fund had
been processed and exhausted for U.S. workers harmed by recruitment practices
covered by Apple's 2023 PERM settlement. DOJ said the claims process
individually assessed thousands of potential claimants and lost income. The
settlement also required external and electronic recruitment, searchable
applications, training, and three years of monitoring.

**Why it matters:** this is stronger than a reserved fund or a compensation
order: the public record reports an individualized payment process reaching its
administrative completion stage. But money for assessed lost income is not the
same as restoring a missed interview, job, career path, status, or bargaining
position. The case gives the atlas a useful remedy comparator across work,
consumer recourse, and institutional legitimacy.

**Do not overread:** “thousands of potential claimants” is not a complete
denominator, and the public update is not an independent worker-level payment
audit. It does not show each worker's amount or timing, restored job
consideration, post-monitoring compliance, household recovery, trust,
organizing, or exit. It also does not establish that an AI system generated or
ranked the applications. The next test needs claimant-level payment timing,
application-channel evidence, later consideration or employment, recurrence,
and worker-reported recovery where lawfully available.

Read the [Apple PERM back-pay implementation finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-086.md).

## 223. Payment distribution can be visible without restoring worker power

The FTC reports that more than $60.6 million was returned through earlier
payment rounds to eligible Amazon Flex drivers after the agency alleged that
customer tips had been withheld. The current FTC route sends Zelle payments to
eligible drivers who did not cash earlier checks. The underlying consent order
required about $61.7 million in driver compensation and changes to pay
representations and tip-treatment consent.

**Why it matters:** this is stronger than a compensation promise or order
alone: an administrator reports actual distribution rounds and a reissue path.
But distribution is not the same as every driver's receipt, correction of the
remaining loss, continued work, alternative work, bargaining power, household
recovery, trust, or exit. The case joins consumer-facing promises to worker
income and shows how one platform can sit on both sides of a remedy chain.

**Do not overread:** the aggregate amount is not a worker-level recovery rate
or average payment. The public record does not provide a complete eligible
denominator, individual payment timing, failed-delivery outcome, or later work
and household evidence. The next test needs a lawful de-identified ledger with
amount owed, payment status/date/channel, reissue or uncashed status,
continued platform work, alternatives, and later security or collective action.

Read the [Amazon Flex tip-refund implementation finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-087.md).

## 224. Geopolitical investment promises can carry local care and supplier commitments before benefits are realized

Commerce reported that a U.S.–Taiwan agreement would drive at least $250
billion in Taiwanese semiconductor and technology investment in U.S. advanced
semiconductor, energy, and AI capacity. A later TSMC update reported $265
billion in planned U.S. investment across twelve planned leading-edge and
packaging facilities. The Arizona project supplies a partial realization layer:
TSMC reports one fab in high-volume N4 production, more than 3,000 employees,
later fabs at different construction stages, and a 43-person technician cohort.

**Why it matters:** geopolitical dependence is being translated into a domestic
capacity promise that reaches beyond factories. The community plan includes a
$1,800-per-child annual childcare subsidy, provider discounts and extended
hours, care-navigation support, and supplier-diversity commitments for local
businesses. This connects state strategy to household time, care, labor, and
small-firm opportunity. It also shows that “domestic capacity” is not one
thing: production, ownership, workforce quality, local burden, and household
benefit move on different clocks.

**Do not overread:** planned investment is not disbursed investment, and one
operating fab is not proof of total output, yield, replaceability, lower prices,
or reduced Taiwan-centered dependence. The care and supplier measures are
commitments, not evidence of take-up, occupied seats, realized contracts,
worker retention, or evenly shared gains. The next test needs project-level
production, utility, workforce, childcare, procurement, ownership, and local
incidence records, compared with a delayed or cancelled commitment.

Read the [Taiwan-linked semiconductor capacity finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-090.md).

## 225. Sovereignty language can advance before domestic defense capability is realized

In the Barracuda-500M case, Polish public records describe a cooperation
agreement for assembly followed by production, technology and know-how
transfer, staged Polish and European supplier localization, and working groups
preparing implementation arrangements. A Polish security statement at the
MSPO defense-industry event framed durable domestic industrial and
technological competence as a basis for strategic resilience, independent
crisis action, and sovereignty while also emphasizing allied solidarity.

**Why it matters:** a geopolitical capability can acquire cultural and
political meaning before it becomes an operational asset. The sovereignty
frame may shape public justification, alliance identity, and expectations of
independence, while the production chain still depends on a future order,
licensed know-how, commissioned capacity, completed units, maintenance, and
external inputs. This connects state capability to legitimacy and national
identity without collapsing official framing into public acceptance.

**Do not overread:** the reviewed public record did not report first assembly,
commissioned production, accepted quantity, delivery, operational use, local
content, or resident/worker/voter reception. A $4 billion U.S. Foreign
Military Financing agreement is a separate financing signal and is not evidence
that Barracuda production was funded. The next test needs an identifier-bearing
purchase, plant, output, acceptance, workforce, maintenance, and public or
external-response record.

Read the [Barracuda-500M implementation-stage finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-043.md).

## 226. Substitute earnings can be visible without proving practical recovery

In the Fair Work Commission's Mansoor v. Rasier decision, a platform worker
deactivated from Uber's Driver Platform had about $9,000 in new Baysec earnings
and an estimated $1,760 increase in Didi earnings during the interruption. The
Commission deducted those substitute earnings when calculating a $6,073.23
gross lost-earnings order and also ordered reactivation.

**Why it matters:** this is a rare same-case bridge where an alternative income
route is observed rather than assumed. It makes practical optionality more
concrete: the worker did something else while contesting the exclusion. But
substitute earnings do not prove the work was freely chosen, equivalent in
conditions, sufficient for household needs, or a durable alternative. The
formal order also does not establish payment receipt, post-order access, trust,
or exit.

**Do not overread:** the decision does not establish net income, hours, risk,
control, benefits, travel, or whether Baysec and Didi were acceptable choices.
The lost-earnings order is not a payment record. The next test needs net
substitute earnings and conditions, order implementation, continued access,
household security, and later use, switching, non-use, trust, or exit.

Read the [Mansoor substitute-earnings and remedy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-099.md).

## 227. An individual employment remedy can be reported without closing recovery

FY2026 summaries from the Justice Department's Immigrant and Employee Rights
Section report several charging-party resolutions in which an individual
payment followed an employment or employment-verification dispute. The
reported paid amounts include $5,600, $16,730, $30,000, and $8,000. A separate
resolution reports an offer of $485,000 in full back pay and benefits, but not
payment.

**Why it matters:** the public enforcement record can reach an agency-reported
individual remedy rather than only a reserve fund, aggregate distribution, or
prospective policy correction. But paid back pay is not automatically a
restored job or opportunity, and an offer is not receipt. This gives the atlas
a useful stage distinction across work, public enforcement, consumer recourse,
and institutional legitimacy.

**Do not overread:** these are five distinct anonymous summaries, not one
worker-level panel or a prevalence estimate. The records do not show payment
instrument or timing, alternative work, accepted reinstatement, durable
compliance, household recovery, trust, organizing, switching, or exit. The
next test needs an episode identifier linking remedy status to worker choice,
later employment, net pay, recurrence, and lived recovery.

Read the [DOJ IER individual-employment-remedy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-098.md).

## 228. The retained routes show institutional visibility, but none yet closes practical exit

In a systematic screen of 17 retained routes across benefits, platforms,
consumer complaints, fraud, housing, care, disaster assistance, employment,
recalls, and household panels, zero met the atlas's strict practical-optionality
standard. To qualify, the same unit must have a dated trigger, an observed
alternative or non-use choice, an institutional response, a verified protected
or sacrificed outcome, and a later behavior or meaning/action endpoint.

**Why it matters:** the broad program is not merely collecting pressures and
formal responses. It now has an explicit test for whether a person actually
regained room to choose, refuse, switch, stay, leave, or act. The screen shows
that visibility and institutional control are common, while optionality,
verified protection, and later meaning/action are rarely joined in one record.
This is a measurement result about the retained evidence—not a claim that
people in the world have no practical exits.

**Do not overread:** the 17-route screen uses different units, denominators,
clocks, and selection mechanisms and cannot produce a practical-exit rate. A
route can have an observed payment, reactivation, benefit transition, or
alternative income and still fail the stronger standard because receipt,
adequacy, durability, or later action is missing. The next test is a lawful
same-episode record joining trigger, effort, alternative, response, receipt,
protected outcome, and later use, switching, trust, or exit.

Read the [practical-exit observability finding](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/findings/us-customer-automation-recourse-002.md).

## 229. A worker can search for an alternative without securing one

In an adjudicated Fair Work Commission case involving a work-from-home
employee, the decision records dismissal without meaningful consultation, an
unsuccessful search for alternative employment, an attempt to seek
reinstatement, and an assessed eight-week lost-income period. The Commission
declined reinstatement because the employment relationship had broken down and
ordered $8,025 less tax plus superannuation, payable by a specified deadline.

**Why it matters:** this is a useful same-unit counterexample to treating
“search” as “option.” The worker made an effort and pursued a formal remedy,
but the public record does not show a secured alternative, restored work, or
payment receipt. It makes the hidden cost of failed optionality visible: search
time, unemployment, lost income, and legal effort can accumulate before any
institutional response becomes usable recovery.

**Do not overread:** this is one publicly identified case, not a population
estimate. The record does not measure search intensity, available offers, net
household effects, payment execution, later employment, trust, recurrence, or
exit. The next test needs payment implementation and later worker-linked
employment, household security, and meaning/action fields.

Read the [adjudicated failed-alternative-employment ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/fwc-no-alternative-employment-remedy-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 230. Child-care difficulty converts time and money into unequal work adaptations

The Census Bureau's 2025 CPS ASEC working paper reports that among households
with a child age 0–13, some households paid for care while others responded to
care difficulty with paid or unpaid leave, cutting hours, quitting, not looking
for work, or supervising children while working. The burden was sharply
unequal: affected households below the poverty line reported much more
converted work-time loss than affected households above 400% of poverty.

**Why it matters:** child care is not only a spending category. When dependable
care is unavailable, unaffordable, or hard to obtain, households convert money,
time, and work opportunity into different adaptations. A household can preserve
formal employment by absorbing care during work, or lose work capacity through
leave, reduced hours, non-search, or exit. These are different forms of
household room, not one affordability score.

**Do not overread:** the paper is a 2024 reference-year cross-section, and the
responses can overlap. It does not identify the sequence, provider, subsidy,
employer rule, duration, wages, child outcome, or later recovery. The next test
needs a dated care-event record linking availability, price or subsidy, informal
support, schedule, work, and later care and employment outcomes.

Read the [child-care work-adaptation finding](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-001.md).

## 231. Child-care incentives do not equal a staffed, dependable slot

GAO's review of Department of Defense child-care programs reports a large
provider workforce, recruitment and retention difficulty, lengthy waitlists,
and thousands of recruitment bonuses or retention allowances. It also
describes fee discounts, training, mentoring, and classroom initiatives.

**Why it matters:** this supplies the institutional-capacity side of the
household adaptation finding. A fee discount can reduce price without creating
a staffed slot; a retention allowance can preserve a worker without shortening
a waitlist; and training can improve quality while temporarily reducing
coverage. Policy inputs and family access therefore move on different clocks.

**Do not overread:** DOD is not civilian child care, and incentives are not
hires, retained workers, staffed slots, quality, or parent work outcomes. The
review does not follow the same families from waitlist to care to work. The next
test needs installation-level vacancies, staffing, waitlist movement, family
use, alternative care, and parent work or readiness outcomes.

Read the [provider-capacity finding](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-005.md).

## 232. Remote work changes the city system, not just the worker's commute

GAO's review finds that telework remained above 2019 levels across community
sizes, while metropolitan planning organizations reported changes in transit
ridership and fare revenue, vehicle-trip timing, commercial real estate,
housing demand, and local planning. More than half of responding MPOs reported
transit adaptations, while about one-third did not intend to—or did not know
how to—incorporate telework into planning.

**Why it matters:** a worker may gain commute time and flexibility while the
collective system loses the demand pattern that supported transit, downtown
retail, and office investment. The benefit for one household can therefore
create a planning and revenue problem for a place, with unequal effects on
workers who cannot telework and on communities dependent on shared services.

**Do not overread:** the evidence does not identify telework's causal effect on
ridership, housing prices, office vacancy, emissions, or political response.
It is not a universal story of urban decline or household improvement. The next
test needs place-month data joining telework frequency, occupation, household
care, trips, transit service and revenue, land use, and distributional effects.

Read the [telework, travel, real-estate, and planning layer](projects/us-transport-mobility/gao-2026-telework-travel-real-estate-planning-layer-v1.md).

## 233. Kinship care can preserve belonging while transferring public costs into family capacity

GAO reports that about 2.4 million U.S. children lived in kinship families in
2023. These families were more likely to live in poverty; grandparents often
headed the household and were more likely to have a disability or be out of
the labor force. For every child in foster care, roughly 18 kinship children
were outside foster care, where eligibility for some supports can differ.

**Why it matters:** family is not only an identity or moral value; it is a
care institution that can absorb housing, childcare, health, school, legal,
and emotional work. Kinship arrangements can protect continuity and belonging
while quietly shifting time, income, disability, and service-navigation costs
onto relatives. A formal foster-care label can then change the support menu even
when the daily care work is similar.

**Do not overread:** the data do not establish that kinship care caused poverty,
disability, labor-force exit, or child needs, nor do they show caregiver hours,
out-of-pocket costs, placement stability, or later outcomes. The next test
needs caregiver capacity, legal status, support eligibility and receipt,
housing, work, health, school, and later family decisions in the same episode.

Read the [kinship families and intergenerational care-support layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/gao-2026-kinship-families-intergenerational-care-support-layer-v1.md).

## 234. A transit route can exist without becoming a practical option

GAO's airport review found that most examined airports had some bus or rail
service, but passenger public-transit use ranged from 4% to 19% where reports
were available, and only about 4% of airport and airline employees nationally
used public transit to commute. Cost, travel time, familiarity, accessibility,
parking, and shift timing all shaped the practical choice.

**Why it matters:** infrastructure availability is not the same as usable
access. A route can be visible on a map but fail for an early-shift worker, a
passenger using a mobility aid, or a household that cannot absorb a long or
unreliable transfer. This links place capacity to consumer choice, labor access,
disability, household transport cost, and public investment.

**Do not overread:** the airport sample is not a national causal estimate, and
low transit use does not by itself prove poor service—people may have different
preferences or alternatives. The next test needs route-day-shift data joining
frequency, fare, reliability, accessibility, trip completion, worker residence,
and actual mode choice.

Read the [airport transit availability and adoption layer](projects/us-transport-mobility/gao-2026-airport-transit-availability-adoption-layer-v1.md).

## 235. County growth can hide contraction in the sectors residents depend on

The 2023 Census Business Dynamics Statistics county-sector table shows that
aggregate county turnover is not a substitute for sector-specific place
capacity. In the selected screen, transportation and warehousing had a median
net-job-creation rate near zero and 1,387 negative-net rows, while health care
and social assistance had a higher median rate but still 1,051 negative-net
rows. Retail, transport, health, and food could move in different directions
inside the same county.

**Why it matters:** residents experience place through particular services and
work systems—care, food, transport, and retail—not through an aggregate county
growth number. A county can look healthy overall while the sector that carries
mobility or care access contracts. This creates a bridge from firm dynamics to
household time, service availability, local trust, and political demand.

**Do not overread:** these are county-sector aggregates, not identifiable firms,
workers, or residents. The data do not show establishment survival, prices,
wages, service use, or causation. The next test needs a small geography-sector
panel joined to actual service access, prices, work quality, or civic response.

Read the [county-sector divergence layer](projects/us-local-business-place/bds-county-sector-divergence-layer-v1.md).

## 236. Mobility constraints can align with weaker care-sector growth without proving a mobility cause

In a bounded 2023 Ohio panel, counties above the within-panel median share of
zero-vehicle households had lower median health and accommodation/food
net-job-creation rates than counties below it. Negative health-sector rows were
11 of 25 in the higher-zero-vehicle group versus 1 of 25 in the lower group.
Retail moved in the opposite direction, and the higher-zero-vehicle counties
also had a lower median long-commute share.

**Why it matters:** mobility constraint is not one simple rural/urban story.
The result suggests that transport access, place-sector capacity, care access,
and work geography should be examined together. The counterexample matters:
the same mobility measure can coexist with different commute and sector
patterns, so a single “access” score can mislead.

**Do not overread:** this is an ecological, descriptive county comparison, not
a resident-level or causal estimate. Zero-vehicle households and commute time
do not reveal provider reach, nonwork trips, prices, appointment use, or worker
security. The next test needs a service-specific outcome with compatible
geography, alternatives, and a dated use or closure record.

Read the [Ohio mobility and sector-flow panel](projects/us-local-business-place/bds-ohio-mobility-panel-v1.md).

## 237. Health-sector job growth does not prove primary-care adequacy

An overlay of 2023 county-sector business flows with the current HRSA primary-
care shortage surface shows why employer activity and usable care must remain
separate. Counties represented by a primary-care HPSA component still had
health-care and social-assistance job growth in the selected comparison, but
the growth pattern did not establish that residents had an appointment,
acceptable travel time, insurance access, or continuity.

**Why it matters:** a place can add health-sector jobs while patients still
face shortage designations, provider limits, long waits, or inaccessible
routes. The finding connects firms, public designation, local capacity, care
access, and household time without treating any one surface as the outcome.

**Do not overread:** the BDS year and HRSA designation vintage differ, and an
HPSA component does not label every resident in a county. The comparison is
descriptive; it does not establish that shortage status caused business flows
or that positive health-sector growth improved care. The next test needs
component type, provider capacity, travel or wait measures, accepted coverage,
and a dated care or household outcome.

Read the [BDS–HRSA capacity-versus-adequacy overlay](projects/us-local-business-place/bds-hrsa-capacity-adequacy-overlay-v1.md).

## 238. Rural and metro places can have different sector capacity even when the aggregate economy looks similar

The 2023 county-sector business-flow comparison finds different metro and
nonmetro profiles. Nonmetro counties had a higher median retail net-job rate,
but lower median health/social-assistance and accommodation/food growth. The
negative-row shares were especially different in health and food: health rows
were negative in 36.0% of comparable nonmetro rows versus 24.5% of metro rows,
and food rows in 33.7% versus 16.5%.

**Why it matters:** “rural” and “urban” are not single capacity conditions.
Residents encounter different mixes of care, food, transport, and retail
turnover, which can affect travel, time, prices, work, and local belonging.
The pattern also warns against treating a higher retail rate as proof that a
place has stronger health or food access.

**Do not overread:** this is a descriptive 2023 county-sector comparison, not
a causal estimate or a resident outcome. It does not show why the profiles
differ, whether openings replaced lost services, or whether residents could
use the resulting firms. The next test needs a matched place panel joining
sector flows to service use, prices, wages, travel, and political response.

Read the [metro/nonmetro sector-turnover profile](projects/us-local-business-place/bds-rural-urban-turnover-profile-v1.md).

## 239. Mobility context can separate social need even when routine-checkup rates barely move

In an Ohio county panel combining business-sector flow, vehicle constraints,
commute context, and modeled CDC PLACES measures, counties above the median
zero-vehicle share had nearly the same modeled routine-checkup estimate as
counties below it—76.95% versus 77.05%. But the higher-zero-vehicle group had
higher medians for transportation insecurity (8.80% versus 7.45%), food
insecurity (15.95% versus 13.20%), frequent mental distress (20.25% versus
18.85%), and lack of health insurance (9.15% versus 8.35%).

**Why it matters:** a single care-use indicator can look stable while the
surrounding practical conditions differ. Routine checkups, transport, food,
mental health, and insurance are related but not interchangeable currencies.
This is a useful guardrail against reading stable checkup prevalence as stable
health access or household security.

**Do not overread:** these are county-level modeled estimates, not patient or
visit records, and the panel is descriptive. It does not establish that
vehicle constraints caused the differences or that health-sector job flow had
an effect. The next test needs provider reach, travel or wait, attempted and
completed care, price or coverage, and a household/work consequence.

Read the [Ohio BDS–ACS–PLACES outcome panel](projects/us-local-business-place/bds-ohio-places-outcome-panel-v1.md).

## 240. Positive health-sector job flow does not separate modeled county need

In a nationwide 2023 county comparison, counties with negative versus
nonnegative health-care and social-assistance net-job-creation rates had nearly
identical modeled medians for food insecurity (14.9% versus 14.8%), lack of
reliable transportation (8.1% versus 8.1%), routine checkups (78.4% versus
78.4%), lack of health insurance (9.9% versus 10.0%), and frequent mental
distress (17.1% versus 17.3%).

**Why it matters:** employer flow is not a proxy for resident care adequacy or
health improvement. A positive sector signal can coexist with a near-null
county outcome comparison because provider capacity, resident need, insurance,
travel, prices, quality, and use sit on different layers.

**Do not overread:** this is a descriptive county aggregate using modeled PLACES
estimates and BDS employer flows from compatible but non-identical measurement
surfaces. It does not prove that health-sector growth has no effect. The next
test needs a dated provider or care episode with access, attempted and
completed use, cost or coverage, and later household/work consequences.

Read the [BDS–PLACES health-flow/outcome bridge](projects/us-local-business-place/bds-places-health-flow-outcome-bridge-v1.md).

## 241. A shortage designation can be linked to a candidate site without proving usable care

In an Ohio HRSA crosswalk, 61 of 94 coordinate-bearing primary-care shortage
rows shared a BHCMIS organization identifier with an official health-center
service-site file. For those shared-identifier rows, the nearest site belonging
to the same organization was within 1 kilometer for 57 rows and within 5
kilometers for 59.

**Why it matters:** this is a stronger access stage than a county shortage
label or a nearby-point calculation. It links an institutional shortage object
to a candidate service organization and location. But the practical route still
has more steps: the site may not be the designated component, may not offer the
needed service, may not accept the person's insurance, may have no open
appointment, or may be unreachable in time and cost.

**Do not overread:** shared organization identity and geographic proximity do
not establish site identity for the designated component, hours, eligibility,
acceptance, wait, attempted care, completed care, or household outcome. The
next test needs site-specific service, hours, travel, coverage, appointment,
and completed-use fields.

Read the [Ohio HPSA-to-site identifier crosswalk](projects/us-local-business-place/hrsa-ohio-hpsa-site-identifier-crosswalk-v1.md).

## 242. Material pressure can narrow shared cultural access without eliminating cultural making

The corrected March 2026 Census HTOPS public-use file contains expense
difficulty and cultural-participation questions in the same respondent release.
Among respondents reporting no difficulty with usual expenses, the weighted
share attending an in-person art exhibit in the prior four weeks was 10.387%,
compared with 5.291% among those reporting somewhat difficulty and 7.182% among
those reporting very difficulty. Agreement that there were plenty of nearby
arts and cultural opportunities fell from 78.554% to 61.248% and 45.698% across
those same groups. Personal art-making was not monotonic: 21.498%, 18.111%,
and 21.228% respectively.

**Why it matters:** economic pressure may constrain shared cultural access and
the perception of local opportunity without eliminating all cultural activity.
Attendance, perceived neighborhood capacity, and personal creation are
different cultural currencies. A household may lose a paid or public-facing
route while retaining a private or low-cost practice.

**Do not overread:** this is a same-round descriptive association. Expense
difficulty is household-reported, cultural outcomes are respondent-reported,
and the data do not identify income causation, arts prices, transportation,
availability, selection into participation, duration, identity, wellbeing, or
later civic action. The next test needs a dated household pressure or access
episode linked to cultural participation, cost, travel, social connection, and
later meaning or action.

Read the [Census HTOPS social-connectedness and community-engagement acquisition gate](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/census-2026-htops-social-connectedness-community-engagement-acquisition-gate-v1.md) and [machine-readable screen](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/data/htops-2026-social-cultural-civic-screen-v1.json).

## 243. Expense difficulty aligns with less routine group participation, not one simple civic-withdrawal pattern

In the same corrected March 2026 HTOPS release, the weighted share reporting
at least one meeting of a club or organization in the prior year fell from
41.205% among respondents reporting no difficulty with usual expenses to
27.202% among those reporting very difficult expenses. Public-meeting
attendance for a local issue did not move monotonically: it was 8.802%,
11.038%, 10.590%, and 10.064% across the four expense-difficulty groups.

**Why it matters:** economic pressure may reduce recurring organizational
participation while formal local issue attendance follows a different route.
Clubs, unions, neighborhood groups, school groups, and public meetings are not
one civic-participation ladder. Lower routine participation should not be
treated automatically as apathy, and higher public-meeting attendance should
not be treated automatically as recovered political power.

**Do not overread:** this is a same-round descriptive association. Expense
difficulty is household-reported; the civic measures are respondent-reported;
the data do not identify time constraints, group type, issue, transportation,
childcare, prior participation, efficacy, trust, or later action. The next test
needs a dated pressure or institutional encounter linked to time, group or
issue, action quality, response, and later participation or exit.

Read the [Census HTOPS social-connectedness and community-engagement acquisition gate](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/census-2026-htops-social-connectedness-community-engagement-acquisition-gate-v1.md) and [machine-readable screen](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/data/htops-2026-social-cultural-civic-screen-v1.json).

## 244. An export-control licensing gate can create supply friction without being a total cutoff

China's 2023 gallium and germanium controls required permission for specified
raw, processed, and semiconductor-adjacent products, with applications asking
for contract, technical, end-user, importer, and end-use information. The
official record later reported that some applications had been approved. A
USITC briefing reported a sharp August–October contraction in Chinese wrought
exports and price increases after the control took effect.

**Why it matters:** strategic dependency can be exercised through permission,
disclosure, screening, transaction time, and uncertainty—not only by refusing
all shipments. The controlled forms reach closer to usable technical inputs,
so geological supply, processed material, and production dependence are
different stages of the same geopolitical surface.

**Do not overread:** the record does not identify how many applications were
filed or denied, a named U.S. buyer, inventory depletion, firm redesign,
production loss, alliance response, or a strategic concession. Shipment and
price movement are not proof of lasting shortage or leverage. The next test
needs a buyer/product/application episode linked to licensing time, shipment,
inventory, substitution, production, and an observed external response.

Read the [China gallium and germanium export-control licensing event](projects/ai-work-control/china-gallium-germanium-export-control-licensing-event-v1.md).

## 245. Foreign operational use can precede a later multilateral procurement framework

The Piorun record keeps two timelines separate. Norway's 2022 contract was
followed by a reported 2023 batch handover and a 2024 live-fire/fielding event.
Poland's later 2026 SAFE/EDIRPA framework reports a much larger common-
procurement route, planned deliveries, and allied procurement participation,
but does not by itself establish delivered inventory or operational use for
that later framework.

**Why it matters:** “export success” can mean domestic production, foreign
contract, handover, fielding, allied demand, or changed adversary behavior.
Those are different geopolitical and state-capacity stages. An earlier user
can reach bounded operational use before a later framework is realized, while
the framework can still signal alliance coordination and demand.

**Do not overread:** Norway's live-fire/fielding signal is not national
readiness, battlefield use, deterrence, or proof of changed adversary behavior.
The SAFE framework is not delivered inventory, and allied procurement is not
proof that SAFE caused each country's choice. The next test needs an
identifier-bearing contract or acceptance record with quantity, training,
maintenance, operational use, and an observed external response.

Read the [Piorun procurement-to-operational-response record](projects/ai-work-control/piorun-procurement-to-operational-response-record-v1.md).

## 246. Operational fielding of one weapon variant does not close the capability chain for its successors

The 2026 Marine Aviation Plan states that the AGM-158C LRASM C-1 variant was
operationally fielded on the F/A-18E/F in FY2024. The same plan says F-35B and
F-35C integration continues and that the upgraded C-3 variant begins its
production cut-in in FY2026. A separate earlier Navy budget records C-1 Early
Operational Capability in Q1 FY2020.

**Why it matters:** this is direct official fielding evidence, but it is
variant- and platform-specific. “LRASM is operational” can conceal different
clocks for initial operational capability, later fielding language, platform
integration, upgraded production, sustainment, and combat use.

**Do not overread:** the record does not provide quantity, serviceable
inventory, maintenance performance, repair time, combat employment, C-3
completion, F-35 operational availability, or an observed external response.
Fielding is stronger than testing, but it is not yet readiness or geopolitical
leverage. The next test needs an identifier-bearing inventory or unit record
linked to maintenance, training, use, replacement, and external behavior.

Read the [LRASM C-1 fielding source note](projects/ai-work-control/data/us-lrasm-c1-fielding-source-note-2026-09-18.md).

## 247. A completed first repair can be followed by a product-level finding that the remedy was ineffective

The CPSC's September 2026 Sauna360 recall expansion says an October 2025
repair—additional hardware and a revised manual—was ineffective. The expanded
notice covers about 6,000 benches, reports 77 additional breakage reports and
six injuries, tells consumers to stop using the product even if they received
the earlier remedy, and offers a second free repair with a professional
installer and additional hardware.

**Why it matters:** this is a rare public durability stage. The institution
does not merely repeat a notice; it revises the remedy after later evidence
shows that the first fix did not close the hazard. “Repair offered,” “repair
received,” and “safe after repair” must therefore remain separate states.

**Do not overread:** the notices do not identify unique owners, first-repair
recipients, failure rates, notice receipt, appointments, completed second
repairs, downtime, residual cost, trust, switching, or safe use. The record
does not show that every first repair failed. The next test needs a purchaser-
linked repair history with notice, appointment, completion, recurrence, and
later use or exit.

Read the [Sauna360 repeat-remedy source note](projects/us-marketplace-product-safety/data/sauna360-repeat-remedy-source-note-2026-09-17.md).

## 248. A free dealer repair can be visible without showing restored safe mobility

The CPSC recall for about 34,000 Honda Talon recreational off-highway vehicles
offers a free dealer inspection and repair after reporting 18 incidents,
including a crash with minor occupant injuries. The route is different from a
replacement program: the vehicle holder must discover the notice, contact a
dealer, arrange inspection or repair, and absorb any scheduling, transport,
downtime, or substitute-use burden.

**Why it matters:** product safety remedies have different practical routes.
Replacement, professional repair, dealer inspection, stop-use instructions,
and repeat repair are not interchangeable implementation states. The agency
and manufacturer can make a remedy visible while the owner's safe mobility and
actual use remain unobserved.

**Do not overread:** the recall does not identify purchasers, notice receipt,
appointments, repair completion, downtime, substitute access, residual loss,
or later household outcome. The reported incident count is not a failure rate,
and a free repair offer is not proof of restored safety. The next test needs a
purchaser-linked repair history with contact, appointment, completion,
recurrence, and later use or exit.

Read the [Honda Talon repair event ledger](projects/us-marketplace-product-safety/data/honda-talon-repair-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 249. Civic participation can rebound in reach while thinning in time

The Census Bureau and AmeriCorps' 2023 Civic Engagement and Volunteering
Supplement reports that 28.3% of Americans age 16 and older formally
volunteered through an organization between September 2022 and September 2023,
up from 23.2% in 2021. Informal helping also rose to 54.2%, from 51.7% in
2019.

The rebound was not a simple restoration of the old participation pattern.
Mean annual hours per formal volunteer fell from 96.5 in 2017 to 70 in 2023,
and the median fell from 40 to 24 hours. About 18% of formal volunteers served
entirely or partly online; virtual or hybrid volunteers reported more hours on
average than people serving only in person.

**Why it matters:** more people can re-enter civic life while the typical
commitment becomes shorter, more episodic, or more distributed across online
and informal routes. Reach, neighborly help, time intensity, organizational
continuity, political efficacy, and durable collective action are separate
currencies.

**Do not overread:** these are repeated cross-sectional survey estimates, not a
matched-person panel or a measure of civic success. They do not show whether
volunteering changed trust, belonging, policy, voting, protest, local capacity,
or later retention. The increase may reflect recovery from pandemic lows rather
than a durable cultural shift, and the hours comparison does not identify who
could not participate because of work, care, health, transport, or money.

Read the [Civic participation layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/americorps-cev-2023-civic-participation-layer-v1.md).

## 250. Teen digital life is becoming ordinary before its exit costs are visible

Pew's 2025 survey of 1,458 U.S. teens ages 13–17 found that 64% had used
chatbots and 28% used them daily. Social platforms were already routine: 61%
visited TikTok daily, 55% Instagram daily, and 46% Snapchat daily. The uses are
not interchangeable: chatbots can be instrumental for information or
schoolwork, while social platforms can function as peer and identity spaces.

Use is also uneven. Chatbot use was 68% among teens ages 15–17 versus 57%
among ages 13–14, and 66% in households earning at least $75,000 versus 56%
below $30,000. These differences may reflect access, schoolwork, family rules,
product fit, or different needs; they do not establish a harm or advantage
effect.

**Why it matters:** adolescent digital participation is becoming ordinary
infrastructure for both sociality and knowledge before the record shows whether
it remains replaceable. Frequency, usefulness, trust, privacy, emotional
reliance, school effects, and the ability to stop are separate outcomes.

**Do not overread:** the survey does not observe recommendation content, logged
attention, accuracy, privacy exposure, family mediation, mental-health effects,
or an attempted exit. High frequency can remain bounded and replaceable; lower
use can reflect restricted access rather than agency. The next test needs a
teen/parent or longitudinal record linking a defined encounter to time,
school/family relationships, trust, a human alternative, and actual exit or
continued use.

Read the [teen digital-life finding](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-003.md)
and [teen social/AI layer](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/pew-teens-social-ai-2025-layer-v1.md).

## 251. Poverty improvement and Medicaid continuity can move on different clocks

The Census Bureau reports that the Hispanic official poverty rate reached
13.9% in 2025, down 1.2 percentage points from 2024 and the lowest on record.
At the same time, Census reports that overall Medicaid coverage declined from
2023 to 2025, including a second consecutive decline between 2024 and 2025,
as pandemic-era continuous coverage ended and states returned to standard
eligibility and enrollment processes.

The coverage change was not uniform across ages. Children experienced a
2.0-point decline from 2023 to 2024, while working-age adults declined 1.1
points. From 2024 to 2025, total Medicaid coverage fell 0.5 points and
working-age-adult coverage fell 0.6 points; children did not change
significantly in that interval.

**Why it matters:** lower poverty is not the same thing as stable health
coverage. A household can improve on an income measure while facing a coverage
interruption, and a household can retain coverage while remaining materially
insecure. Material position and public-program continuity are governed by
different clocks.

**Do not overread:** these are population-level CPS ASEC estimates, not a
matched same-person or same-household transition. The Hispanic poverty result
is not a Hispanic-specific Medicaid result, and the releases do not identify
each person's reason for coverage change or whether replacement coverage was
obtained. The next test needs poverty transitions joined to eligibility,
renewal, churn, replacement coverage, care use, affordability, work, and later
security.

Read the [poverty and Medicaid clock-divergence finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-020.md)
and [source layer](projects/us-safety-net-access/census-2025-poverty-medicaid-clock-layer-v1.md).

## 252. A record median can conceal a stalled bottom and a widening gap

The Census Bureau reports that real median household income reached $87,460 in
2025, up 2.6% from 2024 and the highest level in the series since 1967. That
headline improvement does not describe the whole distribution: income at the
90th percentile rose for a third consecutive year, while income at the 10th
percentile did not differ significantly from 2024.

Over 1967–2025, pretax household income rose about 56% at the 10th and 50th
percentiles but about 121% at the 90th percentile. The 90th-to-10th percentile
ratio widened from 9.23 to 13.06.

**Why it matters:** a median is a midpoint, not a representative household.
The economy can look better at the center while the lower tail is flat and the
distance to the top grows. Percentile movement, prices, taxes and transfers,
debt, liquid assets, care costs, and practical room are separate currencies.

**Do not overread:** these are national CPS ASEC distribution estimates, not a
matched household panel or a causal explanation of percentile movement. They
do not show which households changed behavior, health, work, trust, political
action, or ability to exit a bad option. The next test should join distribution
position to local prices, housing and health costs, taxes and benefits, debt,
liquid assets, work intensity, and later adaptation.

Read the [income-distribution decomposition finding](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/findings/us-household-financial-pressure-014.md)
and [source layer](projects/us-household-financial-pressure/census-2025-income-distribution-decomposition-layer-v1.md).

## 253. Intergenerational living can provide care while narrowing work and money options

The Federal Reserve's 2025 household survey reports that 33% of adults lived
with at least two adult generations, and 49% of adults under 30 lived with a
parent—12 percentage points higher than in 2019. Among parents of children
under 13, 25% used paid childcare in the prior week and 44% used some unpaid
childcare. Among partnered parents of young children, 58% of mothers versus
12% of fathers identified themselves as the usual primary caretaker; among
dual-earner parents, the comparison was 49% versus 8%.

Adult care is distributed through work as well: 18% of adults regularly cared
for an adult relative or friend, and 32% of prime-age caregivers had no paid
job, rising to 44% among daily caregivers.

**Why it matters:** co-residence, family care, paid care, and unpaid care are
not simply signs of breakdown or prosperity. They can provide belonging,
flexibility, and practical protection while also transferring costs into
housing capacity, gendered time, paid work, mobility, and family conflict.
Care is a social institution and a labor-market adaptation at the same time.

**Do not overread:** these are descriptive survey associations, not a dated
care shock, causal employment effect, or proof of coercion. Lower paid-care use
can reflect affordability, availability, preference, nonemployment, or unpaid
support. The next test needs a same-family or person panel linking care need,
paid/unpaid alternatives, cost, travel, schedule, work and health response,
employer/public support, and later stability or exit.

Read the [2025 care and living-arrangements finding](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-003.md).

## 254. Local goodwill can be stronger than confidence in reciprocal help

Pew's March 2025 American Trends Panel found that 76% of adults were
extremely or very likely to bring a neighbor's mail or water plants, while
52% thought neighbors would do the same for them. Sixty-seven percent would
bring a meal to a sick neighbor or friend, compared with 42% who expected that
help. The gap was smaller for lending money: 50% would loan money to a friend
in an emergency, versus 45% who expected a friend to do so.

**Why it matters:** social resilience is not just willingness to give. It also
depends on whether people believe help is available, reciprocal, and usable
when a real shock occurs. A community can contain substantial goodwill while
individuals still feel underprotected or reluctant to ask.

**Do not overread:** these are hypothetical, cross-sectional judgments about
different relationships, not delivered aid or proof that neighbors fail one
another. The survey does not observe helper burden, duration, adequacy, formal
service substitution, recovery, or later civic action. The next test needs a
place-linked or repeated episode recording help requested, help delivered,
cost to the helper, adequacy, and later trust or movement.

Read the [neighbor goodwill and reciprocal-support finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-044.md).

## 255. Unpaid care changes the financial adaptation menu even for people working full time

The Federal Reserve's 2024 household survey separates regular unpaid adult
care from employment status. Among full-time workers, 67.8% of regular
caregivers said prices worsened their finances, compared with 60.2% of
full-time adults not reporting regular unpaid care. Caregivers also reported
more reduced use (70.7% versus 62.2%), more borrowing (24.4% versus 16.4%),
and more reduced savings (57.4% versus 42.2%). They also reported working more
or getting a job more often (29.7% versus 20.6%), showing that the response is
not simply “caregivers leave work.”

The same pattern appears within part-time and nonworking groups, though the
available labor response differs. Among caregivers, nonworking adults had the
lowest reported three-month emergency-fund capacity (48.6%), while full-time
caregivers reported somewhat more capacity (53.5%) alongside the highest
borrowing and savings cuts in the table.

**Why it matters:** unpaid care changes what a household can do when prices
rise. Employment can provide income while still coexisting with borrowing,
reduced savings, reduced consumption, and extra work. Care is therefore not
just a time burden or a family value; it changes the available financial and
work adaptations.

**Do not overread:** this is a weighted, cross-sectional comparison. It does
not measure care hours, recipient need, pay, schedule control, paid
replacement, leave, household income, or whether care caused the financial
response. Each adaptation has its own missing-data universe, and employment
status is not a measure of security or choice. The next test needs a dated
care episode followed through alternatives, work schedule, payment or support,
food/housing/health trade-offs, and later recovery or action.

Read the [SHED care/work adaptation layer](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/shed-care-work-distribution-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable care/work record](records/us-shed-care-work-adaptation-2024.json).

## 256. Formal workplace representation is an uneven layer of bargaining room

The Bureau of Labor Statistics reports that 10.0% of U.S. wage and salary
workers were union members in 2025 and 11.2% were covered by a union or
employee-association contract. The corresponding 2024 rates were 9.9% and
11.1%. The national averages conceal large differences: in 2025 membership
was 4.7% for workers ages 16–24 and 10.8% for workers 25 and older; it was
4.3% in management, business, and financial operations and 14.7% in
professional and related occupations. State rates also varied substantially.

The BLS earnings tables show higher median usual weekly earnings for full-time
union members than for nonunion workers ($1,404 versus $1,174 in 2025), but
workers differ in occupation, industry, age, tenure, geography, and selection
into jobs. The comparison is not a causal union premium.

**Why it matters:** representation is a piece of social infrastructure for
negotiating new technology, schedules, safety, monitoring, pay, and error risk.
The same workplace change can land very differently where workers have a
contract, a collective channel, or no formal representation. Membership and
coverage are not the same thing, and neither guarantees practical voice.

**Do not overread:** union membership is not autonomy, and contract coverage
is not proof of an effective appeal route, better health, household security,
or political action. The BLS 2025 annual estimates exclude October because of
the federal shutdown and are not strictly comparable with other annual
averages. The next test needs a worker or workplace episode linking a rule or
technology change to representation, control, appeal, pay/hours, household
time, and later acceptance, collective action, transfer, or exit.

Read the [BLS union and bargaining-room layer](projects/ai-work-control/bls-union-bargaining-room-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable representation record](records/us-bls-union-representation-2024-2025.json).

## 257. Nonvoting contains different time, health, administrative, and meaning barriers

The Census Bureau's 2024 Voting and Registration Supplement reports that 73.6%
of citizens were registered and 65.3% reported voting. Among reported
nonvoters age 18 and older, the largest stated reasons were not being
interested (19.7%), a conflicting schedule (17.8%), dissatisfaction with
candidates or issues (14.7%), and illness or disability (12.4%). Smaller but
distinct routes included registration problems (3.6%), inconvenient polling
places (2.4%), and transportation problems (2.2%).

The barrier mix varies with life position. Among nonvoters age 18–24, schedule
conflict was 22.3%; among those 65 and older, illness or disability was 35.0%.
Among nonvoters in families under $10,000, illness/disability was 16.7% and
schedule conflict 8.8%; at $150,000 or more, the corresponding figures were
3.7% and 19.5%. Among people living at their address less than one year,
registration problems were 9.8%, compared with 2.3% among those there three
years or longer.

**Why it matters:** political participation is not one ladder from interest
to turnout. A person can be blocked by time, health, mobility, administrative
friction, residence change, or dissatisfaction with representation. Treating
all nonvoting as apathy hides how material and institutional conditions shape
the available route to political voice.

**Do not overread:** these are self-reported, cross-sectional survey reasons,
not causal explanations. The categories are not one additive disengagement
score, and the data do not identify which earlier bill, care burden, work rule,
move, or institutional experience came first. The next test needs repeated
elections or an event-linked design joining exposure, attribution, access,
participation, and later institutional response.

Read the [CPS turnout and participation-friction layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/cps-2024-turnout-participation-friction-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable participation record](records/us-cps-voting-participation-friction-2024.json).

## 258. Digital capability creates a national electricity burden before its local incidence is known

The Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory's 2025 update estimates that U.S.
data centers used about 176 terawatt-hours of electricity in 2023, or 4.4% of
national electricity use. Its 2030 reference case reaches 649 TWh, or 11.8%,
with a modeled uncertainty range of 521–843 TWh and 9.5%–15.3% of national
electricity use.

This turns AI and cloud capability into a physical infrastructure question:
electricity, transmission, cooling, land, water, labor, finance, and public
permission become complements to software and chips. But the scale estimate
does not say which utility or county bears the cost, whether the load is
approved or actually operating, how rates change, or whether the resulting
capacity is publicly useful or replaceable.

**Why it matters:** a technology can become a public affordability,
reliability, and governance issue before its productivity benefits are
distributed. National capability and local burden are separate stages, as are
projected demand, energized capacity, actual use, and public benefit.

**Do not overread:** the 2030 figures are model scenarios, not observed use or
a probability forecast. National terawatt-hours are not a household surcharge
or a county impact estimate, and the record does not establish water, land,
reliability, ownership, provider dependence, or geopolitical leverage. The
next test needs a matched utility/place episode linking approved and actual
load to rates, upgrades, local benefits, public response, and the ability to
switch or refuse.

Read the [data-center energy-load baseline](projects/ai-work-control/us-data-center-energy-load-baseline-v1.md)
and [machine-readable national-load record](records/us-lbnl-data-center-electricity-load-2023-2030.json).

## 259. Synthetic cross-partisan contact can open a short-term window without creating durable depolarization

Five preregistered experiments with online U.S. partisan participants tested
chatbots prompted to represent a political outgroup. In one study, outgroup
warmth rose 4.3 points on a 0–100 thermometer after a ten-minute conversation.
In another, 67% of participants exposed to the outgroup bot chose a subsequent
real outgroup conversation, compared with 61% in an apolitical-chat control
(odds ratio 1.33; 95% CI 1.04–1.71). The controlled comparisons suggest that
the outgroup content—not merely screen time or having a conversation—can lower
the immediate barrier to trying contact.

The persistence result is the important counterweight. In the one-week study,
the immediate warmth effect was *d* = 0.46, while the preregistered one-week
mean effect was *d* = 0.05 and was not statistically significant. A brief AI
interaction can therefore create a short-run opening without producing a
durable change in political identity, trust, cooperation, or voting.

**Why it matters:** AI-mediated interaction is becoming a cultural mechanism,
not just a productivity tool. It may reduce the social cost of approaching an
opposing group, but the meaningful endpoint is whether people then sustain
real, informed, non-coerced contact.

**Do not overread:** these are structured experiments and a preprint, not a
population estimate. Warmth and an immediate choice are not policy agreement,
institutional trust, turnout, or lasting depolarization. The bot may represent
the outgroup inaccurately or strategically, and effects may vary by party,
issue, model, disclosure, and repetition. The next test needs version-stamped
repeated contact, factuality auditing, human alternatives, cooperation,
refusal/exit, and follow-up over at least a month.

Read the [synthetic cross-partisan contact finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-026.md)
and [machine-readable experiment record](records/us-ai-synthetic-contact-cross-partisan-2026.json).

## 260. AI use, personal control, and confidence in institutions are different country-level realities

The UNDP 2025 Global Survey on AI and Human Development shows why “AI
adoption” is too simple a cultural measure. In the United States, 32.6% of
respondents said they used AI at least weekly, while 33.9% felt confident they
could control AI interactions, 29.1% were confident government could use AI,
and 37.5% were confident AI was designed in people's interests.

The comparison countries separate these dimensions in different ways. China
reported 37.4% weekly use, 39.9% confidence in personal control, 88.6%
confidence in government use, and 83.3% confidence that AI serves people's
interests. Germany reported 38.3% weekly use but only 27.8% confidence in
personal control, alongside 44.9% government confidence and 42.5% design
confidence. South Africa reported higher weekly use at 59.4%, 42.8% confidence
in personal control, 57.2% government confidence, and 67.6% design confidence.

**Why it matters:** people can use AI frequently without feeling in control,
or trust public and private AI without that trust proving service quality or
actual agency. The cultural meaning of AI depends on access, institutional
expectations, governance, and perceived replaceability—not use frequency alone.

**Do not overread:** these are cross-sectional country comparisons of roughly
1,000 respondents per country, with different item denominators and no causal
country effect. Translation, survey mode, political context, internet access,
labor-market composition, and response style may contribute to the differences.
The results do not establish productivity, privacy loss, public-service quality,
or realized control. The next test needs the same person followed through AI
exposure, purpose, disclosure, human review, correction, continued use or
avoidance, trust, and later work or civic action.

Read the [UNDP AI agency comparison](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-070.md)
and [machine-readable country record](records/us-undp-ai-human-development-agency-comparison-2025.json).

## 261. An alliance response can be visible before deterrence is proven

On 10 September 2025, Poland reported repeated Russian-drone violations of
its airspace and requested NATO Article 4 consultations. NATO reported that
Polish F-16s, Dutch F-35s, Italian AWACS, NATO tanker support, and German
Patriots participated in the response, and that the North Atlantic Council
expressed solidarity with Poland.

Polish records then describe a further response: NATO announced Eastern Sentry
to strengthen air defense on the eastern flank, Poland summoned Russia's
chargé d'affaires and issued a protest, and a Polish advisory body addressed
disinformation resilience.

**Why it matters:** this adds an observed incident-to-alliance-response stage
to the geopolitical atlas. It is different from a procurement contract,
delivery milestone, or public opinion measure. A state can demonstrate
procedural and allied response before it demonstrates durable deterrence.

**Do not overread:** the public record does not show that a particular weapon
system caused the response, that Eastern Sentry was sustained at a measured
level, that Russia changed behavior, or that Polish and allied publics found
the response legitimate. Official statements are not an independent incident,
damage, civilian-safety, or public-belief audit.

Read the [Poland/NATO incident-response finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-119.md),
[structured incident ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/poland-airspace-violation-alliance-response-event-ledger-v1.json),
and [bounded comparator memo](projects/ai-work-control/poland-airspace-violation-alliance-response-comparator-v1.md).

## 262. A worker-remedy institution can be used before it proves effective recovery

Malaysia's Gig Workers Tribunal was reported to hear its first GrabCar matter
in Kuching on 1 September 2026. The claimant reportedly appeared personally,
GrabCar was represented by an employee, and the matter returned for evidence
and additional witness examination.

**Why it matters:** this adds a distinct stage between legal design and lived
outcome. A remedy institution can be used in a named dispute before the public
record shows a decision, correction, payment, restored access, later work, or
trust. Personal appearance may lower legal cost while leaving evidence,
technical knowledge, time, and organizational capacity unequal.

**Do not overread:** the available reports do not publish the primary case
identifier, filed claim, legal reasoning, award, payment, account correction,
or later work outcome. A hearing does not establish a violation, effective
human review, platform compliance, worker recovery, or exit.

Read the [Malaysia worker-remedy finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-120.md),
[procedural record](projects/ai-work-control/malaysia-grabcar-tribunal-first-hearing-case-record-v1.md),
and [machine-readable case record](records/my-grabcar-tribunal-first-hearing-2026.json).

## 263. A process defect can coexist with a valid reason and no restoration

In one Fair Work Commission case, the decision-maker identified procedural
deficiencies in a platform deactivation process but accepted the worker's
safety-related complaints as a valid substantive reason for the deactivation.
The unfair-deactivation claim was dismissed and no restoration remedy was
ordered.

**Why it matters:** procedural fairness and substantive merits are separate.
A notice or review failure can be real without proving that the underlying
platform decision was invalid. But dismissal of the claim does not show that
the worker experienced no income loss, burden, or later consequence.

**Do not overread:** this is one Australian case, not a prevalence estimate or
a U.S. worker result. The public record does not show later account status,
payment, alternative work, recurrence, trust, or exit.

Read the [procedure-versus-merits finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-121.md),
[case record](records/au-kyei-merits-dismissal-2025.json), and
[implementation recheck](projects/ai-work-control/uber-kyei-merits-followup-recheck-2026-09-18.md).

## 264. Grandparent ties can function as social infrastructure before they become formal care

Pew's 2026 synthesis reports that 70% of grandparents ages 65 and older were
in touch with grandchildren at least a few times a month, while 21% reported at
least 100 hours of grandchild or great-grandchild care over the prior two years.
Separately, 8% of children lived with a grandparent who headed the household in
2024. Among adults with a living grandparent, 36% reported being extremely or
very close; the share was 49% among Black adults, compared with roughly one-third
among Hispanic, Asian, and White adults.

**Why it matters:** this adds an intergenerational-meaning and communication
layer to the atlas. Grandparent relationships can provide belonging, advice,
care, transportation, childcare, or housing without appearing as a formal
benefit or crisis-placement record. Routine contact, care work, and shared
housing are related but separate stages.

**Do not overread:** the evidence does not establish causal effects on health,
child outcomes, work, consumption, trust, or recovery. Contact includes seeing,
phone, or texting; care hours overlap a pandemic period; and closeness is a
self-report, not a measure of cultural identity or household welfare.

Read the [grandparenthood and intergenerational-connection finding](projects/us-aging-care-strain/findings/us-aging-care-strain-017.md),
[layer](projects/us-aging-care-strain/pew-2026-grandparenthood-intergenerational-connection-layer-v1.md),
and [machine-readable record](records/us-pew-grandparenthood-intergenerational-connection-2024-2025.json).

## 265. A platform's own product can help today's customer while changing tomorrow's choice set

An NBER field experiment hid Amazon-owned brands from shoppers and compared
consumer choice and search behavior. In the tested categories, consumers chose
similar products when the private labels were absent, without more search effort
or more cross-platform substitution. Yet the authors' short-run welfare
estimates indicate that removing Amazon brands reduced consumer surplus by about
5.4% (5.5% in the NBER summary), with heterogeneous effects across categories.
Demoting the brands in search did not increase estimated consumer surplus because
some consumers valued those products and their prominent placement.

**Why it matters:** this is a different platform-power mechanism from an ad fee.
A platform-owned product can be a real convenience or quality fit for some
customers while the same firm controls the shelf, data, fulfillment, and rules
under which rivals reach those customers. “Own product present” is therefore not
equivalent to “consumer harm,” and “consumer benefit today” is not equivalent
to durable competition tomorrow.

**Do not overread:** this is one field experiment in selected Amazon categories,
not a long-run market-power estimate. It does not establish seller entry or
exit, platform data use, preferential treatment, future prices, product quality,
or welfare outside the tested setting.

Read the [platform-owned-product finding](findings/us-platform-owned-products-matched-evidence-001.md),
[project](projects/us-platform-owned-products-choice/README.md), and
[machine-readable record](records/us-nber-amazon-vertical-integration-consumer-choice-2025.json).

## 266. Medicaid coverage can increase voting briefly without creating durable political participation

The Oregon Health Insurance Experiment linked random Medicaid-lottery selection,
coverage, and official statewide voter records. Medicaid coverage increased
turnout in the November 2008 presidential election by about 2.5 percentage
points, or roughly 7% relative to the control-group mean. The estimated effect
was larger among men and residents of Democratic counties, with suggestive
evidence that new registration contributed to the increase.

**Why it matters:** this adds a real public-benefit-to-political-participation
stage to the atlas. Coverage is not only a financial or health intervention;
in this historical experiment it was followed by a short-run change in civic
participation. But the effect did not persist through the November 2010
midterm, so public provision can alter participation without creating durable
political engagement.

**Do not overread:** this is a historical Oregon lottery among low-income
uninsured adults, not a current national Medicaid estimate. The study does not
identify whether financial relief, health, administrative contact, registration,
or another mechanism produced the turnout response. It does not show issue
choice, attribution, trust, policy delivery, household recovery, or exit.

Read the [Medicaid coverage-to-turnout finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-015.md),
[Oregon coverage route](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/oregon-medicaid-lottery-coverage-route-v1.md),
and [machine-readable health-cost record](records/us-health-cost-household-choice-end-to-end-2024-2026.json).

## 267. Public coverage can spill across household members without becoming durable household security

The Oregon Medicaid lottery also produced a modest child-enrollment spillover:
for every nine adults induced to enroll, about one additional child enrolled at
the same time. The direct adult and child effects were transitory, with the
child spillover fading over the following year.

**Why it matters:** a public benefit aimed at one eligible adult can change
another household member's coverage route. The household is therefore a real
unit of policy diffusion, not just a collection of independent applicants. But
the result also shows that diffusion can fade before it becomes durable family
security.

**Do not overread:** this is historical Oregon administrative enrollment data,
not a current national estimate. Enrollment is not completed care, adequate
coverage, or health recovery. The evidence does not identify whether
information, application assistance, household eligibility, or another process
caused the spillover, and it does not establish later trust or political action.

Read the [household coverage-spillover finding](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-016.md),
[Oregon coverage route](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/oregon-medicaid-lottery-coverage-route-v1.md),
and [machine-readable health-cost record](records/us-health-cost-household-choice-end-to-end-2024-2026.json).

## 268. Making SNAP easier to claim can raise take-up while weakening targeting

In a randomized field experiment involving 30,000 Pennsylvania adults age 60+
who were likely eligible for SNAP but not enrolled, nine-month enrollment was
about 6% in the status-quo group, 11% after eligibility information alone, and
18% after information plus application assistance.

**Why it matters:** this adds an implementation mechanism beyond “benefit
available” or “benefit received.” Information and help can increase take-up,
but the marginal enrollees induced by the outreach received lower benefits and
were less sick than the average control-group enrollee. A larger caseload can
therefore coexist with weaker targeting and continued failure to reach people
with the greatest need. The administrative price also matters: the study's
rough cost per additional enrollee was about $20 for information alone versus
$60 for information plus assistance, showing that route completion—not merely
contact—can be the expensive stage.

**Do not overread:** this is a historical Pennsylvania experiment among older
likely-eligible non-enrollees, not a current national SNAP estimate. Enrollment
does not establish food security or adequate benefits. The experiment does not
identify whether the neediest people were missed because of information,
paperwork, health, language, trust, or another barrier, and it does not follow
recipients through later security, trust, or political action.

Read the [SNAP take-up and targeting finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-021.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-snap-takeup-targeting-2018.json).

## 269. Expanding one benefit can lower the application cost of other benefits

A border-county and sub-state comparison of ACA Medicaid expansion found that
Medicaid expansion increased participation in SNAP and TANF, with a possible
positive but less precise effect on the EITC. The evidence suggests the main
change was participation conditional on eligibility—not a labor-supply response
that newly made people eligible.

**Why it matters:** public systems are not always separate silos. A Medicaid
eligibility route can reduce the marginal cost of applying for SNAP, especially
in places where SNAP take-up was initially low. One program’s doorway can alter
navigation of another program.

**Do not overread:** this is a border-based quasi-experimental study, not a
randomized individual experiment or current national estimate. Participation is
not the same as benefit adequacy, food security, or completed care. The study
does not follow one household through notices, applications, benefit amounts,
adaptation, trust, or political action.

Read the [cross-program safety-net spillover finding](projects/us-safety-net-access/findings/us-safety-net-access-022.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-medicaid-expansion-safety-net-spillovers-2014-2019.json).

## 270. A prescription event can be followed by mixed health and work transitions

Among 18,691 MEPS person records with valid three-round ordering, 724 had a
first prescription event strictly inside the R3/1-to-R4/2 window and 17,967
formed the complementary comparison group. In the later R4/2-to-R5/3 clock,
health worsening was 19.62% in the prescription-event group versus 22.16% in
the comparison group, while employment status changed 13.15% versus 9.32%.
Both nonemployment onset and nonemployment resolution were also higher in the
event group, though the transition cells were small and uncertain.

**Why it matters:** the health-cost chain has more than one clock. A person can
show no worse later health measure while also showing more movement into and
out of employment status. That is not a simple recovery story or a simple
deterioration story; the prescription event may sit inside a larger health,
work, and household adaptation process.

**Do not overread:** this is a month-ordered descriptive MEPS follow-up, not a
causal treatment effect. It does not show fill or non-fill, adherence,
treatment continuity, payment burden, hours or earnings, household care/time,
remedy receipt, trust, political action, switching, or exit. Employment status
change is not job loss, and nonemployment resolution is not restored security.

Read the [prescription-event follow-up finding](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/findings/us-household-constraint-cascade-001.md),
[method note](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/meps-prescription-event-six-month-followup-v1.md),
and [machine-readable record](records/us-meps-prescription-event-six-month-followup-2024.json).

## 271. A prescription event can sit beside payment, debt, and work pressure

Among 1,773 MEPS people with a first prescription event in the selected
R3/1-to-R4/2 window, the first recorded event had a weighted mean self/family
payment of $12.76, and 28.8% had zero recorded self/family payment. At the
later R4/2 round, the event group also had higher contextual rates of
cost-related care delay (8.09% versus 6.45%), medical debt (19.45% versus
15.17%), debt-collector contact (15.03% versus 13.08%), and nonemployment
(37.53% versus 33.42%) than the complementary round-valid group.

**Why it matters:** one health event can sit beside several different
household currencies—cash payment, delayed care, debt, collections, and work
status. These should not be collapsed into one “medical burden” score.

**Do not overread:** this is a selected, month-ordered descriptive comparison,
not a causal prescription effect. The event may follow an earlier need, the
comparison group is not a no-need control, and the recorded payment is not the
total price or household burden. Fill, adherence, treatment continuity,
household time substitution, remedy receipt, recovery, trust, political action,
switching, and exit remain unobserved.

Read the [prescription cascade finding](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/findings/us-household-constraint-cascade-002.md),
[method note](projects/us-household-constraint-cascade/meps-prescription-event-cascade-v1.md),
and [combined event/work record](records/us-meps-dated-medical-event-household-work-2024.json).

## 272. Financial access can become nearly universal without creating financial room

World Bank Global Findex estimates put U.S. account ownership at 97.0% of
adults in 2024, up from 88.0% in 2011. Female account ownership rose from
84.1% to 97.1%, bringing the measured male/female ownership gap from 7.9
percentage points to roughly zero. In the same 2024 selected-country slice,
98.0% of U.S. adults reported a mobile phone and 92.6% a smartphone.

**Why it matters:** a formal account, a connected device, and actual financial
room are three different things. The U.S. numbers show broad institutional and
device reach, but they do not show liquid savings, affordable terms, payment
safety, accessible interfaces, effective fraud correction, or the ability to
