switch providers. Across countries, the gaps point to different bottlenecks:
India has much lower smartphone reach than account ownership, while Mexico has
phone ownership far above account ownership.

**Do not overread:** these are repeated country-level survey estimates, not a
panel of the same adults or a household welfare index. Several 2024 U.S.
financial-use indicators are unavailable in the returned series; missing is not
zero. The evidence does not establish digital-finance causation, consumer
welfare, trust, political action, or practical exit.

Read the [financial-access finding](projects/us-financial-intermediation/findings/us-financial-intermediation-001.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-world-bank-global-findex-financial-room-2011-2024.json).

## 273. AI capability depends on local complements and can deepen dependency

The World Bank's 2026 development report frames AI capability as an
“adopt–adapt–advance” path rather than a simple race to build frontier models.
Infrastructure, skills, institutions, data, language fit, procurement, and
evaluation determine whether a tool becomes useful local capacity. The report
also describes hardware, data centers, models, and applications as concentrated
among a limited set of countries and firms.

**Why it matters:** a country or public agency can gain useful capability by
adopting imported systems without gaining control over the underlying value
chain. AI can therefore expand service capacity and simultaneously create
dependency, surveillance, budget, or bargaining risks. Public-sector adoption
is not the same as accessible, trusted, or effective front-end service.

**Do not overread:** this is a World Bank framework and chapter-level synthesis,
not a U.S. productivity estimate, supplier-concentration calculation, or proof
of changed geopolitical leverage. It does not measure a particular agency's
procurement, resident outcomes, worker control, trust, or exit.

Read the [AI capability and governance layer](projects/ai-work-control/world-bank-wdr2026-ai-capability-governance-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-world-bank-wdr2026-ai-capability-governance.json).

## 274. Firm capacity is a size-stratified bundle, not one readiness score

The World Bank Enterprise Survey interviewed 2,589 U.S. establishments from
May 2024 through April 2025. Small, medium, and large firms reported different
combinations of growth, training, infrastructure exposure, finance, and
constraints. Formal training was reported by 29.1% of small establishments,
59.1% of medium establishments, and 78.9% of large establishments. Large firms
also reported more electrical-outage exposure (36.2% versus 21.0% among small
firms), while bank-financed investment was non-monotonic across size.

**Why it matters:** firm size changes the conditions under which technology or
new work practices can reach workers. A large firm may have more training
capacity while also facing greater infrastructure exposure; a small firm may
face stronger skills and tax constraints. “Firm readiness” or “AI adoption”
would hide these different pathways.

**Do not overread:** this is a descriptive establishment baseline, not an AI
adoption estimate, productivity effect, worker outcome, or household result.
The indicators have separate universes, and the public profile does not supply
the full precision and nonresponse documentation needed for causal size
comparisons.

Read the [firm-capacity finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-063.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-world-bank-enterprise-survey-firm-capacity-size-2024.json).

## 275. Food hardship is graded, and families can distribute it unevenly

USDA estimates that 13.7% of U.S. households—18.3 million households—were
food insecure at some point in 2024. The severity matters: 8.3% had low food
security, while 5.4% had very low food security, where eating patterns or food
intake were disrupted. Among households with children, 18.4% were food
insecure, but adults and children did not always experience the same level;
9.1% had both adults and children food insecure. Among households with very
low food security in the prior 30 days, 56.4% participated in SNAP, school
lunch, or WIC.

**Why it matters:** food hardship is not a binary consumer outcome. Families
may protect children, reduce variety, use assistance, rely on community food,
or reduce intake. Assistance participation can coexist with very low food
security, so program use is a coping resource—not proof that the household was
restored.

**Do not overread:** these are national annual household estimates, not a
dated price, bill, or benefit episode. They do not show which need was
sacrificed, the timing of help, diet quality, health or work effects, stigma,
trust, political action, or causal program impact.

Read the [USDA food-security layer](projects/us-food-budget-security/usda-2024-food-security-layer-v1.md),
[household budget interpretation](findings/us-food-budget-path-001.md),
and [machine-readable record](records/usda-food-security-2024.json).

## 276. SNAP participation is not the same as a timely administrative route

Across 50 states and the District of Columbia, FY2025 SNAP participation had
a median of 11.3% of the population, while application timeliness had a median
of 87.12% and recertification timeliness a median of 93.75%. Participation was
nearly unrelated to application timeliness in this descriptive state-level
screen (correlation 0.033) and only weakly related to recertification timeliness
(0.211). The named counterexamples make the separation concrete: Georgia had
15.6% participation but 61.24% timely applications, while Wisconsin had 11.6%
participation and 97.22% timely applications.

**Why it matters:** the number of people receiving a benefit and the speed and
reliability of the route are different public-system currencies. A large
program can still have a slow front door, while a state with lower
participation can process applications quickly. The person’s experience also
depends on eligibility, notice, paperwork, recertification, and whether the
benefit arrives before the household need.

**Do not overread:** this is an ecological state/DC comparison. The processing
measures have different case universes and rules, and the Program Access Index
is not a strict eligible-population take-up rate. The screen does not establish
household access, food security, administrative causality, trust, political
action, or recovery.

Read the [SNAP state-route performance layer](projects/us-safety-net-access/usda-snap-state-route-performance-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/usda-snap-state-route-performance-2026.json).

## 277. Charitable food can be available while remaining hard to use

In the Urban Institute's December 2025 Well-Being and Basic Needs Survey,
16.1% of adults reported household charitable-food receipt in the prior year;
the share was 39.5% among food-insecure adults, 21.0% among adults with
children, and 29.6% among adults with a disability. Among charitable-food
participants, 51.6% reported at least one access difficulty, including food
variety, travel time, transportation, hours, safety, or treatment. Among adults
with unmet charitable-food need, the most common reported barriers included
being closed when available (45.5%), not knowing where to go (42.8%), not being
comfortable getting help (39.9%), and transportation (35.2%).

**Why it matters:** a food resource can exist without becoming a usable
consumer or household option. Access depends on time, transport, information,
variety, safety, dignity, and prior experience—not only on whether food was
distributed. Charitable food therefore sits between formal public benefit,
community infrastructure, and practical consumer exit from hunger.

**Do not overread:** these are weighted cross-sectional self-reports, with
overlapping reasons and undisclosed analytic denominators in the published
brief. Receipt is not adequacy, and a reported unfair experience is not an
adjudicated discrimination finding. The evidence does not establish causal
stigma, provider capacity, later food security, trust, or political action.

Read the [charitable-food access layer](projects/us-safety-net-access/wbns-charitable-food-access-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-urban-wbns-charitable-food-access-2019-2025.json).

## 278. SNAP can be interrupted before the household reaches a food outcome

In the Urban Institute's December 2024 Well-Being and Basic Needs Survey, 24%
of working-age adults in SNAP families reported an involuntary stop or
interruption during the prior year; 13% said they could not recertify on time.
Among the 182 respondents in the reason-specific recertification subgroup,
40% reported not having enough time after notice, 32% not receiving a notice,
22% finding the paperwork too difficult, 16% being unable to participate in an
interview, and 15% saying the office lost paperwork. Twenty-one percent
reported recertification as difficult or very difficult.

**Why it matters:** the benefit can fail at the notice, time, paperwork, or
interview stage before we observe food security. This is the administrative
version of the atlas's hidden-price idea: a household may pay with time,
attention, transportation, documentation, or a temporary benefit gap.

**Do not overread:** these are retrospective respondent reports, not linked
administrative case files. The exact denominator, gap length, benefit amount,
agency responsibility, appeal, correction, and later food security are not
observed. Help-seeking is not a success rate.

Read the [SNAP interruption layer](projects/us-safety-net-access/urban-wbns-snap-recertification-interruption-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-urban-wbns-snap-recertification-interruption-2024.json).

## 279. Disaster liquidity can save firms without proving household recovery

An NBER study follows more than 167,000 U.S. firm loan applications across
approximately 1,900 disasters, linking applications to Census records and
business-credit data for up to seven years. The current revision reports that
government recovery loans reduced firm exit and bankruptcy, increased
employment and revenue, unlocked private credit, and reduced delinquency. For
example, employer-firm exit fell 16.9 percentage points and log employment rose
0.376 in the reported Table 4 estimates; outstanding private debt also rose.

**Why it matters:** public liquidity can create a real firm-survival and local
employment effect after disaster. This is an important middle stage between a
disaster and community economic capacity—not merely a promise that assistance
exists. It also shows why firm survival is a different recovery currency from
worker security, customer affordability, owner-household recovery, or local
trust.

**Do not overread:** this is causal firm-level evidence with outcome-specific
universes. It does not show worker pay or job quality, customer prices or
service continuity, owner recovery, unequal loan access, repayment burden,
political action, or geopolitical consequences.

Read the [small-business disaster-liquidity finding](projects/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity/findings/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity-001.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-small-business-disaster-liquidity-causal-2024.json).

## 280. Expanding small-business eligibility can redistribute opportunity toward larger firms

An NBER study using administrative Census firm data and quasi-random timing
reports that expanding a small-business support rule toward larger firms
reallocated government contracts toward those larger firms. The study reports
lower revenue and more exits among affected smaller firms, especially younger,
more productive, and financially constrained firms, along with lower wages and
patenting.

**Why it matters:** a public program's eligibility rule is itself a market-
power mechanism. Support can preserve or expand aggregate capacity while
changing which firms receive contracts, which firms survive, who captures work,
and where innovation occurs. “More firms eligible” does not mean opportunity
was distributed evenly.

**Do not overread:** this is an abstract-level summary of one studied U.S.
program and design; effect sizes, standard errors, analytical counts, program
thresholds, worker hours/benefits, customer prices, and owner-household
outcomes are not retained. Larger firms may provide scale or lower prices, and
the result is not a universal rule for every support program.

Read the [small-business size-rule finding](projects/us-small-business-support-size-cutoff/findings/us-small-business-support-size-rule-001.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-small-business-support-size-rule-2026.json).

## 281. Civic participation can rebound in reach while thinning in time

The Census Bureau and AmeriCorps' 2023 Civic Engagement and Volunteering
Supplement reports that 28.3% of Americans age 16 and older formally
volunteered through an organization between September 2022 and September 2023,
up from 23.2% in 2021. Informal helping also rose to 54.2%, from 51.7% in
2019. But mean annual hours per formal volunteer fell from 96.5 in 2017 to 70
in 2023, and the median fell from 40 to 24 hours. About 18% of formal
volunteers served entirely or partly online.

**Why it matters:** more people can re-enter civic life while the typical
commitment becomes shorter, more episodic, or more distributed across online
and informal routes. Reach, neighborly help, time intensity, organizational
continuity, political efficacy, and durable collective action are separate
currencies. A participation rebound is therefore not automatically a recovery
of collective capacity.

**Do not overread:** these are repeated cross-sectional survey estimates, not a
matched-person panel or a measure of civic success. They do not show whether
volunteering changed trust, belonging, policy, voting, protest, local capacity,
or later retention. The increase may reflect recovery from pandemic lows rather
than a durable cultural shift, and the hours comparison does not identify who
could not participate because of work, care, health, transport, or money.

Read the [civic participation finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-048.md),
[civic participation layer](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/americorps-cev-2023-civic-participation-layer-v1.md),
and [machine-readable screen](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/data/americorps-cev-2023-civic-participation-screen-v1.json).

## 282. Mobility burden is not one poor-to-rich ladder

The 2024 ACS PUMS shows two different transportation pressures. Among
households with past-year income below $35,000, 22.25% had no vehicle, compared
with 3.74% among households at $75,000 or more. But among workers with a
positive commute, 30 minutes or more was reported by 32.30% of the lower-income
group and 40.98% of the higher-income group. Black workers also had a higher
30-minute-plus commute share than White workers in this unadjusted comparison
(42.64% versus 36.64%).

**Why it matters:** vehicle scarcity, commute time, affordability, reliability,
care access, and schedule control are different mobility currencies. A lower
long-commute share can reflect nonemployment, work-from-home selection, or
shorter local jobs rather than easier mobility; a longer commute can coexist
with more income or more housing and job choice. Transport therefore connects
household room, time, place, race, care, and work without reducing them to one
deprivation score.

**Do not overread:** this is a descriptive cross-sectional ACS layer. Household
income is past-year income, commute measures exclude people working from home,
and no replicate-weight intervals are calculated in this first pass. The data
do not establish transport affordability, reliability, trip purpose, missed
care or work, discrimination, or political response.

Read the [ACS mobility subgroup layer](projects/us-acs-transport-context/acs-pums-mobility-subgroups-v1.md),
[state-income transportation comparison](projects/us-acs-transport-context/acs-transport-state-income-context-v1.md),
and [machine-readable records](records/us-acs-pums-mobility-subgroups-2024.json).

## 283. Monetary policy becomes a household story through expected costs

In the BIS/NBER household study, respondents interpreted a hypothetical
federal-funds-rate increase partly as a signal of higher future inflation and
borrowing costs. Financially literate and Federal-Reserve-aware respondents
showed stronger reported durable-spending responses to the randomized
information surprise. The study also finds that inflation expectations carry
more of the perceived spending response than expected income in its
decomposition. A separate Federal Reserve household survey records the
adaptation menu that can follow pressure—substitution, reduced use, delayed
purchases, lower saving, borrowing, and extra work—but it is not the same
sample or a monetary-policy treatment.

**Why it matters:** a rate announcement is filtered through knowledge, debt,
liquidity, contracts, and prior beliefs before it becomes a household action
or a judgment about competence and fairness. The same policy can mean a higher
payment to one person, more deposit income to another, and little immediate
contract change to a third. This is a cultural and distributional mechanism,
not merely a macro transmission coefficient.

**Do not overread:** the BIS study uses a quota-based online sample and
hypothetical or intended short-run outcomes. The SHED adaptation measures come
from a separate survey and do not establish policy attribution. Neither source
shows realized spending, debt, work, health, trust, voting, or political action
for the same household after an actual rate change.

Read the [household monetary-policy finding](projects/us-household-monetary-policy/findings/us-household-monetary-policy-001.md),
[expectations-to-action refinement](projects/us-household-monetary-policy/findings/us-household-monetary-policy-003.md),
and [machine-readable record](records/us-bis-household-monetary-policy-beliefs-2026.json).

## 284. Inclusion can be broad while remaining conditional and place-specific

In an April 2026 survey of Chicago, suburban Cook County, and Lake County,
support for permanent legal status for Dreamers was higher than support for a
conditional citizenship pathway for undocumented immigrants. Chicago support
was 79% for Dreamer status and 66% for the broader pathway; in suburban Cook
County the figures were 72% and 52%, and in Lake County 72% and 61%.

**Why it matters:** public attitudes about immigration are not one inclusion
meter. People may support protection for a named, sympathetic group while
holding more conditional views about the route to citizenship, migration
levels, admission rules, or local resource allocation. Place also shapes the
political meaning of the same policy language: urban and suburban publics can
share a broad welcome while differing on the wider institutional pathway.

**Do not overread:** this is a weighted local survey, not a national estimate
or a causal place comparison. The questions measure reported policy attitudes,
not immigrant experience, service use, safety, belonging, political action, or
later institutional response. Support for one legal-status policy does not
establish support for every immigration policy.

Read the [Chicagoland immigration meaning record](records/us-chicagoland-immigration-belonging-action-2026.json)
and [local survey source](https://globalaffairs.org/research/public-opinion-survey/chicagoland-immigration-survey).

## 285. U.S. geopolitical legitimacy has three different audiences

Pew's 2026 surveys separate foreign reception, domestic judgment, and partisan
meaning. Across 36 non-U.S. countries, a median 35% said the United States
contributes to global peace and stability, compared with 57% of Americans. In
the U.S. supplement, 83% said the United States interferes in other countries'
affairs at least a fair amount, yet Republicans and Democrats differed sharply
on whether that role contributes to peace, respects freedoms, or considers
other countries' interests. Sixty-five percent of Americans said the U.S.
should consider other countries' interests even when compromise is required.

**Why it matters:** geopolitical power is not only weapons, money, or
industrial capacity. Foreign publics, domestic partisans, and state
institutions can assign different meanings to the same conduct. A country may
retain material capability while losing legitimacy or alliance trust; negative
foreign opinion does not automatically prove lost leverage, but it can matter
through cooperation, reputation, talent, aid acceptance, and domestic consent.

**Do not overread:** these are public perceptions, not validated measures of
government behavior, alliance coordination, capability, or political action.
The cross-country figure is a median across surveyed countries, not a
population-weighted global estimate. Partisan differences are descriptive and
do not establish what caused them or whether they changed policy.

Read the [global-role legitimacy layer](projects/ai-work-control/pew-2026-us-global-role-domestic-partisan-international-comparison-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-pew-global-role-legitimacy-three-audiences-2026.json).

## 286. A cash transfer can produce more electoral response than consumption response

An NBER study of Italy's 2014 80 Euro Bonus uses municipal recipient shares,
electoral returns, income and employment distributions, and voter-panel
evidence. A municipality with an average recipient share was estimated to gain
about four percentage points in incumbent vote share, while related evidence
suggested that only a minority of the transfer was spent on consumption. The
study's counterfactual indicates that targeting poorer households could have
raised the consumption response while reducing the electoral return.

**Why it matters:** a public transfer has several possible returns: immediate
consumption, debt or liquidity relief, political gratitude, blame, or later
electoral behavior. The people who create the largest material response are not
necessarily the people whose receipt creates the largest political response.
Targeting is therefore also a political-meaning and legitimacy mechanism.

**Do not overread:** this is an Italy-specific quasi-experimental study, not a
U.S. transfer estimate. The consumption and electoral estimates are not one
same-person outcome, and the counterfactual is modeled. It does not establish
that any U.S. benefit or stimulus would generate the same electoral effect, or
that a vote reflects only the transfer.

Read the [transfer-to-electoral-response finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-022.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-nber-stimulus-transfer-electoral-incentives-italy-2014.json).

## 287. A trusted efficiency label is not yet a realized household saving

ENERGY STAR reports about 90% household recognition, 45% knowingly purchasing
a certified product in the cited survey, and more than 400 million certified
products purchased in 2022. It also reports a modeled typical household saving
of about $450 per year, while product criteria specify category-level energy
and water savings. These are different stages: recognition, market reach,
technical performance, purchase, financing, installation, use, and a lower
utility bill are not interchangeable.

**Why it matters:** a public label can function as a trust signal, quality
shortcut, retailer filter, thrift cue, or climate identity marker before we
know which households can convert it into lower costs. Renters, low-liquidity
households, older homes, split incentives, rebate rules, and contractor supply
can interrupt the route from an efficient product to household room.

**Do not overread:** the program's savings and emissions figures are modeled or
category-level estimates, not a same-household bill ledger. Recognition is not
understanding, purchase is not installation, and partner reach is not equal
access. The evidence does not establish realized savings, comfort, health,
trust, or political meaning for a particular household.

Read the [efficiency-label realization layer](projects/us-energy-household-burden/energy-star-label-cost-adoption-realization-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-energy-star-consumer-efficiency-program-2023-2024.json).

## 288. Grid-security authority can expand before essential-service benefits appear

Executive Order 14421 declares a national emergency concerning the U.S.
bulk-power system and identifies foreign equipment, software, firmware, remote
access, sabotage, cyber exploitation, and supply disruption as security risks.
It connects those risks to growing electricity dependence from data centers,
AI, advanced manufacturing, and defense production, and authorizes possible
identification, monitoring, isolation, replacement, or removal of covered
equipment after specified findings and consultation. A later DOE request for
information invited utilities, suppliers, labor, governments, insurers, and
consumer representatives into the implementation discussion.

**Why it matters:** electricity is being governed simultaneously as a
household service, an industrial bottleneck, and a national-security asset.
Private technology and manufacturing demand can turn a local reliability or
rate question into federal supply-chain and state-power policy. The RFI also
shows a participation stage before we know whose alternatives influence the
final rules.

**Do not overread:** the order is a policy authority event, not evidence of an
outage, vendor fault, secure replacement, lower bill, improved reliability,
local consent, or geopolitical leverage. Domestic sourcing is not automatically
safer or cheaper. The next test is a named transaction, equipment decision,
replacement action, reliability filing, cost allocation, and affected-place
response.

Read the [grid-security authority finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-089.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-eo-14421-bulk-power-security-2026.json).

## 289. A state can have money in reserve while losing operating room

Pew's Fiscal 50 analysis reports $174.2 billion in state rainy-day funds in
fiscal 2025, equivalent to a median 47.8 days of state operations, down from
54.5 days in fiscal 2024. Including ending balances, the median total cushion
was 91.6 days. The range was wide: Wyoming reported 320.2 rainy-day days and
New Jersey zero; 24 states increased capacity while 26 decreased it.

**Why it matters:** a reserve balance is not the same as usable public
capacity. States differ in revenue volatility, spending needs, legal rules,
federal aid, debt, political authority, and willingness to draw funds. A state
can have substantial money on the books while losing operating days, or spend
down a balance on debt reduction or investment without an immediate service
failure. Fiscal capacity is a buffer stage between a shock and the public
route a household actually experiences.

**Do not overread:** these are state-level fiscal comparisons, not a national
cash pool or a household benefit measure. They do not show which services were
protected, who paid through taxes or cuts, how quickly a state responded to a
disaster, or whether residents trusted the decision. The next test is a matched
state episode joining reserves, revenue, political rules, public action,
service delivery, and household or place outcomes.

Read the [state fiscal-buffer/service-capacity layer](projects/us-fiscal-news-household/pew-state-fiscal-buffer-service-capacity-layer-v1.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-pew-state-rainy-day-capacity-fy2025.json).

## 290. A refund program can report high aggregate reach while individual recovery remains hidden

The FTC's 2024 refund report says the Amazon Flex Zelle distribution produced
2,806 successful payments and raised reported participation from 90.3% to
92.3% of eligible drivers. It also reports that 130,952 drivers had received
full compensation totaling more than $58.5 million by December 31, 2024. The
current FTC route sends Zelle payments to eligible drivers who did not cash an
earlier check.

**Why it matters:** this adds a stronger receipt-stage signal to the worker-
remedy atlas. The institution reports not only a fund and payment rounds, but
an aggregate successful-payment measure and a route aimed at earlier
non-cashers. Yet reach is still path-dependent: a driver has to be identified,
contacted, and able to use the payment channel before later recovery can be
counted. Aggregate receipt can improve without revealing who remains unpaid,
what amount was adequate, or whether the work relationship became safer or
more optional.

**Do not overread:** the participation and full-compensation figures are
administrator-reported aggregate measures, not an independent driver-level
audit. The public record does not expose the current eligible denominator,
individual payment date or amount, residual loss, household outcome,
continued or alternative work, trust, or exit. The 2024 report and current
2026 route should not be treated as a linked longitudinal panel of the same
drivers.

Read the [Amazon Flex refund follow-up recheck](projects/ai-work-control/amazon-flex-refund-followup-recheck-2026-09-19.md),
[earlier tip-refund implementation finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-087.md),
and [machine-readable follow-up record](projects/ai-work-control/data/amazon-flex-driver-tip-refund-followup-2026.json).

## 291. An appeal stay can create an interim income floor without proving restored work access

In the Waheed matter, the Fair Work Commission found the platform
deactivation unfair, ordered reactivation within seven days, and ordered lost
remuneration in principle while leaving the final amount to be determined. A
later consent order stayed those orders pending appeal but required Rasier
Pacific to pay $259.38 per week into the worker's nominated bank account from
28 May 2026, without seeking repayment of those interim payments.

**Why it matters:** legal remedies have their own implementation clock. An
appeal can pause reactivation and final compensation while still creating a
conditional interim income obligation. That is a meaningful protective stage
between adjudication and lived recovery, but it is not the same as money
arriving, the account becoming usable, or the worker returning to work.

**Do not overread:** the public record does not show interim-payment receipt,
final lost-pay quantum, appeal outcome, actual account restoration, continued
work, alternative work, household recovery, trust, or switching. The $259.38
is an order condition, not a verified worker-level outcome. This is one
Australian platform case, not a prevalence estimate or a U.S. worker result.

Read the [Waheed appeal-stay follow-up](projects/ai-work-control/uber-waheed-appeal-stay-followup-2026-09-18.md)
and [machine-readable case ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/uber-waheed-interim-payment-obligation-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 292. Digital payment convenience can coexist with weak dispute control before redress is visible

The CFPB's January 2025 enforcement action against Block, the operator of
Cash App, described fraud-related losses and failures in dispute investigation
and security practices. The order required operational changes and up to
$120 million in consumer redress, alongside a separate $55 million civil
penalty. The current public payment-by-case listing did not identify a Block
or Cash App distribution in the recheck used for this atlas.

**Why it matters:** a digital payment system can make sending and receiving
money easy while making the correction path institutionally dependent. The
consumer may report a loss, but the public record can still stop at the
regulator's finding, control requirements, and redress pool rather than show
whether an account was corrected or money returned. This connects consumer
power, platform governance, financial security, and trust.

**Do not overread:** the enforcement record is not a population fraud rate,
and the absence of a public payment listing does not prove that no payment
has occurred. The record does not show individual eligibility, payment or
receipt, residual loss, account correction, repeat effort, continued use,
switching, trust, or exit. A formal redress pool is not verified consumer
recovery.

Read the [Cash App fraud-redress case record](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/cfpb-cash-app-fraud-redress-case-record-v1.md)
and [machine-readable case ledger](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/data/cfpb-cash-app-fraud-redress-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 293. Extended disaster assistance can preserve a recovery route without proving durable housing

California reported that FEMA housing support for survivors of the 2025 Los
Angeles wildfires was extended through July 2027, with more than 35,000
households reported assisted and more than $177 million awarded as of June
2026. A separate CalAssist mortgage bridge reported assistance for more than
1,100 homeowners, with program limits of up to 12 months of mortgage payments
and up to $100,000 while insurance and rebuilding processes continued.

**Why it matters:** disaster recovery has a persistence stage. Extending a
support window and bridging mortgage payments can keep a household connected
to a housing or rebuilding route after the initial award, while also making
residual need, documentation, lender timing, and recontact part of the lived
recovery burden. Assistance duration is therefore a distinct currency from
repair completion, return, or stable housing.

**Do not overread:** these are aggregate program reports, not a linked
household panel. They do not establish individual payment receipt, award
adequacy, insurance release, mortgage balance, completed repair, permanent
housing, work or health recovery, trust, complaint, or move/stay choice. An
extension is not proof that a household remained housed or recovered.

Read the [wildfire recovery-persistence event ledger](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/data/california-la-wildfire-recovery-persistence-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [earlier FEMA assistance-visibility finding](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/findings/us-repeat-energy-crises-003.md).

## 294. Worker safety voice can expose public risk while making the speaker economically vulnerable

On a post-Hurricane Beryl hotel-repair project in Texas, workers raised
concerns about missing asbestos training and certification, inadequate
protective equipment, and illegal asbestos dumping. OSHA investigated and
found the workers' terminations retaliatory under three environmental
statutes, ordering reinstatement and more than $200,000 in back wages,
interest, compensatory damages, and punitive damages.

**Why it matters:** worker voice is also public infrastructure. A complaint
about a workplace can carry information about environmental exposure and
community safety, but retaliation can shift the immediate cost of that
information onto the workers through lost income and job insecurity. The case
links labor control, disaster rebuilding, environmental governance, and public
trust without assuming that a formal complaint automatically protects the
complainant.

**Do not overread:** this is one OSHA order, not a prevalence estimate or a
general result about disaster work. The public record does not show payment
receipt, actual reinstatement, safe return, corrected site conditions,
deterrence, household recovery, later worker action, trust, or exit. An order
is not proof that the public hazard was fully repaired.

Read the [OSHA asbestos-whistleblower event ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/osha-texas-asbestos-whistleblower-remedy-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 295. A worker's question can trigger payroll correction while payment reachability remains open

At Southern Cross Care in New South Wales, an employee reportedly asked about
overtime. The employer then initiated a self-audit, self-reported payroll
non-compliance to the Fair Work Ombudsman, and entered an enforceable
undertaking. The organization reported back-paying 3,603 employees a total of
$10.14 million including interest and superannuation, while the undertaking
required continued efforts to locate and pay affected workers and to improve
payroll controls.

**Why it matters:** worker voice can be an organizational detection system,
not only an individual complaint route. A single question can surface a wider
payroll problem and produce firm-level correction, monitoring, and a remedy
route. But “back-paid” still has a reachability boundary: the institution must
identify, contact, and pay affected workers, and unclaimed wages may be
remitted elsewhere if workers cannot be located.

**Do not overread:** the official release is not an independent worker-level
payment audit or a sector-wide estimate. It does not show each worker's
amount, payment instrument or date, adequacy, household effect, later
employment, recurrence, trust, or exit. The self-audit and reported aggregate
back-pay do not prove durable compliance or recovery for every worker.

Read the [Southern Cross Care underpayment reachability ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/fwo-southern-cross-care-nsw-underpayment-reachability-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 296. Wage theft can use employment-status and tax compliance pressure before formal back-pay repairs part of the loss

The Fair Work Ombudsman reported that a Nepalese migrant worker employed as a
part-time assistant accountant was unpaid for roughly the first ten weeks and
was then required to transfer money to accounts controlled by the owner so the
company could return part of it as wages and satisfy tax and superannuation
obligations. The official release reports total underpayment of $40,164.49,
full back-payment to the worker, and court penalties of $148,000 against the
company and $29,000 against its owner.

**Why it matters:** labor control can operate through paperwork, migration
status, tax compliance, and the worker's need to keep the job—not only through
an hourly wage. The case shows how a worker can carry financial and legal risk
inside the employment relationship, then reach a formal remedy that repairs
the reported underpayment without automatically restoring time, security,
trust, or bargaining power.

**Do not overread:** this is one adjudicated case, not a prevalence estimate
about migrant workers or wage theft. “Paid in full” is an institutional
report, not a worker-signed receipt or adequacy assessment. The public record
does not show payment date or net amount, household effects, later employment,
recurrence, health, trust, or exit.

Read the [Innovative Associates back-pay event ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/fwo-innovative-associates-cashback-backpay-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 297. Utility pressure and child-care work constraints can stack beside next-month renter hardship

In a same-person November-to-December SIPP screen, renters who reported
utility-payment difficulty and child-care arrangements that prevented work had
following-month mortgage-hardship and food-insecurity estimates of 72.8% and
64.6%, respectively. The comparison group of renters with utility difficulty
but no reported child-care work prevention was at 58.0% and 50.2%. The
prevention cell contained only 12 valid person pairs; the broader screen had
2,600 November-to-December pairs and used 240 Fay-BRR replicate weights.

**Why it matters:** household pressure can stack across bills, care, work, and
housing rather than arrive as one isolated cost. The same-person frame puts a
material condition, a care/work constraint, and later security indicators on a
shared timeline. It also makes the practical question sharper: when care is
unreachable, what does the household sacrifice to keep work, housing, food,
or utilities going?

**Do not overread:** the utility and food/housing measures can be
reference-period or repeated fields, while the child-care measure is an annual
fall report copied onto the following record. This is an adjacent-month
descriptive joint-constraint screen, not a dated bill or shutoff event, a
child-care effect, a tenure effect, or a household-weighted prevalence
estimate. The small prevention cell has wide intervals, and the data do not
show the provider route, exact time lost, payment decision, remedy, recovery,
trust, political action, or exit.

Read the [SIPP utility-care following-outcomes bridge](projects/us-household-calendar-integration/sipp-utility-care-following-outcomes-bridge-v1.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-sipp-utility-care-following-outcomes-2024.json).

## 298. Staying with a bank can save effort while quietly giving up interest

The NBER study of more than 900 banks finds that only about 5–15% of
depositors open a new account in a given year and estimates that “sleepy”
deposits account for 57% of average deposit-franchise value. Related consumer
guidance and research describe the practical links that make switching costly:
direct deposit, automatic bills, account setup, branch access, service history,
and the risk of an error during the move.

**Why it matters:** non-switching is not automatically irrational or harmful.
A familiar bank can provide safety, convenience, and a way to resolve a
problem. But the same household infrastructure that makes an account useful
can make a better rate difficult to pursue. The customer's foregone interest,
the bank's stable funding, competition, and local financial-system stability
can therefore move together without distributing value equally.

**Do not overread:** the evidence does not show one customer's net loss after
fees, taxes, service quality, fraud protection, balance, or switching time. A
new account is not a complete transfer of deposits, and easier switching could
help customers while also moving funds toward larger banks. The next test is an
account-level route linking rates, balances, fees, automatic payments,
switching attempts, service use, errors, and later household outcomes.

Read the [bank-depositor inertia finding](findings/us-bank-depositor-inertia-matched-evidence-001.md),
[NBER account study](https://www.nber.org/papers/w34267), and [FDIC switching guidance](https://www.fdic.gov/consumer-resource-center/2024-03/thinking-about-moving-another-bank).

## 299. Health costs travel through care choice, household adaptation, and contested legitimacy

The health-cost evidence now forms a bounded multi-stage bridge. In the 2025
SHED, 26% of adults reported skipping some needed treatment because of cost,
with higher rates among lower-income and uninsured adults. Linked follow-up
records show that persistent care-forgoing can sit beside borrowing, reduced
savings, and delayed purchases. Other layers show that coverage can increase
care use and reduce financial exposure, while medical-debt relief can improve
credit access without repairing average health or financial wellness.

The political endpoint is also time-dependent: the Oregon Medicaid lottery
increased turnout in the 2008 presidential election by about 2.5 percentage
points, but the effect did not persist through the 2010 midterm. A health-cost
or coverage experience can therefore reach household adaptation, institutional
judgment, and political action on different clocks.

**Why it matters:** the atlas can now follow the health-cost problem farther
than “people skipped care” or “coverage increased.” Cost can become forgone
care, debt, reduced savings, outside help, work adaptation, credit exposure,
trust, or a short-lived civic response. These are connected surfaces, not one
health-burden score.

**Do not overread:** the bridge combines SHED panels, MEPS comparisons,
administrative coverage records, credit data, randomized studies, and surveys
with different units, dates, and estimands. It does not follow one dated bill
through care choice, remedy receipt, recovery, trust, and exit. The next valid
test is a same-person or same-household health-cost episode with coverage,
alternatives, payment, care completion, adaptation, institutional response,
later health/security, and directly measured meaning or action.

Read the [consolidated health-cost synthesis](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/findings/us-health-cost-household-choice-end-to-end-001.md)
and [health-cost episode acquisition protocol](projects/us-health-cost-household-choice/health-cost-episode-acquisition-protocol-v1.md).

## 300. Reported back-pay receipt can close one remedy stage without proving recovery

In the Atkins Freight Services enforcement follow-up, the Fair Work Ombudsman
reports that all ten named truck drivers received back-payments after an appeal
was dismissed and approximately $468,000 held in trust was released. The
reported payments ranged from $8,012 to $91,566; the official record also
reports $374,487 in ordered back pay plus superannuation and interest.

**Why it matters:** this is a meaningful positive comparator in a portfolio
where many records stop at an order, distribution, or reactivation. The case
closes an institutionally reported worker-level receipt stage. It shows why
“remedy received” should be kept separate from “remedy ordered” or “funds
released.”

**Do not overread:** the receipt is reported by the enforcement institution,
not independently verified here. The record does not show whether the amount
was adequate after the delay, what happened to each household, later work,
health, trust, recurrence, or exit. One small named case is not an enforcement
success rate or a general worker-recovery estimate.

Read the [Atkins worker-receipt event ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/fwo-atkins-truck-driver-backpay-receipt-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [official Fair Work Ombudsman follow-up](https://www.fairwork.gov.au/newsroom/media-releases/2018-media-releases/january-2018/20180117-atkins-appeal-dismissed).

## 301. A repeat refund can reach people who already cleared the first access hurdle

The FTC reports that ACRO Services sent a second refund round to 5,685 people
who had accepted the first payment, totaling more than $6.2 million. The
sequence is therefore not simply “harm → refund.” It is:

```text
consumer harm
  -> first refund route
  -> first payment accepted
  -> second check sent
  -> second check cashed [open]
  -> debt/account correction and restored financial room [open]
```

**Why it matters:** repeat remedies can be administratively sensible while
also selecting for people who had notice, a usable payment channel, the time to
complete the first route, and enough trust or ability to accept the payment.
People who missed, rejected, or could not use the first route may disappear
from later distributions. Reachability is therefore part of the remedy, not a
minor administrative detail.

**Do not overread:** the FTC page does not identify the same recipients across
all stages, individual amounts, second-check cashing, remaining debt, account
correction, household effect, trust, switching, or exit. A second check sent
to prior acceptors is not a recovery rate or proof that the underlying debt
problem was repaired.

Read the [ACRO repeat-refund event ledger](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/data/ftc-acro-second-refund-event-ledger-v1.json)
and [FTC ACRO refund page](https://www.ftc.gov/enforcement/refunds/acro-services-refunds).

## 302. A restitution schedule can exist while worker receipt remains unobservable

NYC’s Department of Consumer and Worker Protection reports Fair Workweek
settlements requiring more than $1.5 million from Salz Management and more than
$277,000 from Theory for more than 830 named fast-food and retail workers.
Payment schedules, direct-deposit or address-update instructions, and a notice
administrator are public. The record does not publish which workers received,
cashed, or had a payment returned.

The same route includes a proposed unclaimed-restitution fund for situations
where a respondent is insolvent, dissolved, bankrupt, nonresponsive, or unable
to satisfy an obligation. That proposal recognizes a practical failure between
legal entitlement and usable payment, but it is not itself an operating fund or
a recipient outcome.

**Why it matters:** remedy infrastructure has its own stages: settlement,
eligibility, notice, contact update, payment schedule, delivery, cashing,
adequacy, and later security. Publishing a route can shift address, banking,
documentation, and timing work onto the worker while leaving reachability
unknown.

**Do not overread:** this is an official settlement and proposed-rule record,
not worker-level receipt data. It does not show payment amounts by worker,
returned or reissued checks, schedule protection after settlement, household
effects, trust, later employment, or exit. The proposed fund cannot be counted
as money delivered.

Read the [NYC restitution reachability ledger](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/data/nyc-dcwp-restitution-reachability-event-ledger-v1.json),
[NYC settlement announcement](https://www.nyc.gov/site/dca/news/028-26/mamdani-administration-secures-nearly-2m-restitution-800-fast-food-retail-workers),
and [proposed unclaimed-restitution fund](https://rules.cityofnewyork.us/rule/establishment-of-unclaimed-restitution-fund/).

## 303. Institutional completion can be reported without borrower-level recovery

The CFPB’s Wells Fargo private-student-loan servicing consent order required at
least $410,000 in borrower relief, correction of inaccurate credit reporting,
servicing changes, and a redress plan that included locating affected
consumers and following up on returned or uncashed payments. In 2022, the CFPB
terminated the order after reporting that Wells Fargo had fulfilled its
obligations, including remediation and redress for specified consumers.

**Why it matters:** this is a stronger institutional endpoint than an order
that merely promises future action. It shows a regulator can record formal
completion after a remedy system has operated. But “the institution fulfilled
the order” is still a different field from “this borrower received the right
amount, had the account corrected, and recovered financially.”

**Do not overread:** the public record retained here does not identify each
borrower’s payment date, cashing, account state, residual loss, adequacy,
later borrowing, trust, switching, or exit. Order termination is not a
borrower-level recovery rate.

Read the [Wells Fargo redress reachability ledger](projects/us-customer-automation-recourse/data/cfpb-wells-fargo-redress-reachability-event-ledger-v1.json),
[original CFPB consent order](https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/2016-CFPB-0013Wells_Fargo_Bank_N.A.--_Consent_Order.pdf),
and [2022 termination order](https://files.consumerfinance.gov/f/documents/cfpb_wells-fargo-na-2016_consent-order-termination_2022-12.pdf).

## 304. A domestic GaN manufacturing route can be announced before shipment, qualification, or material independence

GlobalFoundries and Navitas describe a US-manufactured Gen 5 GaN route at the
Burlington, Vermont, facility for AI infrastructure, performance computing,
industrial electrification, and national-security applications. The September
2026 announcement documents a domestic foundry path, with first-wafer
shipments described as beginning in September, internal samples planned for
October, and strategic-customer samples expected before year-end. A separate
Navitas investor update places the broader US 8-inch ramp in a 2027–28 target
window.

The sequence is therefore:

```text
firm partnership
  -> US foundry route
  -> scheduled first wafers and samples
  -> customer qualification [open]
  -> scaled production and system deployment [open]
  -> reduced supply dependence or strategic autonomy [open]
```

**Why it matters:** this is a useful firm/infrastructure/geopolitical
realization stage. A domestic downstream manufacturing route can be real and
strategically framed before it becomes delivered product, qualified customer
input, scaled output, or reduced reliance on imported wafers, substrates,
refined gallium, or other production stages. The headline wording says
“delivers,” while the body describes first shipments as beginning in September;
the record therefore preserves a schedule and wording conflict rather than
coding completed shipment.

**Do not overread:** quantities, customer identity, shipment receipt, yield,
qualification, revenue, scaled 200mm output, end-system deployment, worker
outcomes, upstream material provenance, recycling, and import displacement are
not established. A US fab route is not by itself supply-chain autonomy or
geopolitical leverage.

Read the [Navitas–GlobalFoundries Gen 5 GaN realization ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/navitas-globalfoundries-us-gan-first-shipment-schedule-event-ledger-v1.json).

## 305. A vehicle-data privacy order can create formal control without proving usable privacy

The FTC's finalized January 2026 order concerning GM and OnStar requires
consumer-control routes for connected-vehicle data. The order describes a way
for U.S. consumers to request a copy of their data, seek deletion, disable
precise geolocation collection where the vehicle supports it, and opt out of
geolocation and driving-behavior data collection subject to stated exceptions.

This is a distinct platform/data stage because it turns privacy from a general
principle into formal control surfaces attached to a real product relationship.
It still does not show that customers found the controls, used them, obtained
complete deletion, avoided downstream use, or changed vehicles or trust.

**Why it matters:** “A route exists” is not the same as “the user can reach
it,” “the firm completes it,” or “the data is gone.” Disabling collection may
also carry service, safety, convenience, or vehicle-function trade-offs. A
privacy order is therefore a formal remedy design, not automatically a
restored privacy outcome.

**Do not overread:** this is one named enforcement order, not a prevalence
estimate or representative customer study. It does not report request volume,
response time, completion, deletion verification, residual data, trust,
switching, or mobility outcomes.

Read the [GM/OnStar data-control finding](projects/us-digital-habits-attention/findings/us-digital-habits-attention-009.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-ftc-gm-onstar-data-control-order-2026.json).

## 306. A completed transmission project can make infrastructure capacity visible before household savings are verified

The New York Power Authority reports that Smart Path Connect completed and
energized three new substations, modified six existing substations, and built
45 miles of transmission lines in the North Country. Linked with the earlier
Smart Path project, the announcement describes nearly 200 uninterrupted miles
of upgraded 345-kilovolt transmission and reports more than $438 million in
annual savings for New Yorkers.

This is a stronger infrastructure realization stage than an award, forecast,
or approved route: the source reports physical completion and energization. But
the savings claim is still a system-level or program-level outcome. The public
record does not show which households or rate classes paid less, experienced
fewer outages, or received equal benefit.

**Why it matters:** infrastructure has at least three different clocks:
planning need, physical realization, and lived distribution. Smart Path reaches
the second and reports a system-level version of the third, but does not identify
the household distribution. Physical completion is not automatically universal
affordability, equal benefit, or geopolitical leverage.

**Do not overread:** this is a public project announcement, not an independent
household-rate or outage evaluation. The reported savings have no customer
denominator, counterfactual, rate-class allocation, or durability measure in
the retained source.

Read the [Smart Path Connect realization finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-122.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-nypa-smart-path-connect-realization-2026.json).

## 307. An alliance can translate a hybrid incident into posture and leadership changes before deterrence is verified

In a September 2026 statement, NATO described Germany's leadership in a
stronger European alliance, including a new German armoured brigade permanently
stationed in Lithuania, a new command role for the First German-Netherlands
Corps on NATO's eastern flank, and German aircraft and ships supporting
deterrence and defence. The statement also described alliance solidarity with
Germany after a reported Russian hybrid attack at Leipzig airport and linked
the response to continued support for Ukraine.

This adds a current institutional-response stage beyond a generic defense
budget or procurement announcement. The alliance is publicly translating a
reported incident into force posture, command, leadership, and political
messaging. It still does not prove deterrence, public consent, adversary
adaptation, or durable unity.

**Why it matters:** a geopolitical response has separate clocks: incident,
interpretation, posture, operation, public legitimacy, and changed external
behavior. NATO's statement reaches interpretation and posture; it does not
close the later stages.

**Do not overread:** this is one official alliance statement, not an independent
incident investigation or public-opinion survey. The reported hybrid attack,
brigade readiness, adversary adaptation, and durable alliance behavior remain
open.

Read the [Germany/NATO response finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-123.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-nato-germany-hybrid-response-alliance-posture-2026.json).

## 308. A transmission refund can show regulatory correction before household recovery

New York's Department of Public Service announced a settlement requiring a
transmission developer to return more than $13 million, plus interest, to New
York ratepayers in 2026. The agency said its review found apparently abnormal
operating and maintenance expenses and proposal-preparation costs collected
from customers. The settlement also created a forward-looking rule for future
proposal costs.

An implementation explanation says the expected refund would flow through
monthly transmission-charge credits on a load-ratio-share basis, while an
individual utility system might not see a separately itemized invoice credit.
That is an allocation mechanism, not verified household receipt.

This adds a different infrastructure-to-consumer stage: a regulator can correct
the cost system before the household-level result is visible. It still does
not show who receives a bill credit or payment, when it appears, whether it is
adequate, or whether household affordability, trust, or switching changes.

**Why it matters:** a refund amount is not a recovery rate. The denominator,
allocation, timing, receipt, and later durability are part of the consumer
outcome.

Read the [transmission ratepayer-refund finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-124.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-ny-psc-transmission-ratepayer-refund-2025-2026.json).

## 309. A centralized privacy request can scale control before deletion is verified

California's DROP system lets one eligible consumer send a deletion and
do-not-sell request to more than 600 data brokers. CalPrivacy reported more
than 325,000 sign-ups and submissions by July 2026, then more than 500,000
registrants and 654 participating brokers in an August follow-up. About 25%
of brokers had reported processing deletion requests after the August 1
processing start, with hashed intake and broker-level statuses such as
deleted, exempted, opted out of sale, not found, and pending. The current DROP
route says brokers must process requests at least every 45 days, while status
updates can take up to 90 days to appear; the result therefore has a temporal
interface as well as a legal one.

This makes privacy control more reachable and shows an implementation response,
but not necessarily a completed privacy outcome. The public record still does
not show the consumer-level match rate, deletion rate, residual data,
re-collection, trust, or whether people changed behavior because of the route.

Read the [DROP privacy-control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-125.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-calprivacy-drop-data-broker-deletion-2026.json).

## 310. AI companions are becoming a social-support surface before substitution is proven

A September 2026 survey from Elon University's Imagining the Digital Future
Center reports that 27% of internet-using U.S. adults have social interactions
with AI systems. Among AI companion users, 31% call the AI a friend, 59% say it
provides needed support, 53% turn to it for advice about difficult situations,
and 51% say it helps when they are stressed or upset. Emotional meaning is also
visible: 39% sometimes tell AI things they would not tell other people, and 38%
would feel a personal loss if the interaction ended.

This shows a new cultural-support surface. It does not prove that AI replaces
human relationships, reduces loneliness, creates dependence, improves
wellbeing, or changes later behavior.

Read the [AI-companion social-meaning finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-126.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-ai-companion-social-meaning-support-2026.json).

## 311. NATO medical readiness is being rehearsed with civilian institutions before crisis performance is known

NATO's Vigorous Warrior 2026 exercise in Estonia brought together 38 Allied
and partner nations and included Estonian hospitals, health, rescue, police,
border-guard, and ambulance institutions. It tested medical support,
casualty evacuation, underground treatment facilities, unmanned evacuation,
and protection of medical sites.

This is a real exercise and civil-military coordination stage, not just a
defense commitment. It still does not show how the network would perform in a
real crisis, whether patients would receive continuous care, who would absorb
the local burden, or whether the exercise changes an adversary's behavior.

Read the [NATO medical-readiness finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-127.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-nato-vigorous-warrior-civil-military-medical-readiness-2026.json).

## 312. Privacy control is measurable at the request desk before it is verified in a person's data or life

Walmart's 2025 California privacy metrics report 3,199 deletion requests,
with 1,962 completed and 1,237 denied, and a 22-day median completion time.
The company separately reports access and sale-opt-out requests, with their
own completion and denial counts. This makes verification, administrative
completion, denial, and timing visible as distinct stages.

It still does not independently verify that every relevant data element was
removed, that the result was adequate, or that the person changed provider,
trust, or behavior. Sale opt-out, access, and deletion are different forms of
control and should not be collapsed.

Read the [Walmart privacy-request finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-128.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-walmart-ccpa-privacy-request-completion-2025-2026.json).

## 313. People can control who reaches them more reliably than they can make a platform act

A 2026 study of 1,067 usable UK adult responses found that more than 80% had
used at least one platform safety feature. Among people aware of the tools,
more than 85% had unfollowed and more than 80% had blocked someone, with more
than 60% always satisfied with those outcomes. Reporting was a weaker route:
about 60% had used it, but only 18% were always satisfied. Feed-control tools
also had lower satisfaction, around 30–35%.

The result separates direct user control from institutional responsiveness.
Blocking or unfollowing can stop a particular account; reporting asks the
platform to judge and act. Safety work is therefore both agency and labor
transferred to users. This is comparative UK evidence, not a US prevalence
estimate, and it does not prove reduced harm, improved wellbeing, trust, or
durable exit.

Read the [platform-safety control finding](projects/ai-work-control/findings/ai-work-control-129.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-uk-platform-safety-control-satisfaction-2026.json).

## 314. Financial strain can weaken political efficacy before it becomes a final voting decision

CIRCLE and When We All Vote surveyed 5,549 US adults ages 18–29 before the
2026 midterms. Six in ten said they sometimes or often had trouble meeting
basic financial needs. Financially struggling youth were less likely to say
voting matters, less likely to have voted in 2024, and less likely to say they
were extremely likely to vote in 2026. The relationship is not simply apathy:
housing, groceries, health care, and wages were central concerns that could
also motivate action when young people saw a credible route to improvement.

This shows a material-pressure → efficacy and stated-readiness bridge. It does
not establish causality, final turnout, vote choice, or which mechanism—time,
resources, health, information, belonging, or distrust—did the work.

Read the [youth financial-strain and political-engagement finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-049.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-circle-youth-financial-strain-political-engagement-2026.json).

## 315. Family can be a wider social infrastructure than the household

Pew's June 2025 survey of 6,871 U.S. adults, including 4,271 Black adults,
asked who people consider family and how close they feel to different family
members. Among Black adults who had the relevant relationship, 73% reported
being extremely or very close to a non-relative they considered family, 77% to
a spouse or partner, 66% to a parent, and 63% to a sibling. Black adults were
also more likely than non-Black adults to report closeness to a grandparent
(48% versus 33%), cousin (42% versus 20%), or aunt/uncle (36% versus 19%).

This is not just a family-values statistic. A cousin, close friend, church or
community tie, or extended family member may be part of the actual route to
advice, transportation, childcare, money, emotional support, or institutional
navigation—even when that support is not a formal benefit or a co-resident
household resource.

The evidence does not show delivered aid, burden, adequacy, reciprocity, or
durability. Closeness can coexist with obligation, unequal labor, conflict, or
limited material capacity.

Read the [family-network finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-050.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-pew-black-family-networks-support-2025-2026.json).

## 316. More families combine paid work, but the trade is not one-dimensional

Pew's analysis of Census data reports that in 2025, 52% of different-sex
couples with children under 18 had two full-time working parents, up from 31%
in 1975. In a separate survey of 2,242 working parents, 83% of parents in
two-full-time-parent families said the arrangement helped their finances, but
only 49% said it helped children's well-being and 22% said it hurt it.

This shows that the work-family bargain has multiple currencies. Two incomes
may create financial room while reducing schedule slack, increasing care
coordination, or changing how parents judge family life. The evidence does not
show causality, childcare quality, unpaid hours, job security, or whether a
family had a real alternative.

Read the [working-parent arrangement finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-051.md)
and [machine-readable records](records/us-pew-working-parent-work-family-boundary-2026.json).

## 317. People use different information systems for different parts of life

In a March 2026 Pew survey, 37% of U.S. adults named news media as their main
source for healthcare-system information, compared with 18% government sources,
13% social media, and 13% friends or family. For foreign policy, news media
rose to 51%. For Social Security, news media and government sources were tied
at 31% each. For federal regulation of technology and media companies, 44%
named news media, 10% government sources, and 19% social media.

This means there is no single practical information environment. Source choice
varies by topic and may shape what people understand as credible, usable, or
politically meaningful. The survey does not measure accuracy, trust,
persuasion, algorithmic exposure, or later action.

Read the [topic-specific information-source finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-052.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-pew-information-source-topic-diversity-2026.json).

## 318. Alliance legitimacy is real but not the same as operational support

Pew's 2026 survey found a median of 65% favorable views of NATO across 13
member states, versus 30% unfavorable. Support was not uniform: 59% in both
Greece and Turkey viewed NATO unfavorably. In the United States, 75% of
Democrats and 42% of Republicans expressed a favorable view of the alliance.

This adds a public-legitimacy stage to the geopolitical lane. Formal alliance
commitments, exercises, and procurement can coexist with national and partisan
divides over the meaning and cost of the alliance. The survey does not show
support for a specific operation, burden sharing, readiness, deterrence, or
crisis behavior.

Read the [NATO public-legitimacy finding](projects/us-cost-trust-politics/findings/us-cost-trust-politics-053.md)
and [machine-readable record](records/us-pew-nato-public-legitimacy-2026.json).

## 319. Digital safety recourse can become enforceable without becoming lived safety

The TAKE IT DOWN Act gives covered platforms a formal removal route for
nonconsensual intimate images and known identical copies, sets a 48-hour
response obligation after a valid request, and gives people an FTC complaint
route when a platform fails to provide the process or act.

This adds a new stage to the digital-power map: safety is no longer only a
platform promise; it can become a legally enforceable user claim. But the
public record still does not show whether a particular request was valid,
whether the image and copies were actually removed, whether the material
returned, or whether the survivor's safety, wellbeing, trust, or platform exit
improved. Formal speed and escalation are not the same as lived recovery.

Read the [platform-removal record](records/us-ftc-take-it-down-act-platform-removal-2026.json),
the [FTC announcement](https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2026/05/ftc-begins-enforcing-take-it-down-act),
and the [FTC compliance guidance](https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/resources/complying-take-it-down-act).

## 320. Public benefit eligibility can become an entry ticket to energy infrastructure

DOE's Clean Energy Connector uses LIHEAP participation to help identify
households for community-solar subscriptions. Households opt in through a
subscription manager, and qualifying subscriptions must provide at least a 20%
bill-credit discount after participation costs are accounted for.

This is more than a generic clean-energy promise: it creates a concrete bridge
from public-benefit eligibility to private or distributed infrastructure,
consumer choice, and a required bill-credit term. But the public page does not
show how many households completed enrollment, what appeared on an actual bill,
whether subscription charges offset the credit, or whether savings persisted.
Eligibility, opt-in, bill credit, net savings, energy burden, and exit remain
separate stages.

Read the [Clean Energy Connector record](records/us-doe-clean-energy-connector-low-income-bill-credit-2024-2026.json)
and the [DOE program page](https://www.energy.gov/cmei/systems/clean-energy-connector).

## 321. Public energy infrastructure can reach households while distribution stays hidden

Washington, D.C.'s Solar for All program reports nearly 10,000 households
served, about $500 in average annual electric-bill savings, and roughly $5
million in total annual utility-bill savings. Its program terms describe free
subscriptions and credits applied through Pepco billing. The current terms add
the route conditions: required documents and Pepco enrollment precede
activation, credits may take up to 60 days to begin after Pepco receives the
updated enrollment, the benefit is a bill credit rather than cash, and account
deactivation can cancel the subscription and require reapplication.

This is stronger than an infrastructure announcement: a public program reports
household reach and a material savings endpoint. But it remains an aggregate
program claim. The public materials do not show each customer's credit,
subscription timing, net charges, arrears, savings distribution, continued
eligibility, or whether the household's energy burden actually fell over time.
The terms clarify the interface and its continuity risk, but they still do not
show a named household's enrollment date, actual credit line, net savings,
arrears, or durable energy security.

Read the [DC Solar for All household-savings record](records/us-doee-solar-for-all-household-bill-savings-2025-2026.json),
the [program update](https://doee.dc.gov/release/doee-launches-next-phase-solar-all-epa-award),
the [customer terms](https://doee.dc.gov/service/solarterms), and the [reach-scope audit](projects/us-repeat-energy-crises/solar-for-all-reach-scope-audit-v1.md).

## 322. Monthly credit receipt can be visible before the household's net energy outcome

A 2025 District of Columbia Public Service Commission filing reports project-
level subscriber counts and matching counts of subscribers receiving monthly
credits on listed community-renewable-energy projects—for example, 309 of 309,
303 of 303, 297 of 297, and 260 of 260.

This is a real implementation endpoint beneath the program promise: the
regulatory record shows that listed subscribers were reported as receiving a
monthly credit. It still does not reveal the individual bill, dollar amount,
timing, other charges, arrears, billing errors, or whether the credit produced
lasting energy security. Project-level receipt is therefore stronger than
eligibility or projected savings, but weaker than customer-level net benefit.

Read the [DCPSC monthly-credit record](records/us-dcpsc-solar-for-all-monthly-credit-receipt-2025.json)
and the [DCPSC filing](https://edocket.dcpsc.org/apis/api/Filing/download?attachId=231182&guidFileName=f8e79b1b-f57a-4c90-b284-62dc6f35f109.pdf).

## 323. A billing-data failure can produce a proposed remedy before individual correction is visible

DCPSC filings say the community-solar credit audit could not determine whether
Pepco properly credited subscribers because required billing data were not
provided in time for a core audit purpose. A later filing describes a proposed
error-rate method of $5.70 per month per Solar for All subscriber for each
month in the audit period.

This is a powerful institutional middle stage: the system recognizes that its
own measurement and billing pathway is incomplete and proposes a way to make
people whole without reconstructing every account. But a proposed formula is
not a final order, a posted bill credit, or verified recovery. It may over- or
under-compensate particular customers, and the record does not show receipt,
adequacy, trust, or later energy security.

Read the [DCPSC remedy record](records/us-dcpsc-solar-credit-error-rate-remedy-2025.json),
the [crediting-data filing](https://edocket.dcpsc.org/apis/api/Filing/download?attachId=230494&guidFileName=b566be82-6e0c-4c65-85fb-8028e24574ed.pdf),
and the [remedy recommendation filing](https://edocket.dcpsc.org/apis/api/Filing/download?attachId=232090&guidFileName=a2c0004c-540e-4ec7-a081-3b04e819e37e.pdf).

## 324. A customer-facing correction can be planned without proving corrected receipt

DOEE customer guidance describes a separate Solar for All billing-error episode:
credits were front-loaded into June and July statements, affected customers
received a debit letter, and the September bill was supposed to be adjusted for
the excess credits. The guidance also states an expected annual savings figure
of up to $500, but it does not provide a complete affected-customer denominator
or later account-level bills.

This adds a real correction pathway to the atlas: error recognition, notice,
and planned adjustment. It still cannot be coded as verified correction receipt
or adequate recovery. The later bill, adjustment amount, arrears effect, and
customer response remain open.

Read the [Solar for All remedy record](records/us-dcpsc-solar-credit-error-rate-remedy-2025.json)
and the [DOEE customer FAQ](https://doee.dc.gov/sites/default/files/dc/sites/ddoe/service_content/attachments/SfA%20Frequently%20Asked%20Questions.pdf).

## 325. Public benefit receipt is not the same as material security

The SNAP route-to-security synthesis puts several public-system stages beside
one another without pooling them. Following-month rent/mortgage and utility
hardship remains substantial across SNAP entry, exit, and continued receipt;
recertification interruptions involve notice, time, paperwork, interview, and
lost-document barriers; and charitable-food participation can coexist with
unmet need and discomfort or access problems.

The important result is not that SNAP “failed” or that receipt has no value.
It is that a public benefit is a route, not a security switch. Completion,
receipt, adequacy, correction, household recovery, and later trust/action are
different stages, and the current public evidence does not join them in one
same-episode ledger.

Read the [SNAP route-to-security triangulation](projects/us-safety-net-access/public-system-route-to-security-triangulation-v1.md)
and the [same-episode implementation specification](projects/us-safety-net-access/same-episode-event-ledger-implementation-v1.md).

## 326. Capability acceptance is not operational leverage

The Polish F-35 record now reaches several real realization stages: the first
aircraft arrived, formal acceptance occurred, first pilots completed training,
and eight aircraft were reported in country. But the same record keeps trained
ground personnel, spares, readiness certification, sortie generation, mission
use, and external response open. The separate JASSM-ER audit remains at a
planned delivery window without a qualifying public delivery or acceptance
record.

This gives the geopolitical lane a useful comparison: “the state has the
capability” is not one fact. Procurement, delivery, acceptance, readiness,
operation, public legitimacy, deterrence, and changed outside behavior are
different clocks. A ceremony or aircraft count can be meaningful institutional
progress without proving strategic leverage.

Read the [F-35 realization ledger](projects/ai-work-control/data/poland-f35-husarz-arrival-acceptance-event-ledger-v1.json),
the [JASSM-ER non-observation record](records/us-poland-jassm-er-delivery-nonobservation-2026.json),
and the [Eastern Sentry persistence and legitimacy audit](projects/ai-work-control/poland-eastern-sentry-persistence-legitimacy-audit-v1.md).

## 327. Public infrastructure voice is not public agreement

The Virginia Golden-Mars transmission case records more than 600 registered
